Duality for cohomology of split tori on curves over local fields
Amalendu Krishna, Jitendra Rathore, Samiron Sadhukhan

Abstract




2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 14C25, 14F22; Secondary 14F30, 19D45

Abstract.

We prove duality theorems for the étale cohomology of logarithmic Hodge-Witt
sheaves and split tori on smooth curves over a local field of positive
characteristic. As an application, we obtain a description of the Brauer group of the
function fields of curves over local fields in terms of the characters of the
idele groups. We also show that the classical Brauer-Manin pairing between the Brauer
and Picard groups of smooth projective curves over local fields has analogues for
arbitrary smooth curves, smooth projective curves with modulus and singular projective
curves over such fields.

1.
Introduction

The duality theorems of Tate (see
[
57
]
,
[
58
]
) and Lichtenbaum

[
32
]
for the étale cohomology of split tori over
p
-adic fields and
smooth projective curves over such fields are fundamental results in
arithmetic geometry. In recent years, these duality theorems have
been extended by Scheiderer and van Hamel
[
49
]

(see also
[
16
]
and
[
60
]
)
to more general settings of smooth affine curves over
p
-adic fields.
These generalizations have found many applications,
especially in the study of local-global principles for cohomology of commutative group
schemes over the function fields of curves over
p
-adic fields.

The analogue of Tate’s duality over local fields of
positive characteristics was proven by Milne
[
37
]
while the analogue of
Lichtenbaum duality over such fields was proven
by Saito
[
46
]
(see also
[
37
]
).
However, the analogues of the results of Scheiderer and van Hamel for smooth affine
curves and their function fields
over local fields of positive characteristics are currently unknown.
This paper is an attempt to fill this gap.

To obtain these generalizations, we prove a new duality theorem for the logarithmic
Hodge-Witt cohomology on smooth projective curves over a local field of positive
characteristic. We introduce Brauer group with modulus, and extend the classical
Brauer-Manin pairing to the setting of 0-cycles and Brauer groups with modulus over
an arbitrary local field.
The duality theorem for the logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology on smooth projective
varieties over finite fields was established long ago by Milne
[
38
]
. Over
a local field, a partial result was obtained by Kato-Saito
[
25
]
.
Below, we describe the main results of this paper.

1.1.
Duality for cohomology of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}

We fix a local field (i.e., a complete discrete valuation field with finite residue
field)
k
of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
X
be a geometrically connected smooth projective
curve over
k
and let
j\colon X^{o}\hookrightarrow X
be a dense open immersion.
Let
\iota\colon D\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of an effective Cartier divisor
whose support is the complement of
X^{o}
. We let
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
denote the rank one split
torus over
k
. We let
H^{q}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})={\varprojlim}_{n}\ H^{q}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{\mathbb{G}}_{m,(X,nD)})
, where
{\mathbb{G}}_{m,(X,nD)}={\rm Ker}({\mathbb{G}}_{m,X}\twoheadrightarrow\iota_{*}({\mathbb{G}}_{m,nD}))
.
This is an enriched version of the usual cohomology with compact support

H^{q}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
of
X^{o}
in positive characteristic. One easily checks that
the canonical map
H^{q}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},c}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\to H^{q}_{{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
factors through

H^{q}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
.

In characteristic zero, the duality theorem for
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}

on
X^{o}
(e.g., see
[
49
]
) is essentially insensitive to the topology of the
underlying étale cohomology groups because it holds true if we simply endow these
groups with discrete topology and pass to their profinite completions. This makes
the proof of duality relatively simpler. In contrast,
endowing each cohomology group with correct topology is a challenging part of the proof
of the duality theorem in positive characteristic. We briefly explain the topologies
that we use and refer to the body of the paper for details.

Recall that
{\rm Pic}(X)
is the group of

k
-points of the Picard scheme
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X)
. Since the latter is a locally of finite type

k
-scheme,
{\rm Pic}(X)
is endowed with the adic topology induced by the
valuation topology of
k
. Since
{\rm Pic}(X^{o})
is a quotient of
{\rm Pic}(X)
, it is equipped
with the quotient topology. As a key step in our proofs, we show in this
paper that the relative Picard group

{\rm Pic}(X|D)
is also equipped with the adic topology such that the canonical
map
{\rm Pic}(X|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X)
is a topological quotient.
We endow
H^{1}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
with
the inverse limit topology using a canonical isomorphism

{\rm Pic}(X|D)\cong H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,{\mathbb{G}}_{m,(X,D)})
.
All other cohomology groups will be endowed with the discrete topology.

For a Hausdorff topological abelian group
G
with the topology
\tau
, we let

G^{{\rm pf}}
denote the profinite
\tau
-completion of
G
. That is,
{G}^{\rm pf}
is
the inverse limit
{\varprojlim}_{U}\ {G}/{U}
with the inverse limit topology,
where
U
runs through
\tau
-open subgroups of finite index in
G
. It is clear that

{G}^{{\rm pf}}
is a profinite abelian group. We refer to §
4.2
for the
definitions of non-degenerate and perfect pairings of topological abelian groups.
In this paper, we prove the following duality theorem for étale cohomology.

Theorem 1.1
.

For every integer
q\neq 0
, there is a bilinear pairing

H^{q}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\times H^{3-q}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

which induces perfect pairings of topological abelian groups

H^{1}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})^{{\rm pf}}\times H^{2}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}},

H^{2}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\times H^{1}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

and

H^{3}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\times H^{0}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

For smooth curves over
p
-adic fields, this theorem is
due to Lichtenbaum
[
32
]
in the proper case, and to
Scheiderer-van Hamel
[
49
]
(see also
[
16
]
) in the open
case. In positive characteristic, the
q=1
case of the theorem is
due to Saito
[
46
]
(see also
[
37
]
) in the proper
case.

Remark 1.2
.

One can construct the pairing of Theorem
1.1
for

q=0
as well but it will not be perfect (not even non-degenerate).

1.2.
Duality for cohomology of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m,K}

Let
X
be as above and let
K
denote the function field of
X
.
We let
\widehat{I}(X)={\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\prod^{\prime}}}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}
denote the
restricted product with respect to the subgroups
(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}})^{\times}
,
where
\widehat{K}_{x}
is the quotient field of
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}
.
The idele class group
C(K)
is the cokernel of the canonical inclusion

K^{\times}\hookrightarrow\widehat{I}(X)
.
This group is endowed with the inverse limit of the adic topologies of the
relative Picard groups of
X
(cf. §
12.3
). We let
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
have the
discrete topology. As an application of Theorem
1.1
, we prove the
following.

Theorem 1.3
.

There is a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\times C(K)^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

This result provides an explicit description of
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
in terms of the
characters of the idele class group. In fact, we prove a duality theorem
for all étale cohomology groups of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m,K}
in this paper (cf. Theorem
12.8
).
When
K
is the quotient field of an excellent normal 2-dimensional complete local domain
with finite residue field, an analogue of Theorem
1.3
was shown by
Saito
[
46
]
.

1.3.
Duality for logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is based on the duality theorem for the
p
-adic
étale motivic cohomology groups of smooth projective curves over
k
, which
we now describe. We fix an integer
n\geq 1
.
Let
X
be as above and let
W_{n}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}
denote the
logarithmic Hodge-Witt complex on
X_{\text{\'{e}t}}
à la Bloch-Deligne-Illusie.

For smooth projective schemes (of arbitrary dimensions) over a finite field, Milne
showed that the logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology (i.e., the
étale cohomology of
W_{n}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}
) groups satisfy
Poincaré duality. However, the analogous result over local fields (of
positive characteristics) is presently unknown. The following result settles this
problem for curves.

To state the duality theorem, we need to recall that over finite fields,
Milne’s duality yields a perfect pairing of finite groups. However, the
logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology groups over local fields are generally
not finite. To offset this problem, we need to equip these groups with
suitable non-discrete topologies. In fact, dealing with the infinitude of
cohomology groups and choosing correct topology on them are perhaps the major part
of proving a duality theorem over local fields. For the topologies that we
endow these groups with, the reader is referred to §
3.2
. The following
theorem was proven by Kato-Saito
[
25
, Prop. 4]

when
j=0
by a different method.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
X
be as in Theorem
1.1
and let
q,j
be any integers. Then the cup
product on the étale cohomology of the logarithmic Hodge-Witt sheaves
induces a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups

H^{q}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\times H^{2-q}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Remark 1.5
.

We actually prove this theorem under weaker assumptions on
X
. Namely, we
need to assume that
X
is regular but do not require it to be smooth over
k
.
We work under the weaker hypothesis that
X
is generically smooth over
k
.
The validity of
the theorem under generic smoothness is useful for some concrete applications.
For instance, we hope that it will allow one to prove the duality theorem for the

p
-adic étale motivic cohomology groups with compact support for smooth
but non-proper curves. Such a result will have applications to the
class field theory of smooth open curves over local fields. This is the topic of

[
31
]
.

1.4.
Brauer-Manin pairing for modulus pairs

Let
\iota\colon D\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of an effective Cartier divisor
as above. We shall refer to the pair
(X,D)
as a (1-dimensional) modulus pair.
Let
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)
be the Chow group of 0-cycles for the modulus pair
(X,D)
.
We shall show in this paper that
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)
is naturally endowed with
an adic topology such that the canonical map

{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm CH}_{0}(X)
is a topological quotient.
In order to extend the classical Brauer-Manin pairing to the modulus setting, we
introduce the Brauer group of the modulus pair
(X,D)
which we denote by

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
(see Definition
8.9
).
This is a certain subgroup of
{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
which is
defined in terms of Kato’s filtration on the étale motivic cohomology group

H^{2}(K,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))
, where
K
is the function field of
X
. We endow

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
with the discrete topology. As a key step for proving
Theorem
1.1
, we establish the following new Brauer-Manin pairing.

Theorem 1.6
.

There is a continuous pairing of topological abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

such that the induced pairing

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

is perfect.

The case
D=\emptyset
of this theorem is due to Lichtenbaum
[
32
]

in characteristic zero and Saito
[
46
]

(see also
[
37
, Thm. III.7.8]
) in positive characteristic.
In this paper, we provide an independent and short proof of the harder part of
Saito’s result as an
immediate corollary of Theorem
1.4
(see Corollary
9.6
).

1.5.
Brauer-Manin pairing for singular curves

Recall that the Brauer group of a singular variety
Y
does not satisfy
the Brauer injectivity property, i.e., the canonical map
{\operatorname{Br}}(Y)\to{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
fails
to be injective in general, for
K
the function field of
Y
.
This defect is analogous to the
one the classical Chow group (one defined in
[
10
]
)
{\rm CH}_{0}(Y)
suffers from.
In this paper, we introduce a refined Brauer group of
Y
which we denote by

{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(Y)
. We refer to this as the Levine-Weibel Brauer group.
The refined Brauer group satisfies some nice properties, including the Brauer
injectivity property.

Let
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(Y)
denote the Levine-Weibel Chow group of

Y
. One may recall that this coincides with
{\rm Pic}(Y)
if
\text{\rm dim}(Y)=1
.
We show in this paper that
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(Y)
is naturally endowed with an
adic topology such that the pull-back map
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(Y)\to{\rm CH}_{0}(Y_{n})

is continuous if
Y
is a geometrically integral
projective curve over
k
with smooth normalization
Y_{n}
.
We endow
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(Y)
with the discrete topology and prove the following extension of
the Brauer-Manin pairing to singular curves.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
Y
be a geometrically integral projective curve over a local field
k
whose
normalization is smooth over
k
. Then there is a continuous pairing of topological
abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(Y)\times{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(Y)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

such that the induced pairing

{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(Y)\times{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(Y)^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

is perfect.

1.6.
A brief outline of proofs

The main ingredients for proving Theorem
1.1
are
Theorems
1.4
and
1.6
. A key step in the
proof of Theorem
1.4
is a duality theorem of Zhao
[
61
]
.
In §
2
, we recall this duality theorem. In §
3
,
we equip the logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology of a smooth projective curve
X
over
a local field
k
of positive characteristic with a suitable topology
using some deductions. We construct
a trace homomorphism for the top logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology of
X
in
§
4
and complete the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
A trace map in this case was also constructed by Kato-Saito

[
25
, § 3, Prop. 4]
. But our trace homomorphism is, a priori, different.
It has an advantage that one can easily show its compatibility with the
trace homomorphisms for closed points under the Gysin homomorphisms.
This compatibility is crucial in our proofs.
In the next three sections, we equip the relative Picard group
of modulus pairs
(X,D)
with the adic topology. We also prove some key properties of the
Albanese map for
X
and the Pontryagin dual of the relative Picard group.

We introduce the Brauer group of modulus pairs in §
8
and establish the
Brauer-Manin pairing between the Brauer and Chow groups of a modulus pair in
§
9
. One of the key steps in the proof of Theorem
1.6

is a duality theorem for the cohomology of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
on
X
and its compatibility with
Kato’s duality for 2-dimensional local fields obtained by completing the function
field of
X
at closed points. This is done in §
10
.
In §
11
, we complete another step for proving Theorem
1.6
,
namely, we show that the Brauer-Manin paring for a modulus pair
(X,D)
is
continuous. We complete the proof of Theorem
1.6
in §
12
, and
then apply it to complete the proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
Theorem
1.3
.
In §
13
, we introduce a refined version of the Brauer group of singular
varieties and prove Theorem
1.7
as an application of Theorem
1.6
.

1.7.
Notations

We shall work over a field
k
of characteristic
p\geq 0
throughout this
paper. We shall let
k_{s}
(resp.
\overline{k}
) denote a fixed separable (resp. algebraic)
closure of
k
. We let
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
denote the category of
separated Noetherian
k
-schemes and
{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
the category of smooth (in particular,
finite type)
k
-schemes. A
k
-scheme will mean an object of
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
and a finite type

k
-scheme will mean an object of
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}^{{\rm ft}}_{k}
.
The product
X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}Y
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
will be written as

X\times Y
. We let
X^{(q)}
(resp.
X_{(q)}
) denote the set of points on
X
having
codimension (resp. dimension)
q
. We shall let
X_{\rm sing}
(resp.
X_{\rm reg}
) denote the
singular locus (resp. regular locus) of
X
with the reduced closed subscheme
structure. We let
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X)
denote the free abelian group of 0-cycles on
X
.

We let
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k/{\rm zar}}
(resp.
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k/{\rm nis}}
, resp.
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k/{\acute{e}t}})

denote the Zariski (resp. Nisnevich, resp. étale) site of
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
.
Unless we mention the topology specifically,
all cohomology groups in this paper will be considered with respect to the
étale topology.
We shall let
cd_{p}(X)
denote the étale
p
-cohomological dimension of
X\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}

if
{\rm char}(k)=p>0
. We shall let
cd(X)
denote the étale cohomological
dimension of torsion sheaves on
X
. We shall let
G_{k}
denote the abelian
absolute Galois group of
k
.

For an abelian group
A
, we shall write
{\operatorname{Tor}}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}}(A,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n)
as

{}_{n}A
and
A/{nA}
as
A/n
. We shall let
A\{p^{\prime}\}
denote the
subgroup of elements of
A
which are torsion of order prime to
p
.
We let
A\{p\}
denote the subgroup of elements of
A
which are torsion of
order some power of
p
. The tensor product
A\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}B
will be written as

A\otimes B
. We let
{\mathbf{Ab}}
denote the category of abelian groups and

{\mathbf{Tab}}
denote the category of topological abelian groups with continuous homomorphisms.
For
A,B\in{\mathbf{Tab}}
, we shall let
{\rm Hom}_{\rm cf}(A,B)=({\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(A,B))_{\rm tor}
.
Unless a specific topology is mentioned, we shall assume all finite abelian groups to
be endowed with the discrete topology. In this paper, we shall use the general
notation
\{A_{n}\}
for pro-abelian or ind-abelian group indexed by

{\mathbb{N}}=\{1,2,\ldots\}
. However, we shall also use specific notations

``{{\underset{n}{\varinjlim}}}"A_{n}
for an ind-abelian group and

``{{\underset{n}{\varprojlim}}}"A_{n}
for a pro-abelian
group if we need to make a distinction between them.

2.
Recollection of Zhao’s duality

In this section, we recall Brauer group, Milnor
K
-theory, Hodge-Witt sheaves and
the duality theorem of Zhao
[
61
]
for the
logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology. This will be the key ingredient in the proof of
Theorem
1.4
. We also recall the
Pontryagin duality for a special class of locally compact Hausdorff topological abelian
groups.

2.1.
Pontryagin duality for torsion-by-profinite groups

Let
G
be a locally compact Hausdorff topological abelian group.
We shall say that
G
is ‘torsion-by-profinite’ if there is an exact sequence

(2.1)

0\to G_{\rm pf}\to G\to G_{\rm dt}\to 0,

where
G_{\rm pf}
is an open and profinite subgroup of
G
and
G_{\rm dt}

is a torsion group with the quotient topology (necessarily discrete).
We shall call (
2.1
) a torsion-by-profinite presentation of
G
.
Note that such a presentation of
G
is not unique.

Recall that
G
admits its Pontryagin dual
G^{\star}:={\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(G,{\mathbb{T}})
,
where
{\mathbb{T}}
is the circle group such that the evaluation map
G\to(G^{*})^{*}
is
an isomorphism of locally compact topological abelian groups
(e.g., see
[
43
, § 2.9]
or
[
9
, Thm. 4.32]
)
if
G^{\star}
is endowed with the compact-open topology.
One knows that
G^{\star}\cong{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
if
G
is torsion-by-profinite
group (e.g., see
[
43
, Lem. 2.9.2]
), where

{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}={\mathbb{T}}_{{\rm tor}}
is endowed with the discrete topology.
Furthermore, one has an isomorphism of topological
abelian groups
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(G^{\star},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(G^{\star},{\mathbb{T}})
. In other words, letting
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
denote the
category of torsion-by-profinite topological abelian groups with continuous
homomorphisms, we have the following.

Lemma 2.1
.

The category
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
is closed under taking Pontryagin dual such that

G\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
is profinite if and only if
G^{\star}
is a discrete torsion group.
Moreover, the canonical map
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\to G^{\star}
is an
isomorphism of topological abelian groups for every
G\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
.

In this paper, we shall always consider
{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
as topological
abelian groups with discrete topologies. For any topological
abelian group
G
, we shall denote
{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
by
G^{\star}
.
For
G\in{\mathbf{Ab}}
, we shall let
G^{\vee}={\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Ab}}(G,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
.

Lemma 2.2
.

If
G\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
and
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
is a closed subgroup, then
G^{\prime}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
.

Proof.

This is elementary and we skip the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\beta\colon G\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime\prime}
be a continuous surjective homomorphism in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
such
that
G^{\prime\prime}_{{\rm pf}}\subseteq\beta(G_{\rm pf})
with respect to some torsion-by-profinite
presentations of
G
and
G^{\prime\prime}
as in (
2.1
). Then
the induced map
\beta^{\prime}\colon{G}/{{\rm Ker}(\beta)}\to G^{\prime\prime}
is an isomorphism of
topological abelian groups if
{G}/{{\rm Ker}(\beta)}
is endowed with the quotient
topology.

Proof.

If
G^{\prime\prime}
is profinite, the claim is an easy application of the fact that a continuous
bijective homomorphism from a compact Hausdorff topological abelian group to a
Hausdorff topological abelian group is a topological isomorphism. For the general
case, we let

H=\beta^{-1}(G^{\prime\prime}_{\rm pf})
. Then
H\subset G
is an open subgroup and
G/H
is discrete.
Furthermore, there is a surjective continuous homomorphism

\beta\colon H\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime\prime}_{\rm pf}
such that
(G_{\rm pf}\cap H)\twoheadrightarrow G^{\prime\prime}_{\rm pf}
by our
assumption. Lemma
2.2
implies that
\beta\colon H\to G^{\prime\prime}_{\rm pf}
is a continuous surjective homomorphism from a torsion-by-profinite group to a
profinite group. In particular, this is a topological quotient. Since

G^{\prime\prime}_{\rm pf}
is open in
G^{\prime\prime}
, one deduces that
\beta
is an open map on
G
.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Let

0\to G^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha}G\xrightarrow{\beta}G^{\prime\prime}\to 0

be a short exact sequence in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
. Assume that with respect to some
torsion-by-profinite presentations of
G
and
G^{\prime\prime}
as in (
2.1
),
one has
{G^{\prime\prime}}_{{\rm pf}}\subseteq\beta(G_{\rm pf})
. Then the sequence

0\to(G^{\prime\prime})^{\star}\xrightarrow{\beta^{\star}}G^{\star}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\star}}(G^{\prime})^{\star}\to 0

is exact.

Proof.

The sequence

0\to(G^{\prime\prime})^{\star}\xrightarrow{\beta^{\star}}G^{\star}\xrightarrow{\alpha^{\star}}(G^{\prime})^{\star}

is exact by Lemma
2.3
and
\alpha^{\star}
is surjective by
Lemma
2.1
and
[
9
, Cor. 4.42]
.
∎

Recall from §
1
that for a topological abelian group
G
, the
profinite completion of
G
(with respect to its topology) is the profinite topological
abelian group
G^{\rm pf}={\varprojlim}_{U}{G}/U
with the inverse limit topology, where
the limit is taken over all open subgroups of finite index in
G
.
The following result is elementary whose proof is left to the reader.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
G
be a topological abelian group. Then the
canonical map
(G^{\rm pf})^{\star}\to G^{\star}
induces an isomorphism

(G^{\rm pf})^{\star}\xrightarrow{\cong}(G^{\star})_{\rm tor}
.

Suppose that
\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a pro-object in
{\mathbf{Tab}}
such that
I
is cofiltered.
We let
G={\varprojlim}_{i}G_{i}
and endow it with the inverse limit
topology. Let
\pi_{i}\colon G\to G_{i}
be the projection map.
If the topology of each
G_{i}
is generated by open subgroups, then the same holds for

G
as well. More precisely,
G
admits a fundamental system of neighborhoods of
the identity of the form
\pi^{-1}_{i}(U_{i})
, where
U_{i}\subset G_{i}
is an open subgroup.
This happens, for instance, when each
G_{i}
is a Hausdorff, totally disconnected
and locally compact topological abelian group. If
k
is a local field and

X
is a commutative group scheme over
k
, then
X(k)
is endowed with
a topology of this kind (cf. §
6.3
). We shall use the following
result in this paper.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be above such that the topology of each
G_{i}
is generated by
open subgroups. Then the canonical map
{\varinjlim}_{i}G^{\star}_{i}\to G^{\star}
is
surjective. This map is bijective if
\pi_{i}\colon G\to G_{i}
is surjective for each

i\in I
.

Proof.

Let
\chi\colon G\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be a continuous character and let
H={\rm Ker}(\chi)
.
Then our hypothesis implies that there exists
i\in I
and an open subgroup

U_{i}\subset G_{i}
such that
V_{i}:=\pi^{-1}_{i}(U_{i})\subset H
. It follows that

\chi
factors through
\chi^{\prime}\colon G/{V_{i}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
We thus get a diagram

(2.2)

\textstyle{G_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{i}}
\textstyle{{G_{i}}/{U_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{i}}
\textstyle{G/{V_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}},}

whose left square is commutative. Since
G/{V_{i}}
and
{G_{i}}/{U_{i}}
are discrete,
we get a continuous character
\phi^{\prime}\colon{G_{i}}/{U_{i}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
such that
the left triangle commutes. Letting
\phi=\phi^{\prime}\circ\alpha_{i}
, we see that

\phi
is continuous and
\phi\circ\pi_{i}=\chi
. The second part of the lemma
is clear.
∎

2.2.
Milnor
K
-theory and logarithmic
Hodge-Witt sheaves

For a commutative ring
A
, we let
K_{*}(A)
denote the Quillen
K
-theory of
finitely generated projective
A
-modules. We let
\widehat{K}^{M}_{*}(A)
denote the graded
commutative ring defined as the quotient of the tensor algebra

T_{*}(A^{\times})
by the two-sided ideal generated by the homogeneous elements

a_{1}\otimes a_{2}
such that
a_{1}+a_{2}=1
. We let
{K}^{M}_{*}(A)
denote the improved
Milnor
K
-theory of
A
à la Gabber-Kerz
[
27
]
.
There are natural multiplicative homomorphisms
\widehat{K}^{M}_{r}(A)\xrightarrow{\alpha_{A}}{K}^{M}_{r}(A)\xrightarrow{\beta_{A}}K_{r}(A)
such that
\alpha_{A}
is surjective (resp. an
isomorphism) if
A
is a local ring (resp. a field). Furthermore,
\alpha_{A}
is an
isomorphism if
A
is local with infinite residue field and
\beta_{A}
is an isomorphism
for
r\leq 2
if
A
is local (see
[
27
, Prop. 10]
).

For a scheme
X
, we let

{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{r,X}
(resp.
\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}^{M}_{r,X}
, resp.
{\mathcal{K}}_{r,X}
) denote the sheaf
(in Zariski, Nisnevich or étale topology) on
X
associated to the presheaf

U\mapsto K^{M}_{r}({\mathcal{O}}(U))
(resp.
\widehat{K}^{M}_{r}({\mathcal{O}}(U))
, resp.

K_{r}({\mathcal{O}}(U))
). For an ideal
I\subset A
in a local ring, we let
\widehat{K}^{M}_{r}(A,I)={\rm Ker}(\widehat{K}^{M}_{r}(A)\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{K}^{M}_{r}(A/I))
. Given a closed immersion
\iota\colon Y\hookrightarrow X
, we let
\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}^{M}_{r,(X,Y)}={\rm Ker}(\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}^{M}_{r,X}\twoheadrightarrow\iota_{*}(\widehat{{\mathcal{K}}}^{M}_{r,Y}))
. The group
{K}^{M}_{r}(A,I)
and the sheaf
{{\mathcal{K}}}^{M}_{r,(X,Y)}

are defined in a similar manner.

Let
k
be a field of exponential characteristic
p>1
.
Recall from
[
19
]
that for a
k
-scheme
X
,
\{W_{m}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\}_{m\geq 1}

denotes the pro-complex of de Rham-Witt (Nisnevich) sheaves on
X
.
This is a pro-complex of sheaves of differential
graded algebras with the structure map
R
and the
differential
d
. Let
[-]_{m}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to W_{m}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
be the multiplicative
Teichmüller homomorphism. Recall that the pro-complex

\{W_{m}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}\}_{m\geq 1}
is equipped with the
Frobenius homomorphism of graded algebras
F\colon W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}\to W_{m-1}\Omega^{r}_{X}
and the additive Verschiebung homomorphism

V\colon W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}\to W_{m+1}\Omega^{r}_{X}
.
We let
ZW_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}={\rm Ker}(d\colon W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}\to W_{m}\Omega^{r+1}_{X})
and
BW_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}={\rm Image}(d\colon W_{m}\Omega^{r-1}_{X}\to W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X})
. We write
{\mathcal{H}}^{r}(W_{m}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X})={ZW_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}}/{BW_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}}
.

Recall from
[
19
]
that
W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}
is the (Zariski, Nisnevich or
étale) subsheaf of
W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}
which is the image of the
map
{\rm dlog}\colon{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{r,X}}/{p^{m}}\to W_{m}\Omega^{r}_{X}
, locally given by

{\rm dlog}(\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{r}\})={\rm dlog}[a_{1}]_{m}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\rm dlog}[a_{r}]_{m}
.
It is easily seen that this map exists (e.g., see
[
33
, Rem. 1.6]
).
Moreover, it is an isomorphism in any of the above topologies if
X
is regular
(e.g., see
[
14
, Lem. 2.3]
). Equivalently, there is a short exact
sequence of (Zariski, Nisnevich or étale) sheaves

(2.3)

0\to{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{r,X}\xrightarrow{p^{n}}{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{r,X}\to W_{n}\Omega^{r}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}\to 0.

The multiplicative structure of
{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{*,X}}/{p^{m}}
and
W_{m}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}

together with the multplicativity of
{\rm dlog}
induces a graded commutative ring structure
on
W_{m}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}
.

We end this subsection by recalling the Brauer group. For any Noetherian scheme
X
,
recall that
{\operatorname{Br}}(X)
denotes the cohomological Brauer group
H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
.
A result of Gabber (e.g., see
[
4
, Thm. 4.2.1]
) says that
{\operatorname{Br}}(X)
coincides with
the Azumaya Brauer group
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\prime}(X)
if
X
is quasi-projective over an affine scheme.
Since all schemes in this paper will satisfy this condition, we shall make no
distinction between the Azumaya and cohomological Brauer groups. Brauer group of
curves over local fields will be one the main objects of study in this paper.

2.3.
The set-up

The set-up for our duality theorem will be the following.
We fix a equicharacteristic complete discrete valuation ring (cdvr)
R
with finite
residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
. We let
{\mathfrak{m}}
denote the maximal ideal of
R
and
k

denote the quotient field of
R
. It is well known that
R
is canonically isomorphic
to the formal power series ring
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}[[\pi]]
, and
k={\mathbb{F}}_{q}((\pi))
, where
q=p^{n}
for
some prime number
p\geq 2
and some positive integer
n\geq 1
. We shall fix this
isomorphism throughout our discussion. We let
S={\rm Spec\,}(R)
and let
\eta
(resp.

s
) denote the generic (resp. closed) point of
S
.

We let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a connected Noetherian regular scheme with a flat and projective
morphism
{\mathbb{F}}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to S
of relative dimension
d\leq 1
.
We let
X
denote the generic
fiber and
{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
the (scheme-theoretic) closed fiber of
{\mathbb{F}}
. We shall assume that

Y:=({\mathcal{X}}_{s})_{\rm red}
is a simple normal crossing divisor on
{\mathcal{X}}
. A morphism
{\mathbb{F}}

satisfying these properties will be called a semi-stable model for the
k
-scheme
X
.
We let
u\colon X\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}
and
\iota\colon Y\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}
be the inclusions.
We let
f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
and
g\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{s}\to{\rm Spec\,}({\mathfrak{f}})
denote the
structure maps. We shall assume that
X
is geometrically connected and
generically smooth over
k
. Recall that the generic smoothness of a morphism of schemes

f\colon Z\to W
means that
f
is smooth in a neighborhood of each generic point of

Z
. All results in the remainder of §
2
(and also in the next two
sections) will be proven under this set-up. We let
K
denote the function field of

{\mathcal{X}}
.

The following elementary observation will be useful in this paper. We skip its
proof.

Lemma 2.7
.

{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
(equivalently,
Y
) is geometrically connected over
{\mathfrak{f}}
.

2.4.
Zhao’s duality theorem

We now recall the
d=1
case of the duality theorem of Zhao
(see
[
61
, Cor. 1.4.10, Thm. 3.1.1]
) which will be
a key ingredient in the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
We fix an integer
n\geq 1
.

Theorem 2.8
.

(1)

There is a canonical isomorphism of
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}
-modules

{{\rm Tr}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon H^{3}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}.

(2)

The graded commutative ring structure of
H^{*}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{\bullet}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})

gives rise to a pairing

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\times H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}

such that the induced map

{\Phi}^{ij}_{\mathcal{X}}\colon H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})

is an isomorphism.

(3)

The map

{\Psi}^{ij}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Tab}}}(H^{3-i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})

is a topological isomorphism if
H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
is endowed
with the discrete topology and
H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
is endowed with the
profinite topology by virtue of
\Phi^{ij}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
and the Pontryagin duality.

Proof.

This result was proven by Zhao in
[
61
]
except that he additionally assumed
that
{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
is reduced and
X\in{\mathbf{Sm}}_{k}
. But these
assumptions are unnecessary as we shall explain by outlining Zhao’s proof.

Recall that
\iota\colon Y\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}
is the inclusion of the reduced special fiber,
which is assumed to be a simple normal crossing divisor in
{\mathcal{X}}
.
The proof of (1) goes as follows. Using a duality theorem of Sato for normal
crossing projective schemes over
{\mathfrak{f}}
, the
proof of (1) is reduced to showing a purity isomorphism (i.e., Corollary 1.4.9 of op.
cit.). The proof of this corollary has the following ingredients.
The first is Proposition 1.2.4 of op.
cit. for
Y
, which is a result of Sato that holds for any normal crossing scheme.
The second is Corollary 1.3.14 of op. cit., which is a result of Shiho that holds
for any closed immersion of regular
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-schemes. The third is Proposition 1.4.6
of op. cit., which is a result of Moser that holds for any finite type
{\mathfrak{f}}
-scheme.
The final step is Theorem 1.4.4 of op. cit., whose proof also requires
only the above three ingredients.

To prove (2), we let
\iota^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{s}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}
and
\iota^{\prime\prime}\colon Y\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{X}}_{s}

be the inclusions. We then have a cup product pairing (see § 3.1 of op. cit.)

H^{i}(Y,\iota^{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))\times H^{3-i}(Y,R\iota^{!}(W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))\to H^{3}(Y,R\iota^{!}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})).

On the other hand, there are canonical maps

(2.4)

\begin{array}[]{lll}H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})&\xrightarrow{\iota^{\prime*}}&H^{i}({\mathcal{X}}_{s},\iota^{\prime*}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))\\
&\xrightarrow{\iota^{\prime\prime*}}&H^{i}({\mathcal{X}}_{s},\iota^{\prime\prime}_{*}\circ\iota^{\prime\prime*}\circ\iota^{\prime*}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))\\
&\cong&H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},\iota_{*}\circ\iota^{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))\\
&\cong&H^{i}(Y,\iota^{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})).\end{array}

The first arrow is an isomorphism by the proper base change theorem for the
étale cohomology of torsion sheaves (e.g., see
[
35
, Cor. VI.2.7]
).
The second arrow is the canonical counit of adjunction map which is
an isomorphism since the closed immersion
\iota^{\prime\prime}
has empty complement and

\iota^{\prime\prime}_{*}
is exact. Since
H^{i}(Y,R\iota^{!}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))\cong H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
, the above pairing can be written as

(2.5)

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\times H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{3}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{{\rm Tr}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}.

The proof of the perfectness of (
2.5
)
is easily reduced to the case when
n=1
using the exact sequence
(see
[
53
, Prop. 2.8, 2.12]
)

(2.6)

0\to W_{n-m}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}\xrightarrow{\underline{p}^{m}}W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}\xrightarrow{R^{n-m}}W_{m}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}\to 0

and compatibility of (
2.5
) with respect to maps

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\underline{p}}H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n+1}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
and
H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n+1}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{R}H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
. Here,

\underline{p}^{m}
is induced by the multiplication map
{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{j,{\mathcal{X}}}}/{p^{n-m}}\xrightarrow{p^{m}}{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{j,{\mathcal{X}}}}/{p^{n}}
via the isomorphism
{\rm dlog}
.

The proof of the case
n=1
is a direct consequence of
Cor. 2.5.2 (which is the coherent duality) and Prop. 3.1.2 (which proves the
compatibility between the trace maps of Sato and that of the coherent duality)
of op. cit..
The latter result is a statement only about
S
. The proof of the former result
does not depend on the special fiber at all, but it requires us to know that
the locally free sheaf
\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
has rank two. However, this can be checked
by restricting to the open subscheme of
{\mathcal{X}}
where
X
is smooth over
k
.
The generic smoothness of
X
over
k
suffices for this purpose.
∎

3.
The topology of logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology

As we mentioned in §
1
, we need to endow the logarithmic Hodge-Witt
cohomology of complete curves over local fields with suitable topology in order to
extend Milne’s duality for logarithmic Hodge-Witt
cohomology over finite fields to local fields. This is the goal of the present
section. We shall continue to work under the set-up of §
2.3
.

3.1.
Some finiteness results

In this subsection, we prove some technical lemmas regarding certain
logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology groups as preparation for proving Theorem
1.1
.
We let
n\geq 1
be any integer. We begin with the following vanishing statement.

Lemma 3.1
.

We have
W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}=0
for
n\geq 1
and
j\geq 3
.
Furthermore,
H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})=0
for
i\geq 3
and

j\geq 0
.

Proof.

To prove the first claim, we can assume, using (
2.6
), that
n=1
.
By the exact sequence (where
\overline{{\operatorname{id}}}
is the canonical quotient map and
\overline{F}
is
induced by
F
, see
[
14
, § 5.2]
)

(3.1)

0\to W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}\to W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{\mathcal{X}}\xrightarrow{\overline{{\operatorname{id}}}-\overline{F}}\frac{W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{\mathcal{X}}}{dV^{n-1}\Omega^{j-1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\to 0,

it suffices to show that
\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}=0
for
j\geq 3
.
Since
{\mathcal{X}}
is connected and
\Omega^{j}_{\mathcal{X}}
is locally free
(e.g., see
[
61
, Prop. 2.1.2]
), it suffices to show that
\Omega^{j}_{\mathcal{X}}
is
generically zero. We can thus replace
{\mathcal{X}}
by the largest affine open subscheme

X_{\operatorname{sm}}\subset X
such that
X_{\operatorname{sm}}
is smooth over
k
. Since
X_{\operatorname{sm}}\neq\emptyset
by our assumption, the claim is therefore reduced to showing that

\Omega^{j}_{U}=0
for
j\geq 3
if
U
is any smooth curve over
k
.

Now, we look at the exact sequence (e.g., see
[
34
, Thm. 25.1]
)

(3.2)

0\to\Omega^{1}_{k}\otimes_{k}{\mathcal{O}}_{U}\xrightarrow{d}\Omega^{1}_{U}\to\Omega^{1}_{U/k}\to 0.

Taking the higher exterior powers, we get
\Omega^{j}_{U}\cong\Omega^{j-1}_{k}\otimes_{k}\Omega^{1}_{U/k}
for
j\geq 2
. Hence, it suffices to show that
\Omega^{j}_{k}=0
for

j\geq 2
. But this is well known because
R={\mathbb{F}}_{q}[[t]]
and a direct computation shows
that
R\xrightarrow{\cong}\Omega^{1}_{R}
, which implies that
\Omega^{j}_{R}=0
for

j\geq 2
. Note that this also shows that
\Omega^{2}_{X}\cong\Omega^{1}_{X/k}
.

To prove the second claim, it suffices to show using
(
2.4
) that
H^{i}(Y,\iota^{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))=0
for

i\geq 3
. But this is clear since
cd_{p}(Y)\leq 2

(e.g., see
[
35
, Chap. VI, Rem. 1.5(b)]
).
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

The boundary map of the localization sequence

\partial\colon H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{3}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The localization sequence in question is the exact sequence

(3.3)

H^{2}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{3}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{3}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}).

It suffices therefore to show that
H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})=0
for

i\geq 2
. By (
2.4
), we have

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\cong H^{i}(Y,\iota^{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}))
.
But the latter group is zero for
i\geq 3
since
cd_{p}(Y)=2
, as we observed before.

To prove the vanishing of
H^{2}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
, it suffices to show,
using Theorem
2.8
, that
H^{1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})=0
.
To that end, we look at the exact sequence

(3.4)

0\to H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}H^{0}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})\to H^{1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})\to H^{1}({\mathcal{X}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}).

Since
{\mathcal{X}}
is integral and normal, the map
u^{*}
on the left is an isomorphism
and the map
u^{*}
on the right is injective. It follows that the middle group is zero.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

The group
H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
is finite unless

i\in\{2,3\}
and
j\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
3.1
, we can assume
j\leq 2
. We have seen in the
proof of Lemma
3.2
that
H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{0}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})=0
for

i\leq 1
. We have
H^{0}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})=0
for every
j\geq 0

by the Gersten injectivity
W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}\hookrightarrow u_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})

(see
[
53
, Thm. 4.1]
). The finitude claim for

H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
follows from Theorem
2.8
since

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})\cong H^{i}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})
(see (
2.4
))
and the latter group is finite by
[
48
, Thm. 1.2.2]
.
We are left with showing the finitude of
H^{1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
.

We look at the exact (localization) sequence

(3.5)

H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}_{0}}H^{0}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\hskip 85.35826pt

\hskip 199.16928pt\to H^{1}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}_{1}}H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}).

It suffices to show that
{\rm Coker}(u^{*}_{0})
and
{\rm Ker}(u^{*}_{1})
are finite.
To prove the finiteness of
{\rm Coker}(u^{*}_{0})
, note that (
2.3
) yields
the commutative diagram of exact sequences

(3.6)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{{\mathcal{X}}})}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}_{0}}
\textstyle{{}_{p^{n}}{\rm Pic}({\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{p^{n}}{\rm Pic}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

We next note that there are exact sequences

(3.7)

0\to H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\xrightarrow{v_{k}}{\mathbb{Z}}\to 0

and

(3.8)

0\to{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\iota^{\prime}_{*}}{\underset{Z\in{\rm Irr}(Y)}{\bigoplus}}{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}}{\rm Pic}({\mathcal{X}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}{\rm Pic}(X)\to 0,

where the first sequence follows from the geometric integrality condition
(cf. Lemma
2.7
). The second sequence is well known (e.g., see

[
52
]
) if we let
\iota^{\prime}_{*}(1)=[{\mathcal{X}}_{s}]
, the class of the Weil divisor
associated to the special fiber
{\mathcal{X}}_{s}
and
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}
denotes the cycle class map.
It easily follows from these exact sequences that the kernels and cokernels
of the left and right vertical arrows in (
3.6
) are finite. Hence, so are
the kernel and cokernel of the middle vertical arrow (the assertion about the kernel
will be used only in the next lemma).

To prove the finitude of
{\rm Ker}(u^{*}_{1})
, we look at the commutative diagram
with exact rows (following (
2.3
))

(3.9)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\rm Pic}({\mathcal{X}})}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}_{1}}
\textstyle{{}_{p^{n}}{\operatorname{Br}}({\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\rm Pic}(X)}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{}_{p^{n}}{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

It follows from
[
4
, Thm. 5.6.1(v), 10.3.1(ii)]
that

(3.10)

{\operatorname{Br}}({\mathcal{X}})={\operatorname{Br}}({\mathcal{X}}_{s})={\operatorname{Br}}(Y)=0.

On the other hand, it follows from (
3.8
) that the kernel of the
left vertical arrow in (
3.9
) is finite. We conclude that

{\rm Ker}(u^{*}_{1})
is finite. We have thus shown that
H^{1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})

is finite. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

It can be shown that each of the groups
H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
is
infinite in general if
i\in\{2,3\}
and
j\in\{0,1\}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

For all
i,j\geq 0
, the kernel of
u^{*}\colon H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is finite.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
2.8
and Lemma
3.3
.
∎

3.2.
Logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology as topological
groups

We shall now endow the logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology of
X
with
torsion-by-profinite topology.
For
i,j\geq 0
, we consider the localization sequence

(3.11)

\cdots\to H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\hskip 28.45274pt

\hskip 256.0748pt\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{i+1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to\cdots.

By Lemma
3.5
, it breaks into exact sequences

(3.12)

H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{u^{*}}F_{ij}\to 0;

(3.13)

0\to F_{ij}\to H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\partial}H^{i+1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})

such that
{\rm Ker}(u^{*})
is finite.

Recall from Theorem
2.8
that each
H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
is
a discrete torsion group of exponent
p^{n}
and
H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})

is a profinite topological abelian group. It follows that
{\rm Image}(\iota_{*})
is
a finite closed subgroup of
H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
. In particular, its
subspace topology induced from
H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
as well as
its quotient topology induced from
H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
is discrete.

For
i,j\geq 0
, we endow
F_{ij}
with the quotient topology via
(
3.12
). Then
F_{ij}
becomes a quotient of the profinite group

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})
by a closed subgroup.
It follows that
F_{ij}
is a profinite abelian group and (
3.12
)
is an exact sequence of topological abelian groups.

We endow
H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
with the unique topology for which

F_{ij}
is an open subgroup of
H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
and

{H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})}/{F_{ij}}={\rm Ker}(\iota_{*})
is a discrete
quotient of
H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
.
This endows
H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
with the structure of a topological
abelian group such that (
3.13
) is an exact sequence of topological
abelian groups. If we combine this with everything we have shown in
§
2.4
and §
3.1
, we get the following.

Proposition 3.6
.

For each
i,j\geq 0
, the group
H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is
equipped with the structure of a torsion-by-profinite topological abelian group
satisfying the following.

(1)

(
3.11
) is an exact sequence in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
for
every
j\geq 0
.

(2)

H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})=0
unless
0\leq i,j\leq 2
.

(3)

H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is profinite unless
i\in\{1,2\}
and

j\in\{0,1\}
.

(4)

H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{0}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is a discrete torsion group.

(5)

H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{0}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is an infinite discrete torsion group.

(6)

H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is neither a profinite nor a discrete torsion group.

Proof.

We only need to explain (4) and (6). But the former follows from

[
48
, Thm. 1.2.2]
and the isomorphism
H^{2}({\mathcal{X}},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})\cong H^{2}(Y,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}})
while the latter follows from the perfectness of
the Brauer-Manin pairing for
X
(e.g., see
[
46
, § 9]
).
∎

Using Lemma
2.1
, Proposition
3.6
and considering the
Pontryagin duals associated to
(
3.11
), we get a chain complex in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
:

(3.14)

\cdots\to H^{i+1}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\xrightarrow{\partial^{\star}}H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\xrightarrow{(u^{*})^{\star}}H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\hskip 28.45274pt

\hskip 256.0748pt\xrightarrow{(\iota_{*})^{\star}}H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\to\cdots.

Lemma 3.7
.

The sequence (
3.14
) is exact.

Proof.

We look at the exact sequences

(3.15)

0\to{\rm Ker}(\iota_{*})\to H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to{\rm Ker}(u^{*})\to 0;

(3.16)

0\to{\rm Ker}(u^{*})\to H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to F_{ij}\to 0;

(3.17)

0\to F_{ij}\to H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to{\rm Ker}(\iota_{*})\to 0.

It follows from Proposition
3.6
and the definition of the topologies of the
various groups in (
3.11
) that the above three are exact
sequences in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
and they satisfy the hypothesis of Lemma
2.4
.
We can therefore apply this lemma to get short exact sequences

(3.18)

0\to{\rm Ker}(u^{*})^{\star}\to H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\to{\rm Ker}(\iota_{*})^{\star}\to 0;

(3.19)

0\to{\rm Ker}(\iota_{*})^{\star}\to H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\to(F_{ij})^{\star}\to 0;

(3.20)

0\to(F_{ij})^{\star}\to H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\to{\rm Ker}(u^{*})^{\star}\to 0.

The desired result follows by piecing these exact sequences together.
∎

4.
Duality for logarithmic Hodge-Witt
cohomology

We continue to work under the set-up of §
2.3
.
In this section, we shall prove the duality theorem for the logarithmic Hodge-Witt
cohomology of the proper curve
X
over
k
. We begin by constructing the trace map.
For a scheme
Z
, we let
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z)
denote the derived category of étale
sheaves of abelian groups on
Z
. We let
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(Z,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m)
denote the
derived category of étale sheaves of
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m
-modules on
Z
with

m\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(Z)
.
For a local ring
A
, we shall let
A^{h}
(resp.

A^{sh}
, resp.
\widehat{A}
) denote the Henselization (resp. strict Henselization,
resp. completion) of
A
with respect to its maximal ideal. For a closed point

x\in X
, we shall let
K_{x}
(resp.
K^{sh}_{x}
, resp.
\widehat{K}_{x}
) denote the
quotient field of the Henselization
{\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x}
(resp. strict Henselization

{\mathcal{O}}^{sh}_{X,x}
, resp. completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}
) of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
.
We fix an integer
n\geq 1
.

4.1.
The trace maps

We let
j\colon{\rm Spec\,}(K)\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of the generic point of
X
and let

\iota_{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of a closed point.
By the Gersten resolution of the logarithmic Hodge-Witt sheaves, one has an exact
sequence of étale sheaves

(4.1)

0\to W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}\to j_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}}{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}(\iota_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\to 0,

where
{\operatorname{res}}
is the sum of residue maps in Kato’s complex
(see
[
53
, Thm. 5.2]
).
It follows that there is an exact triangle

(4.2)

0\to\left({\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}(\iota_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\right)[-1]\to W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}\to j_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\to 0

in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X)
. We let
\nu^{2}_{n,X}
denote the complex

\left(j_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}}{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}(\iota_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\right)
.

For every
x\in X_{(0)}
, we have the canonical trace map

\epsilon_{x}\colon(f_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\to W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k,{\operatorname{log}}}
, induced by the
norm
N_{x}\colon(f_{x})_{*}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k(x)})\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k}
via the dlog map,
where
f_{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
is the structure map.
We let
\epsilon_{X}:=\sum_{x}\epsilon_{x}
. By the reciprocity law for Milnor

K
-theory (e.g., see
[
22
, Lem. 4]
), it is clear that the composite map

g_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}}{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}(f_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{X}}W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k,{\operatorname{log}}}

is zero if we let
g=f\circ j
, where recall that
f\colon X\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
is the
structure map. In other words, we have a morphism of complexes

(4.3)

f_{*}\colon f_{*}(\nu^{2}_{n,X})\to W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k,{\operatorname{log}}}[-1]

whose composition with
(f_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})[-1]\to f_{*}(\nu^{2}_{n,X})

is
\epsilon_{x}[-1]
for every
x\in X_{(0)}
.
We now prove some key lemmas.

Lemma 4.1
.

One has
R^{q}j_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})=0
for
q\geq 1
.

Proof.

The stalk of
R^{q}j_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})
at a closed point
x\in X
is

H^{q}(K^{sh}_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K^{sh}_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})
. To show that this cohomology is zero,
we can assume
q=1
because
cd_{p}(K^{sh}_{x})\leq 1
.
By
[
24
, Lem. 21]
, we can pass to the completion
\widehat{K^{sh}_{x}}

of
K^{sh}_{x}
.
We let
A_{x}
denote the completion of
{\mathcal{O}}^{sh}_{X,x},\ F_{x}=\widehat{K^{sh}_{x}}
and
consider the exact sequence

(4.4)

H^{1}(A_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{A_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{1}(F_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{F_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{2}_{\overline{x}}(A_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{A_{x},{\operatorname{log}}}),

where
\overline{x}
is the closed point of
{\rm Spec\,}(A_{x})
. By the proper base change theorem,
we have
H^{1}(A_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{A_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})\cong H^{1}(k(\overline{x}),\iota^{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{A_{x},{\operatorname{log}}}))
, where

\iota\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(\overline{x}))\hookrightarrow{\rm Spec\,}(A_{x})
is the inclusion of the closed
point. But the latter group vanishes since
k(\overline{x})
is
separably closed (e.g., see
[
35
, Thm. VI.1.1]
). It remains to show that

H^{2}_{\overline{x}}(A_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{A_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})=0
.

Since
k(\overline{x})
is separably closed, we have the well known equality

[k(\overline{x}):k(\overline{x})^{p}]=p
(e.g., see
[
25
, § 3]
).
Since
F_{x}\cong k(\overline{x})((T))
, it is an elementary exercise
that
[F_{x}:(F_{x})^{p}]=p^{2}
(e.g., see the proof of
[
61
, Lem. 1.4.5]
).
We therefore conclude from
[
53
, Thm. 3.2]

that there is a purity isomorphism
\iota_{*}\colon H^{1}(k(\overline{x}),W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(\overline{x}),{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2}_{\overline{x}}(A_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{A_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})
. This proves our claim because

H^{1}(k(\overline{x}),W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(\overline{x}),{\operatorname{log}}})
is clearly zero.
To finish the proof of the lemma, we note that the stalk of
R^{q}j_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})

at the generic point of
X
is
H^{q}(K_{s},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K_{s},{\operatorname{log}}})
, which is
zero if
q\geq 1
.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

The canonical map
f_{*}(\nu^{2}_{n,X})\to Rf_{*}(\nu^{2}_{n,X})
is an isomorphism in

{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k)
.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
4.1
,
it suffices to show that
R^{q}f_{*}(j_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}}))=0

and
R^{q}f_{*}((\iota_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}}))=0
for all
q\geq 1
and

x\in X_{(0)}
. Using the exactness of
(\iota_{x})_{*}
for
x\in X_{(0)}
and
Lemma
4.1
for the generic point of
X
, it suffices to show that

R^{q}g_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})=0
and
R^{q}(f_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})=0

for all
q\geq 1
and
x\in X_{(0)}
.
This latter claim for
x\in X_{(0)}
is obvious since

f_{x}
is a finite morphism.

On the other hand, we have

R^{q}g_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\cong H^{q}(K^{\prime},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K^{\prime},{\operatorname{log}}})
,
where
K^{\prime}
is the product of the function fields of the
connected components of
X_{s}:=X\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(k)}{\rm Spec\,}(k_{s})
. We are now done because

H^{q}(K^{\prime},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K^{\prime},{\operatorname{log}}})
is clearly zero if
q\geq 2
, and its
vanishing for
q=1
was shown by Kato-Saito
[
25
]

(see the proof of their Lemma 1(2) on p. 252). We remark that even though the
cited lemma of Kato-Saito assumes that
X
is smooth over
k
, the proof of
the vanishing of
H^{1}(K^{\prime},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K^{\prime},{\operatorname{log}}})
only requires
X
to be generically
smooth over
k
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

There exists a canonical morphism

{{\operatorname{tr}}}_{X}\colon Rf_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k,{\operatorname{log}}}[-1]
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(k)
whose composition with the canonical
morphisms

(f_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}}[-1])\to f_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to Rf_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})

is
\epsilon_{x}[-1]
for every
x\in X_{(0)}
.

Proof.

Combine the isomorphism
W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\nu^{2}_{n,X}

in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X)
with Lemma
4.2
and the morphism
f_{*}
in
(
4.3
).
∎

Recall that there is a canonical isomorphism
{\rm inv}_{k}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(k)\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
. If
{k^{\prime}}/k
is a finite field extension of degree
m
and

v\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k^{\prime})\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
is the projection, then the norm map

N_{{k^{\prime}}/k}\colon v_{*}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k^{\prime}})\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k}
between the étale sheaves
induces the pull-back and push-forward maps

v^{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(k)\to{\operatorname{Br}}(k^{\prime})
and
v_{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(k^{\prime})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(k)

such that
{\rm inv}_{k^{\prime}}\circ v^{*}=m({\rm inv}_{k})
on
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and

{\rm inv}_{k}\circ v_{*}={\rm inv}_{k^{\prime}}
.

Identifying
{}_{p^{n}}{\operatorname{Br}}(k^{\prime})
with
H^{1}(k^{\prime},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k^{\prime},{\operatorname{log}}})
,
we get a commutative diagram

(4.5)

\textstyle{H^{1}(k^{\prime},W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k^{\prime},{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{*}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm inv}_{k^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(k,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm inv}_{k}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}}

in which the horizontal and the diagonal arrows are isomorphisms.
It follows that
v_{*}
is an isomorphism such that
{\rm inv}_{k}\circ v_{*}={\rm inv}_{k^{\prime}}
. We shall therefore identify
v_{*}
with the identity map of

{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}
. Similarly, we shall identify
v_{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(k^{\prime})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(k)
with
the identity map of
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
throughout this paper.
In the sequel, we shall write
v_{*}=\epsilon_{x}
if

k^{\prime}=k(x)
for some closed point
x
on a curve over
k
.

Proposition 4.4
.

There exists a unique homomorphism
{\rm Tr}_{X}\colon H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}
such that
{\rm Tr}_{X}\circ(\iota_{x})_{*}
is the identity map for every

x\in X_{(0)}
. Furthermore,
{\rm Tr}_{X}
is bijective.

Proof.

By (
4.1
) and Lemma
4.1
, we have an exact sequence

(4.6)

H^{1}(K,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{\operatorname{res}}}{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{1}(k(x),W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to 0.

This implies that
H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is generated by
the images of
(\iota_{x})_{*}(1)
as
x
varies in
X_{(0)}
. The uniqueness assertion
follows immediately from this. The existence follows directly from Lemma
4.3

and (
4.5
). The claim that
{\rm Tr}_{X}
is bijective follows from
the fact that its source and target are finite of equal
cardinality by Theorem
2.8
and Lemma
3.2
while

{\rm Tr}_{X}\circ(\iota_{x})_{*}
is bijective.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

The assertion that
H^{2}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
is isomorphic to
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}

is not new (at least when
X
is smooth over
k
)
as it was already shown by Kato-Saito in
[
25
, Prop. 4]
.
It also follows from Theorem
2.8
and Lemma
3.2
.
However, the above proposition provides an explicit isomorphism which has the
advantage that it commutes with the trace maps for all closed points of
X
.
This is unclear in the construction of
[
25
]
. We shall
need this explicit nature of
{\rm Tr}_{X}
later in the paper in a critical way.
Another advantage is that it does not require
X
to be smooth
everywhere. Although this flexibility is not important in this paper,
it will be vital in the study of duality and class field theory for open smooth
curves over
k
which do not admit smooth compactifications.
We were unable to prove if
{\rm Tr}_{X}
coincides with the isomorphism of
Kato-Saito, whose construction, we believe, is quite intricate.

Given
x\in X_{(0)}
, we now look at the canonical maps of étale sheaves

(\iota_{x})_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\to(\iota_{x})_{*}R(\iota_{x})^{!}(W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}},

on
X
, where
[t_{x}]
is the Teichmüller image of the chosen uniformizer

t_{x}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
and the first arrow is obtained by taking cup product with

{\rm dlog}([t_{x}])
. This arrow is an isomorphism
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X)
by
[
61
, Cor. 1.3.14]
. The same is also true if we replace
X
by

{\rm Spec\,}({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x})
.

Furthermore, the diagram of the induced maps on cohomology

(4.7)

\textstyle{H^{1}(k(x),W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota_{x})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cup[t_{x}]}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cup[t_{x}]}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{x}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(K_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{x}}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{x}({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x},{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x},{\operatorname{log}}})}

is commutative, where the left vertical arrow
\cup[t_{x}]
is the isomorphism of

[
21
, § 3.2, Lem. 3]
. It follows from the proper base change theorem that
the group on the bottom right corner is zero. Since the
other two groups in the bottom row are isomorphic to
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}

(e.g., see
[
21
, Prop. 3.1]
), it follows that
\partial_{x}
is
bijective.

Using Proposition
4.4
and (
4.7
), we get a commutative
diagram

(4.8)

\textstyle{H^{1}(k(x),W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota_{x})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{h_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{X}}

\textstyle{H^{1}(k,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm inv}_{k}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}},}

where
h_{x}:=\cup[t_{x}]
is an isomorphism.

Letting
{\rm Tr}_{x}:={\rm inv}_{k}\circ\epsilon_{x}\circ(h_{x})^{-1}
, we get the
following.

Corollary 4.6
.

For every
x\in X_{(0)}
, there is a commutative diagram

(4.9)

\textstyle{H^{1}(k(x),W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{k(x),{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\iota_{x})_{*}}

\textstyle{H^{1}(K_{x},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{K_{x},{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{x}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}}

in which all arrows are isomorphisms.

4.2.
Duality theorem for
H^{*}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})

Recall that a pairing between locally compact Hausdorff topological abelian groups

\lambda\colon A\times B\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is called continuous if
\lambda
is
continuous with respect to the product topology of
A\times B
.
Equivalently, either (and hence both) of the maps
A\to B^{\vee}
and

B\to A^{\vee}
is continuous and factors through the
continuous dual (e.g., see
[
18
, Prop. A.14]
).

\lambda
is called non degenerate on the left (resp. right) if the
induced map
A\to B^{\vee}
(resp.
B\to A^{\vee}
) is injective.
One says that
\lambda
is perfect (in particular, continuous) if it induces isomorphisms

A\xrightarrow{\cong}B^{\star}
and
B\xrightarrow{\cong}A^{\star}
of topological
abelian groups.

We now return to the set-up of §
2.3
.
Recall from Proposition
4.4
that there is a canonical isomorphism
of
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}
-modules

(4.10)

{\rm Tr}_{X}\colon H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}.

Using Lemma
3.2
, we get a unique isomorphism

{\rm Tr}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\colon H^{3}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}

such that
{\rm Tr}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\circ\partial={\rm Tr}_{X}
(cf. Theorem
2.8
).
The cup product pairing

(4.11)

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\times H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\to H^{3}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{\rm Tr}_{\mathcal{X}}}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}

gives rise to maps

H^{i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\Phi^{ij}_{\mathcal{X}}}H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star};\ H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\Psi^{ij}_{\mathcal{X}}}H^{3-i}({\mathcal{X}},W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}

in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
. Furthermore, it follows from Theorem
2.8
that these maps are
topological isomorphisms. In other words, (
4.11
) is a perfect pairing
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
.

We shall now prove our duality theorem for the logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology
of
X
. This will prove Theorem
1.4
. This duality recovers as well as
generalizes
[
25
, Prop. 4]
. Let the notations and hypotheses be as in
§
2.3
. In particular,
X
is a geometrically connected, regular, and
generically smooth curve over the local field
k
of exponential characteristic

p>1
.
{\mathcal{X}}
is a projective and flat regular semi-stable model of
X
over

{\rm Spec\,}({\mathcal{O}}_{k})
.

Theorem 4.7
.

For every pair of integers
i,j\geq 0
, the cup product on
logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology induces a perfect pairing of topological
abelian groups

(4.12)

H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\times H^{2-i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{\cup}H^{2}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\xrightarrow{{\rm Tr}_{X}}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}.

Proof.

The existence of the bilinear pairing is clear. We shall prove its perfectness in
several steps. In order to save space, we
shall write all cohomology groups by suppressing the underlying schemes. We shall
also use the short hand
{\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
for
W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}},{\operatorname{log}}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X}
for

W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}
.

We now fix
i,j\geq 0
and we look at the diagram

(4.13)

\textstyle{H^{i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi^{ij}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}
\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{ij}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}
\scriptstyle{{2}}
\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{i+1}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi^{i+1j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}
\textstyle{H^{i+1}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{i+1j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}}
\textstyle{H^{3-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{1}}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\vee},}

where the vertical arrows on the lower floor are the canonical inclusions.
The top and the bottom rows are clearly exact while the exactness of the middle row is
shown in Lemma
3.7
. All squares on the lower floor clearly commute. We
let the composite vertical arrow in the middle be the map
(-1)^{i}\widetilde{\Phi}^{ij}_{X}
,
where
\widetilde{\Phi}^{ij}_{X}\colon H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})\to H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})^{\vee}

is induced by (
4.12
).
We now show that the composition of each of the top and the following
bottom squares in (
4.13
) commutes. We now show that the
composition of each of the squares on the upper floor with the
one below it on the lower floor in (
4.13
) commutes.

The composite squares on the left and right corners commute because they are induced by
the canonical commutative diagram

(4.14)

\textstyle{\iota_{*}R\iota^{!}{\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\times\hskip 8.5359pt{\mathcal{F}}^{j^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{\iota_{*}R\iota^{!}{\mathcal{F}}^{j+j^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hskip 17.07182pt{\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\hskip 14.22636pt\times\hskip 14.22636pt\iota_{*}R\iota^{!}{\mathcal{F}}^{j^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{\iota_{*}R\iota^{!}{\mathcal{F}}^{j+j^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{X}}}.}

For the two middle composite squares, note that if we apply the
classical formula relating the cup product and the boundary maps in
sheaf cohomology (e.g., see
[
3
, Thm. 7.1]
or
[
56
, Lem. 3.2]
)
to the cohomology sequences associated to the (derived) tensor product
of
{\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
with the exact triangle

\iota_{*}R\iota^{!}{\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to{\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\to Ru_{*}{\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X}

for
0\leq j\leq 2
, we get that the diagram

(4.15)

\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\times H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}}
\textstyle{H^{3}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2}_{{\mathcal{X}}})}
\textstyle{H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})\times H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathcal{F}}^{2}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}

is commutative up to multiplication by
(-1)^{i}
. Since
{\rm Tr}_{{\mathcal{X}}}\circ\partial={\rm Tr}_{X}
,
it follows that the two middle composite squares commute.

Since all vertical arrows on the lower floor of (
4.13
) are injective
and all lower squares are commutative, it follows that the squares on the left and
the right corners on the upper floor are commutative. Next, an easy diagram chase shows
that the square labeled (1) is Cartesian. This implies that the image of the
composite middle vertical arrow lies in the subgroup
H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})^{\star}
.
Furthermore, if we let
\Phi^{ij}_{X}\colon H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})\to H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})^{\star}
denote the induced map, then
all squares on the upper floor commute. Since all vertical arrows on this floor,
