except possibly the middle one, are isomorphisms, it follows that
\Phi^{ij}_{X}
is also an
isomorphism.

To finish the proof of the theorem, it remains to show that
\Phi^{ij}_{X}
is a
continuous and open homomorphism. To show this, recall that
F_{ij}

is a quotient of
H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})
and is an open subgroup of
H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})
such
that
{H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})}/{F_{ij}}
is discrete. Hence, it suffices to show in
the square labeled (2) in (
4.13
) that the composite map

H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})\to H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\to H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})^{\star}

is continuous and open. Let us call this composite map
\theta
.
Now, we have already seen above that
\Phi^{ij}_{{\mathcal{X}}}
is a
topological isomorphism. On the other hand,

H^{3-i}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\to H^{2-i}({\mathcal{F}}^{2-j}_{X})^{\star}
is clearly continuous
because the Pontryagin dual is an auto-functor in the category of locally compact
Hausdorff topological abelian groups. This proves that
\theta
is continuous.

To show that
\theta
is open, it suffices to show that the map

H^{i+1}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\to H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})^{\star}
is open for any
i,j\geq 0
.
From (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), we see that this
map has a factorization

(4.16)

H^{i+1}_{Y}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{{\mathcal{X}}})^{\star}\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Im}(\partial^{\star})={\rm Ker}(\iota_{*})^{\star}\hookrightarrow H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})^{\star}.

The first arrow in this factorization is clearly open because it is a quotient map
in
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Pfd}}}
by Lemma
2.3
. On the other hand,
(F_{ij})^{\star}
is
discrete and
H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})^{\star}\twoheadrightarrow(F_{ij})^{\star}
is a continuous surjective
homomorphism. It follows that its kernel
{\rm Im}(\partial^{\star})={\rm Ker}(\iota_{*})^{\star}
is open in

H^{i}({\mathcal{F}}^{j}_{X})^{\star}
. We have thus shown that the composite map in
(
4.16
) is open. This concludes the proof.
∎

For integers
m,n\geq 1
, we have a diagram of bilinear parings

(4.17)

\textstyle{{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i,X}}/{p^{m}}\times{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{j,X}}/{p^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{p^{n}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i+j,X}}/{p^{m}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{n}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i,X}}/{p^{m+n}}\times{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{j,X}}/{p^{m+n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{\rm can}}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{i+j,X}}/{p^{m+n}},}

where ‘
{\operatorname{\rm can}}
’ indicates the canonical surjection. It easily follows from the
bilinearity of the product in Milnor
K
-theory that this diagram is commutative.
Passing to the cohomology and using (
2.3
), we get a bilinear pairing

(4.18)

``{{\underset{n}{\varinjlim}}}"H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\times``{{\underset{n}{\varprojlim}}}"H^{2-i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to``{{\underset{n}{\varinjlim}}}"{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{p^{n}}

between ind-abelian and pro-abelian groups.

We endow
{\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})

with the direct limit (i.e., the weak) topology and

{\varprojlim}_{n}\ H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
the inverse limit
topology. Taking the limits in (
4.18
) and using
Lemma
2.6
, we get the following.

Corollary 4.8
.

There is a pairing of topological abelian groups

{\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\times{\varprojlim}_{n}\ H^{2-i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}}

which induces an isomorphism of abelian groups

{\varprojlim}_{n}\ H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to\left({\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{2-i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\right)^{\star},

a continuous epimorphism of topological abelian groups

{\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\to\left({\varprojlim}_{n}\ H^{2-i}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{2-j}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\right)^{\star}.

5.
The relative Picard scheme

After the duality theorem for the logarithmic Hodge-Witt cohomology, the next key
ingredients in the proof of Theorem
1.1
and the Brauer-Manin pairing for
modulus pairs are the representability and certain topological properties of the relative
Picard group. These results are of independent interest and we shall establish
them in the next two sections using
[
29
]
and
[
50
]
.

We fix an arbitrary field
k
of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
. We let

X
be a smooth projective geometrically connected curve over
k
and let
D\subset X

be an effective Cartier divisor whose support is a non-empty
finite closed subset
S=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
of
X
. We shall use
{\mathfrak{m}}
as another
notation for
D
and call it the modulus divisor, following the terminology of

[
50
]
. We let
\iota\colon D\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion.
We shall call
(X,D)
a modulus pair (of dimension one).
We write
D=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}n_{i}[x_{i}]
as a Weil divisor and let
\deg(D)=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}n_{i}[k(x_{i}):k]
denote the degree of
D
. Note that
\deg(D)=1
if and
only if
D={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
, where
x\in X(k)
.
We let
X^{o}=X\setminus S
.
We let
A={\mathcal{O}}_{X,S}
and
I\subset A
the ideal defining
D
in
{\rm Spec\,}(A)
.
We write
A_{D}=A/I
. We let
K
denote the function field of
X
.
For any
k
-scheme
Z
, we let
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(Z)
denote the free abelian group on
Z_{(0)}
.
We shall let
{\mathcal{O}}(Z)
(resp.

{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(Z)
) denote the group
H^{0}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}_{Z})
(resp.
H^{0}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{Z})
).

5.1.
Relative Picard and 0-cycles with modulus

Recall that for an integral quasi-projective scheme
Y
over
k
and an
effective Cartier divisor
E\subset Y
, the Chow group of 0-cycles with modulus

{\rm CH}_{0}(Y|E)
is defined to be the quotient of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(Y\setminus E)
by the subgroup

{\mathcal{R}}_{0}(Y|E)
generated by the 0-cycles
\nu_{*}({\rm div}(f))
, where
\nu\colon C_{n}\to Y

is the canonical morphism from the normalization of an integral curve
C\subset Y

not contained in
E

and
f\in{\rm Ker}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{C_{n},\nu^{-1}(E)}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(\nu^{*}(E)))
.
The relative Picard group
{\rm Pic}(Y|E)
(usually written as

{\rm Pic}(Y,E)
in the literature) is the set of isomorphism classes
of pairs
({\mathcal{L}},u\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{E}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{L}}|_{E})
, where

{\mathcal{L}}
is an invertible sheaf on
Y
and
{\mathcal{L}}|_{E}:={\mathcal{L}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}}{\mathcal{O}}_{E}
.
One says that
({\mathcal{L}},u)\cong({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
if there is an isomorphism

w\colon{\mathcal{L}}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
such that
u^{\prime}=w|_{E}\circ u
.

{\rm Pic}(Y|E)
is an abelian group under the tensor product of invertible sheaves and
their trivializations along
E
whose identity element is
({\mathcal{O}}_{Y},{\operatorname{id}})
.
There is a canonical homomorphism
{\rm Pic}(Y|E)\to{\rm Pic}(Y)
which is surjective if

\text{\rm dim}(Y)=1
.

We now specialize to the one-dimensional modulus pair
(X,D)
over
k
that we fixed
above. Let
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)
denote the subgroup of pairs
({\mathcal{L}},u)
such that

\deg({\mathcal{L}})=0
. We let
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)_{0}
be the kernel of the
composite map
\deg\colon{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm CH}_{0}(X)\xrightarrow{\deg}{\mathbb{Z}}
.
There is a degree preserving cycle class map
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{X|D}\colon{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}(X|D)

(e.g., see
[
30
, Lem. 3.1]
) such that
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{X|D}([Q])=({\mathcal{O}}_{X}(Q),u(1)=f^{-1})
, where
Q\in X^{o}_{(0)}
and
(f)\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X,S\cup\{Q\}}

is the ideal of
Q
.

Lemma 5.1
.

The homomorphism
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{X|D}
is bijective.

Proof.

This is a combination of
[
55
, Lemmas 2.1, 2.3, 2.4]
.
∎

From the above definitions, it is clear that there is an exact sequence

(5.1)

0\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(X)\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D)\xrightarrow{\partial_{X|D}}{\rm Pic}(X|D)\xrightarrow{\vartheta_{X|D}}{\rm Pic}(X)\to 0,

where
\iota^{*}
is the canonical inclusion and

\partial_{X|D}(u)=({\mathcal{O}}_{X},u\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{D}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}|_{D})
.
Note that this exact sequence holds even if
X
is only integral.
In §
7
, we shall study the exactness of the sequence obtained by
taking the Pontryagin dual of (
5.1
).
Here, we note the following consequence.

Lemma 5.2
.

The canonical map
{\rm Pic}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}(X)
is an isomorphism if
\deg(D)=1
,

5.2.
The singular curve attached to
(X,D)

Assume now that
\deg(D)\geq 2
. In order to prove
the representability of
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
, the strategy is to replace it with the ordinary
Picard group of a singular curve (without modulus).
The following result from
[
50
, Chap. IV, § 1, no. 3, 4]
helps us in
achieving this.

Proposition 5.3
.

There exists a unique geometrically integral
k
-scheme
X_{\mathfrak{m}}

together with a finite morphism
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon X\to X_{\mathfrak{m}}
such that the
following hold.

(1)

\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(S)
is the unique singular point
P_{0}\in X_{\mathfrak{m}}
which is
k
-rational.

(2)

\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon X^{o}\xrightarrow{\cong}X_{\mathfrak{m}}\setminus\{P_{0}\}
.

(3)

If we let
I_{\mathfrak{m}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}},P_{0}}
denote the maximal ideal, then
the pull-back map
\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}},P_{0}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X,S}

induces a bijection
I_{\mathfrak{m}}\xrightarrow{\cong}I
.

Proof.

Since we do not assume any condition on
k
while the construction of
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
given
in
[
50
]
assumes
k
to be algebraically closed, we sketch the proof of
this proposition to explain that this additional hypothesis is not necessary.

Since
S
has an affine neighborhood in
X
, one easily observes that it suffices
to show the existence of
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
when
X={\rm Spec\,}(R)
is affine. We let

B=k+I=\{a+b|a\in k,b\in I\}\subset A
. Then
B
is a
k
-subalgebra
of
A
and there is a commutative diagram of short sequences of
B
-modules:

(5.2)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{A/B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{A_{D}}/k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
A_{D}
is finite over
k
, it follows from the above diagram that
there is a finite-dimensional
k
-vector space
V\subset A
such that

A=V+I
. It follows that there is a surjective map of

B
-modules
(k\oplus V)\otimes_{k}B\twoheadrightarrow A
. In particular,
A
is
a finite
B
-module. An application of the Eakin-Nagata theorem
(e.g., see
[
34
, Thm. 3.7]
) shows that
B
is Noetherian.
Using
[
1
, Thm. 5.10]
, we conclude furthermore that
B
is a 1-dimensional
local ring with maximal ideal
I
.

We now let
L
be the fraction field of
B
and
take the tensor product of the middle row in (
5.2
)
with
L
over
B
to get a commutative diagram of short exact sequences

(5.3)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A/B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\otimes_{B}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({A_{D}}/k)\otimes_{B}L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
{A_{D}}/k
is a torsion
B
-module, we get
({A_{D}}/k)\otimes_{B}L=0
.
Since
A\otimes_{B}L
is a localization of
A
, the map
A\to A\otimes_{B}L

is injective. It follows that
A\subset L
. This implies that
the inclusion
L\hookrightarrow K
is a bijection. Putting everything together, we conclude
that the conditions (1) to (3) of the proposition hold if we replace
X

by
{\rm Spec\,}(A)
. We now let
B^{\prime}
be the intersection (in
K
)
of the localizations of
R

at all maximal ideals except those supported on
S
and let

R^{\prime}=B\cap B^{\prime}
. It is then easy to check that
X_{\mathfrak{m}}:={\rm Spec\,}(R^{\prime})
is the
desired singular curve with the conductor ideal
I_{\mathfrak{m}}:=I
.
Since
R^{\prime}_{\overline{k}}\hookrightarrow R_{\overline{k}}
and the latter ring is an integral domain,
it follows that
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
is geometrically integral. Note also that
P
has to be
a singular point of
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
since
\deg(k(P))=1
while
\deg(D)\geq 2
, by our
assumption.
∎

Let
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
be the singular curve with the unique singular point
P_{0}

associated to the modulus pair
(X,D)
as obtained in Proposition
5.3
.
We shall call this ‘the
{\mathfrak{m}}
-contraction of
X
’ in the sequel.
We shall identify
X_{\mathfrak{m}}\setminus\{P_{0}\}
with
X^{o}
via
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}
throughout our
discussion.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
{k^{\prime}}/k
be any field extension and let
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}:=D_{k^{\prime}}\subset X_{k^{\prime}}
. Then
the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
-contraction of
X_{k^{\prime}}
is canonically isomorphic to
(X_{\mathfrak{m}})_{k^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the construction of
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
using its property
that
P_{0}\in X_{\mathfrak{m}}(k)
.
∎

Recall that the Levine-Weibel Chow group of 0-cycles
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(Y)
of an integral
curve
Y
over
k
is the quotient of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(Y_{\rm reg})
by the subgroup generated by the
divisors of those rational functions on
Y
which lie in
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{Y,Y_{\rm sing}}
.
It is well known that assigning each closed point of
Y_{\rm reg}
its
class in
{\rm Pic}(Y)
induces a canonical cycle class isomorphism

{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{Y}\colon{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(Y)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Pic}(Y)

(e.g., see
[
2
, Lem. 3.12]
).
The following is a key lemma.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
{k^{\prime}}/k
be any field extension.
Then the identity map of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})
induces via
\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}
,
a degree preserving isomorphism

\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon{\rm Pic}((X_{\mathfrak{m}})_{k^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Pic}(X_{k^{\prime}}|D_{k^{\prime}})

whose composition with
{\rm Pic}(X_{k^{\prime}}|D_{k^{\prime}})\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X_{k^{\prime}})
is the usual
pull-back between the Picard groups of
(X_{\mathfrak{m}})_{k^{\prime}}
and
X_{k^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Since the base field
k
is arbitrary, we can assume
k^{\prime}=k
by
virtue of Lemma
5.4
.
To construct
\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, we can replace
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
and
{\rm Pic}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
with

{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)
and
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
, respectively, via the cycle class isomorphisms.
We first show that
\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}({\rm div}(f))=0
if
f\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}},P_{0}}
.
To that end, we let
g
denote the residue class of
f
in

k(P_{0})^{\times}=k^{\times}
and let
\tilde{f}=fg^{-1}
. Then we see that

\tilde{f}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}},P_{0}}
is
such that
{\rm div}(f)={\rm div}(\tilde{f})
in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})
.
Since
\tilde{f}\in K_{1}(A,I)
, we also see that

\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}({\rm div}(\tilde{f}))=0
in
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)
. This proves the desired
factorization of the pull-back map between the Picard groups. It is clear that

\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is degree preserving (e.g., see
[
54
, Tag 0AYU, Lem. 33.44.4]
).

To complete the proof of the lemma, we look at the commutative diagram of
short exact sequences

(5.4)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{A_{D}^{\times}}/{k^{\times}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{A^{\times}_{D}}/{k^{\times}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}(X,D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

This shows that the middle vertical arrow is an isomorphism.
∎

It follows from Lemma
5.5
that there is commutative diagram

(5.5)

\textstyle{{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{X|D}}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}(X|D),}

in which all arrows are isomorphisms and degree preserving. We can now prove the
existence of the relative Picard variety of the modulus pair
(X,D)
.

Theorem 5.6
.

There exists a group scheme
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)

over
k
such that
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)(k^{\prime})\cong{\rm Pic}(X_{k^{\prime}}|D_{k^{\prime}})
for every field
extension
{k^{\prime}}/{k}
. The identity component
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
of
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)

is a smooth and irreducible quasi-projective group scheme over
k
of dimension
equal to
\text{\rm dim}_{k}H^{1}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}})
such that

{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)(k^{\prime})\cong{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{k^{\prime}}|D_{k^{\prime}})
. If
X^{o}(k)\neq\emptyset
, then
the connected components of

{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)
are parameterized by
{\mathbb{Z}}
and each component is isomorphic
to
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
. We have
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)_{k^{\prime}}\cong{\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{k^{\prime}}|D_{k^{\prime}})
for every
field extension
{k^{\prime}}/{k}
. If
f\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to(X,D)
is a strict morphism of
modulus pairs (i.e.,
f^{*}(D)=D^{\prime}
), then there is a canonical homomorphism of group
schemes
f^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)\to{\mathbf{Pic}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})
.

Proof.

If
\deg(D)=1
, then
D={\rm Spec\,}(k(x))
for some
x\in X(k)
.
In this case, we have
{\rm Pic}(X_{k^{\prime}}|D_{k^{\prime}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Pic}(X_{k^{\prime}})
for
every field extension
{k^{\prime}}/{k}
by Lemma
5.2
. The theorem therefore
follows directly from its known case
D=\emptyset
. We now assume
\deg(D)\geq 2

and let
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon X\to X_{\mathfrak{m}}
be the
{\mathfrak{m}}
-contraction of
X
(cf.
Proposition
5.3
).
Since
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
is geometrically integral, it follows from the flat base change property
of the higher direct images of coherent sheaves that for every
k
-scheme
T
,
the map
{\mathcal{O}}_{T}\to(f_{T})_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}\times T})
is an isomorphism of sheaves on
T
,
where
f\colon X_{\mathfrak{m}}\to{\rm Spec\,}(k)
is the structure map and
f_{T}\colon X_{\mathfrak{m}}\times T\to T
is the base change of
f
to
T
. Since
P_{0}\in X_{\mathfrak{m}}(k)
,
the map
f
has a section.

Using the last two properties of
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
, we conclude
from
[
29
, Thm. 9.2.5, 9.4.8]
that there exists a group scheme
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})

over
k
such that
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})(T)\cong{{\rm Pic}(X_{\mathfrak{m}}\times T)}/{{\rm Pic}(T)}
for
every
k
-scheme
T
. In particular, we have

{\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})(k^{\prime})\cong{\rm Pic}((X_{\mathfrak{m}})_{k^{\prime}})
for every field
extension
{k^{\prime}}/{k}
. Furthermore, the identity component
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
of

{\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
is a geometrically irreducible quasi-projective group scheme over
k

and
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})(k^{\prime})\cong{\rm Pic}^{0}((X_{\mathfrak{m}})_{k^{\prime}})
by
[
29
, Lem. 9.5.1]
.
It follows from
[
40
, Cor. 4.2]
(see also
[
29
, Prop. 9.5.19]
)
and the fppf descent property of smoothness that

{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
is a smooth group scheme over
k
, i.e., it is an
algebraic group over
k
. In particular, it is geometrically integral.
The assertion about
\text{\rm dim}({\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D))
follows from its smoothness and

[
29
, Cor. 9.5.13]
.

If
P\in X^{o}(k)
, then the connected components of
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
are
parameterized by
{\mathbb{Z}}
, and each connected component is isomorphic to
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})

via the transformation
{\mathcal{L}}\mapsto{\mathcal{L}}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(-mP)
on the scheme points,
where
\deg({\mathcal{L}})=m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. The theorem now follows
from Lemmas
5.4
and
5.5
if we let

{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D):={\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
. The last claim is clear because the Picard scheme
is a contravariant functor.
∎

5.3.
Picard scheme of singular curves

Suppose that
Y
is a geometrically integral singular curve with the normalization

f\colon X\to Y
. Let
Z\subset Y
be a conductor subscheme for
f
with
support
Y_{\rm sing}
and let
D=Z\times_{Y}X
. Assume that
X
is smooth over
k
.
Then
(X,D)
is a 1-dimensional modulus pair, and it is easy to see from the
construction in Proposition
5.3
that the
{\mathfrak{m}}
-contraction of
X
has a unique
factorization

(5.6)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathfrak{m}}}
\textstyle{X_{\mathfrak{m}}.}

We conclude from Theorem
5.6
that there are morphisms of group schemes

(5.7)

{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\psi}^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}}{\mathbf{Pic}}(Y)\xrightarrow{f^{*}}{\mathbf{Pic}}(X)

whose composition is the canonical morphism
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbf{Pic}}(X)
.

We now recall the exact sequence

(5.8)

0\to k^{\times}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(Z)\to H^{1}_{\rm nis}(Y,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(Y,Z)})\to H^{1}_{\rm nis}(Y,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,Y})\to 0.

Using the isomorphisms
H^{1}_{\rm nis}(Y,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(Y,Z)})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{1}_{\rm nis}(X,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(X,D)})\cong{\rm Pic}(X|D)
(see
[
30
, Lem. 3.1]
for the
second isomorphism), we get an exact sequence

(5.9)

0\to{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(Z)}/{k^{\times}}\to{\rm Pic}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}(Y)\to 0.

Since this remains true over every algebraic extension of
k
, it follows that
the map
\widetilde{\psi}^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}
in (
5.7
) is surjective.

6.
Relative Picard group over local fields

The goal of this section is to prove some topological properties of the relative
Picard group and the albanese map with respect to the adic topology that the
schemes acquire when the base field is a local field. Before we do this, we need to
study the smoothness properties of the albanese
morphism to the relative Picard scheme and of the morphism from the
relative to the ordinary Picard scheme.
We continue with the notations and assumptions of §
5

(in particular,
k
is still arbitrary).

6.1.
Generic smoothness of albanese map

Until we reach Theorem
6.6
, we shall assume in this subsection that

\deg(D)\geq 2
and
X^{o}(k)\neq\emptyset
. We fix a point
P\in X^{o}(k)
.
For any integer
n\geq 1
, let
\mathbf{Div}^{n}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
be the quasi-projective

k
-scheme which parameterizes effective Cartier divisors
E\subset X_{\mathfrak{m}}
of
degree
n
. By
[
29
, Defn. 9.4.6]
, there is a canonical morphism
of
k
-schemes
{{\rm AJ}}^{n}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}\colon\mathbf{Div}^{n}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
which
sends
E
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(E-nP)
if the support of
E
lies in
X(k)
.
Since
\mathbf{Div}^{1}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\cong X^{o}
by

[
29
, Exc. 9.3.8]
, we get a
k
-morphism
{\rm alb}_{X|D}\colon X^{o}\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
(called the albanese morphism)
which has the property that
{\rm alb}_{X|D}(x)=[x]-[P]
for
any
x\in X^{o}(k)
. Furthermore, it induces

(6.1)

{{\rm AJ}}_{X|D}\colon{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})_{0}\cong{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})(k).

For
n\geq 1
, the
n
-th power of the albanese map descends to a morphism

{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
, where
{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})
is the

n
-th symmetric power of
X^{o}
. We let

(6.2)

\phi^{n}_{X|D}\colon(X^{o})^{n}\xrightarrow{\pi_{n}}{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})\xrightarrow{{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}}{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}}).

denote the composite morphism.
Let
p_{a}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})=\text{\rm dim}_{k}(H^{1}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}))
denote the arithmetic genus of

X_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Let us now assume that
E\subset X_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an effective Cartier divisor disjoint
from the unique singular point
P_{0}
of
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
and let
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(E)
.
Equivalently,
E
is an effective Cartier divisor on
X
disjoint from
S
and

{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X}(E)\cong\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}({\mathcal{L}})
.
Since
E
is effective, there is a canonical inclusion
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
.
This yields a canonical inclusion

k\cong H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})
such that the composite
k\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\to A/I
is the canonical inclusion of rings
k\hookrightarrow A/I
.
Using this and the tensor product of the exact sequence

(6.3)

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}\to(\psi_{\mathfrak{m}})_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\to{{\mathcal{O}}_{D}}/{{\mathcal{O}}_{P_{0}}}\to 0

with
{\mathcal{L}}
, we get a commutative diagram with exact rows

(6.4)

\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A_{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{A_{D}}/k.}

Hence, we get an exact sequence

(6.5)

0\to H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D))\to H^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}})\to k\to 0.

For a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
, let
|{\mathcal{L}}|
denote the complete
linear system
{\operatorname{Proj}}_{k}(H^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}}))
.
If
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(E)
for an effective divisor
E
on
X^{o}
, we let

U_{E}=|{\mathcal{L}}|\setminus|{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D)|\subset|{\mathcal{L}}|
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any Cartier divisor
E
on
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
, the linear system
|{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(E)|

represents effective Cartier divisors
F
on
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
such that

{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(F)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(E)
as line bundles on
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Proof.

One checks that the proof of a similar statement for
smooth schemes given in
[
17
, Prop. II.7.7]
(see also

[
12
, § 13.13, p. 395]
) are based on two claims
both of which are met in the present case.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed and let
E\subset X^{o}
be an
effective Cartier divisor of degree
n\geq 1
.
Then
E
is canonically an element of
{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})(k)
and there is a canonical
isomorphism of
k
-schemes
({\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D})^{-1}({\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}(E))\cong U_{E}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}
has factorization
{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Div}^{n}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\xrightarrow{{{\rm AJ}}^{n}_{X|D}}{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
. As
k
is
algebraically closed,
{{\rm AJ}}^{n}_{X|D}
is given by
E\mapsto[E]-[nP]\in{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
. Using Lemma
6.1
, it is clear from this that

({\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D})^{-1}({\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}(E))=|{\mathcal{L}}|\cap{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})
.
It follows from (
6.4
) that, up to multiplication by elements of

k^{\times}
, the latter set consists of those sections of
{\mathcal{L}}
which
do not die in
k
under the map
H^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}})\to k

in the exact sequence (
6.5
). In other words,

|{\mathcal{L}}|\cap{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})=U_{E}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Assume that
k
is algebraically closed. Then for all integers
n\gg 0
, all
non-empty fibers of the morphism
{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})

are affine spaces over the corresponding residue fields of
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
,
and have equal and positive dimensions.

Proof.

For any effective Cartier divisor
E\subset X^{o}
, we let

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\mathfrak{m}}}(E)
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}=\psi^{*}_{\mathfrak{m}}({\mathcal{L}})
.
Since
|{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D)|
is a hyperplane of the projective space
|{\mathcal{L}}|
,
it follows from Lemma
6.2
that all fibers
of the morphism
{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
are affine
spaces over the corresponding residue fields of
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
. It remains to prove
the independence of these fiber dimensions if
n\gg 0
.

Suppose that
E
is such that
\deg(E)=n\geq\deg(D)+p_{a}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})+2g-1
, where

g=p_{a}(X)
denotes the
genus of
X
. Then
\deg({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D))=\deg({\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})-\deg(D)=\deg(E)-\deg(D)\geq 2g-1
. It follows from the Serre duality and the Riemann-Roch theorem for
X
that

(6.6)

H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D))=0=H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}).

Using the cohomology exact sequence associated to the sheaf exact sequence

(6.7)

0\to{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D)\to{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{D}\to 0,

we get an exact sequence

(6.8)

0\to H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}(-D))\to H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\to H^{0}(D,{\mathcal{O}}_{D})\to 0.

In particular, the map
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\to{H^{0}(D,{\mathcal{O}}_{D})}/k
is surjective.
On the other hand, by tensoring (
6.3
) with
{\mathcal{L}}
and passing to
cohomology, we get an exact sequence

(6.9)

0\to H^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\to{H^{0}(D,{\mathcal{O}}_{D})}/k\to H^{1}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\to 0.

We conclude that
H^{1}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}})=0
.

Now, we use the Riemann-Roch theorem for
X_{\mathfrak{m}}
(e.g., see

[
17
, Exc. IV.1.9]
) to get the identity

\text{\rm dim}_{k}(H^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}},{\mathcal{L}}))=n+1-p_{a}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
.
In other words,
\text{\rm dim}_{k}(U_{E})=\text{\rm dim}_{k}(|{\mathcal{L}}|)=n-p_{a}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\geq\deg(D)+2g-1\geq 1
. Since
\deg(D)+2g-1
depends only on
(X,D)
, we
conclude that all fibers of
{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}
are of a fixed positive dimension.
∎

We now let
k
be any field.

Proposition 6.4
.

For all integers
n\gg 0
, the morphism

{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
is smooth and surjective.

Proof.

Via the standard descent properties of the smooth and surjective morphisms with
respect to fpqc morphisms to the base scheme
(e.g., see
[
12
, Prop. 14.48, § 16.C]
), and the commutativity of

{{\mathfrak{m}}}
-contraction and
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
with the field extensions, we easily
reduce the proof to the case where we can assume
k
to be algebraically closed.

Since
k=\overline{k}
and
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})(k)
, it already follows from

[
7
, Defn. 7.1, Thm. 7.2]
that
{\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}
is surjective for all

n\gg 0
. Using the fpqc descent, the smoothness claim now follows from
Lemma
6.3
and
[
17
, Exc. III.10.9, Thm. III.10.2]
.
∎

For
n\gg 0
and
1\leq i<j\leq n
, we let

\Delta^{n}_{ij}(X^{o})\subset(X^{o})^{n}
be the closed subscheme (via a permutation)

\Delta_{ij}\times(X^{o})^{n-2}\subset(X^{o})^{n}
,
where
\Delta_{ij}\subset X^{o}\times X^{o}
is the diagonal of the product of
the
i
-th and
j
-th factors of
(X^{o})^{n}
. We let
U_{n}=(X^{o})^{n}\setminus({\underset{1\leq i<j\leq n}{\bigcup}}\Delta^{n}_{ij}(X^{o}))
.
Then
U_{n}
is invariant under the action of the symmetric group
S_{n}
and there is a
Cartesian square

(6.10)

\textstyle{U_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{(X^{o})^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}}
\textstyle{{U_{n}}/{S_{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o}).}

Furthermore,
\pi_{n}\colon U_{n}\to{U_{n}}/{S_{n}}
is an étale
S_{n}
-torsor
(e.g., see
[
8
, Prop. 0.9]
).
In particular, it is finite and étale. We let
U_{n}(X^{o})={U_{n}}/{S_{n}}
.

Corollary 6.5
.

For all
n\gg 0
, the morphism
\phi^{n}_{X|D}\colon U_{n}(X^{o})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
is smooth and has dense image.

Proof.

Since
\phi^{n}_{X|D}={\rm alb}^{n}_{X|D}\circ\pi_{n}
(see (
6.2
)) and

U_{n}(X^{o})\xrightarrow{\pi_{n}}{\operatorname{\rm Sym}}^{n}(X^{o})
is étale with dense image,
the corollary follows from Proposition
6.4
.
∎

We can now prove the following generic smoothness of the albanese map for a
modulus pair. We let
k
be any field and
(X,D)
a 1-dimensional modulus pair over
k

such that
X
is connected and smooth over
k
and

X^{o}(k)\neq\emptyset
. We fix a point
P\in X^{o}(k)

so that
{\rm alb}_{X|D}
is defined with respect to the base point
P
.

Theorem 6.6
.

For all
n\gg 0
, the albanese map

\phi^{n}_{X|D}\colon U_{n}(X^{o})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
is smooth and has dense image.

Proof.

Our assumption implies that
X
is geometrically integral over
k
.
If
\deg(D)=1
, then the canonical map
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)
is an
isomorphism of algebraic groups over
k
(see Lemma
5.2
)
and one knows classically that
the albanese map
U_{n}(X^{o})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)
is smooth and has dense image.
If
\deg(D)\geq 2
, the theorem follows from Theorem
5.6
and
Corollary
6.5
.
∎

6.2.
The map from relative to ordinary Picard scheme

In order to compare the relative and ordinary Picard schemes, we need to identify

{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
with the generalized Jacobian variety of
(X,D)
à la Rosenlicht-Serre

[
50
]
under some additional assumptions.
We fix a field
k
and a 1-dimensional modulus pair
(X,D)
over
k
such that
X
is
connected and smooth over
k
. We write

D=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}n_{i}[x_{i}]
.
If
\deg(D)\geq 2
, we let
\psi_{\mathfrak{m}}\colon X\to X_{\mathfrak{m}}
be the
{\mathfrak{m}}
-contraction
of
X
. The following is the main result of Rosenlicht-Serre
for which we refer to
[
50
, Chap. V, § 4.22, 4.23]
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Assume that
{\rm Supp}(D)\subset X(k)
and
P\in X^{o}(k)
.
Then there exists a quasi-projective algebraic group
J_{X|D}
over
k
satisfying
the following properties.

(1)

There is a canonical isomorphism of group schemes
J_{X_{k^{\prime}}|D_{k^{\prime}}}\cong(J_{X|D})_{k^{\prime}}
over
k^{\prime}
for every field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
.

(2)

The assignment
Q\mapsto[Q]-\deg(Q)[P]
on
X^{o}_{(0)}

defines a morphism of
k
-schemes
(called the albanese morphism)
{\lambda}_{X|D}\colon X^{o}\to J_{X|D}
.

(3)

The albanese morphism induces a homomorphism of abelian groups

{{\rm AJ}}_{X|D}\colon{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)_{0}\to J_{X|D}({k}).

(4)

Given any commutative group scheme
G
over
k
and a
k
-morphism

f\colon X^{o}\to G
which induces a homomorphism

f_{*}\colon{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)_{0}\to G(k)
, there is a unique homomorphism of
group schemes
\theta\colon J_{X|D}\to G
over
k

such that
f(x)=f(P)+(\theta\circ\lambda_{X|D})(x)
for every
x\in X^{o}
.

Assume that
\deg(D)\geq 2
. Using the albanese map
{\rm alb}_{X|D}\colon X^{o}\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
and (
6.1
), it follows from the property (4) of

J_{X|D}
that there exists a unique homomorphism (recall here that

{\rm alb}_{X|D}(P)=0
) of group schemes
\theta_{X|D}\colon J_{X|D}\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})\cong{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
over
k
such that
{\rm alb}_{X|D}=\theta_{X|D}\circ\lambda_{X|D}
.

Corollary 6.8
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.7
, the albanese map

\lambda_{X|D}\colon X^{o}\to J_{X|D}
induces an isomorphism of
algebraic groups

\theta_{X|D}\colon J_{X|D}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
.

Proof.

If
\deg(D)=1
, it easily follows from
[
50
, Chap. V, § 3.13]

that the canonical map
J_{X|D}\to J_{X}
is an isomorphism. We have seen
above that this holds also for
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
.
If
\deg(D)\geq 2
, we can replace
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
with
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X_{\mathfrak{m}})
.
In the latter case, we can argue as in the proof of Proposition
6.4
to
reduce the proof to the case where we can assume that
k
is algebraically closed.
But this case is classically known.
∎

We can now compare the relative and ordinary Picard schemes of a modulus pair.
For any
n\geq 1
, let
{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
be the étale sheaf on
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}
given
by
{\mathbb{W}}_{n}(Z)={\mathbb{W}}_{n}({\mathcal{O}}(Z))
, where
{\mathbb{W}}_{n}(R)
is the ring of big Witt-vectors
over the ring
R
of length
n
(e.g., see
[
44
, App. A]
).
One knows that
{\mathbb{W}}_{n}
is representable by a unipotent linear algebraic group over
k

(e.g., see
[
50
, Chap. V, § 3.13, Lem. 20]
).

Proposition 6.9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.7
, there is an exact sequence of
algebraic groups

(6.11)

0\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)_{\rm aff}\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)\to 0

over
k
, where
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)_{\rm aff}
is a linear algebraic group
canonically isomorphic to
({\mathbb{G}}_{m})^{r-1}\times(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}{\mathbb{W}}_{n_{i}-1})
.

Proof.

Using Corollary
6.8
, we can work with
J_{X|D}
, in which case
the proposition is well known (e.g., see
[
50
, Chap. V, § 3, no. 13]
).
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically integral curve over
k
and let

D\leq D^{\prime}
be two effective Cartier divisors on
X
.
Then the canonical map
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D^{\prime})\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
is smooth, affine and
surjective. If
Y
is a singular curve over
k
with normalization
X
and

Z\subset Y
is a conductor subscheme for the normalization such that

D=Z\times_{Y}X
, then the map
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(Y)

in (
5.7
) is smooth, affine and surjective.

Proof.

To prove either of the statements, we can pass to the algebraic closure of
k
.
In this case, the first statement follows directly by comparing
(
6.11
) for
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D^{\prime})
and
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
.
The second statement follows directly from (
5.9
).
∎

6.3.
The case of local fields

We shall assume in this subsection that
k
is a local field. Recall that
k
is a
topological field with respect to its valuation (also called adic) topology such that
the ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
is an open subring. One also knows
that for any locally of finite type
k
-scheme
Y
, the set
Y(k)
has a unique
structure of a totally disconnected locally compact Hausdorff topological space
(see
[
5
, Prop. 5.4]
). This topology is characterized by the property that if

Y\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{k}
as a locally closed subscheme, then it coincides with the subspace
topology on
Y(k)
induced by the product of the adic topology of
k
on

{\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{k}(k)\cong k^{n}
.
This assignment of adic topology defines a functor
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Sch}}}_{k}\to{\mathbf{Top}}
, where the latter
is the category of topological spaces with continuous maps.
We shall call this the adic topology of
Y(k)
and say that
Y(k)
is an adic space.
For any property of the adic topology of
Y(k)
that we shall use in this paper, the
reader is referred to
[
5
]
and
[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
.

Every finite-dimensional
k
-vector space
M

is equipped with the product topology of the adic topology of
k
, called the adic
topology of
M
. If
A
is a finite-dimensional
k
-algebra, the adic topology of

A^{\times}
is the subspace topology induced from
A
. These topologies make

A
and
A^{\times}
into topological abelian groups. If
{k^{\prime}}/k
is a finite field
extension and
A
is a finite-dimensional
k^{\prime}
-vector space, then
the adic topology of
A
(and
A^{\times}
if
A
is a
k^{\prime}
-algebra) coincides
with its adic topology when considered as a
k
-vector space
(e.g., see
[
20
, § 7.2]
).
In this paper, all finite-dimensional algebras over a local field
and their unit groups will
be assumed to be endowed with the adic topology unless mentioned otherwise.

We now let
(X,D)
be as in §
6.2
. We assume that
X^{o}(k)\neq\emptyset

and fix a point
P\in X^{o}(k)
so that
{\rm alb}_{X|D}
is defined with respect to the base
point
P
. Let
Y
be a singular curve
over
k
with normalization
X
and a conductor subscheme
D\subset X
.
Since
{\rm Pic}(X|D)={\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)(k)
and
{\rm Pic}(Y)={\mathbf{Pic}}(Y)(k)
,
we see that
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
and
{\rm Pic}(Y)
are
locally compact Hausdorff topological abelian groups. Furthermore, it follows from

[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
that
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)
and
{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)
are open subgroups
of
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
and
{\rm Pic}(Y)
, respectively. In the following result, we shall use
the adic topology on all sets.

Corollary 6.11
.

We have the following.

(1)

For all
n\gg 0
, the map
\phi^{n}_{X|D}\colon U_{n}(X^{o})(k)\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)
is a continuous open map whose image is dense.

(2)

{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
is a profinite abelian group.

(3)

The map
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
is a topological quotient.

(4)

If
D^{\prime}\geq D
, the map
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D^{\prime})\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)
is a topological quotient.

(5)

The map
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)
is a topological quotient.

Proof.

The statement (1) follows directly from Theorem
6.6
and

[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
. To prove (2), we note that
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)
is an
abelian variety over
k
. This implies that
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
is compact as an adic
space (see op. cit.). Since it is also totally disconnected,
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
must be
profinite. Since the maps
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D^{\prime})\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)
and

{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(Y)
are surjective, the remaining statements
of the corollary follow directly from Corollary
6.10
and

[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
.
∎

7.
Pontryagin dual of the Chow group with modulus

The goal of this section is to strengthen Corollary
6.11
.
More precisely, we shall show that all maps in the exact sequence
(
5.1
) are continuous with respect to the adic topology and the
resulting dual complex is partially exact. This will be a key step in the proofs of
the main results. We begin by recalling the Kato topology and its relation with the
adic topology.

7.1.
Kato topology

Let
R
be an equicharacteristic excellent Henselian discrete valuation ring with
maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}
whose residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}
is a local field of exponential
characteristic
p\geq 1
. Let
L
denote the quotient field of
R
. In this case, one can
find a two dimensional excellent normal local integral domain
A
whose residue field is
finite such that
R\cong(A_{\mathfrak{p}})^{h}
for some height one prime ideal
{\mathfrak{p}}\subset A
.
For any ideal
I\subset A
not contained in
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
n\geq 1
, we let

{\rm fil}^{I}_{n}{K}^{M}_{1}(R)
be the subgroup of
R^{\times}
generated by
(1+{\mathfrak{m}}^{n})

and
(1+I)\subset A^{\times}\subset{K}^{M}_{1}(R)
. We let
{\rm fil}^{I}_{0}{K}^{M}_{1}(R)=R^{\times}
.

When
p>1
, Kato defined a subgroup topology on
R^{\times}

(see
[
20
, § 7]
and
[
46
, § 2.3]
)
for which the fundamental system of open neighborhoods of the identity is given
by subgroups of the form
{\rm fil}^{I}_{n}{K}^{M}_{1}(R)
, where
I
and
n

vary as above. It follows from
[
20
, § 7, Lem. 1]

(see also
[
46
, § 2.3]
) that this topology of

R^{\times}
does not depend on the choice of
A
. We shall call this the
Kato topology on
R^{\times}
. The Kato topology on
L^{\times}
is the unique topology
which is compatible with its group structure and for which
R^{\times}
with its
Kato topology is an open subgroup.

It is easy to check that the Kato topology of
R^{\times}
coincides
with the subspace topology induced from the Kato topology of
\widehat{R}^{\times}
via the
inclusion
R^{\times}\subset\widehat{R}^{\times}
.
In particular, the same also holds for the inclusion
L^{\times}\subset\widehat{L}^{\times}
.
When
p=1
, we shall assume the Kato topology on
R^{\times}
and
L^{\times}
to be
discrete. By choosing a uniformizer
\pi\in{\mathfrak{m}}
, we get a canonical
isomorphism of
{\mathfrak{f}}
-algebras
\phi\colon{\mathfrak{f}}[[T]]\xrightarrow{\cong}\widehat{R}
which sends
T
to
\pi
.
In §
6.3
, we recalled the adic topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{f}}
.
We shall use the following description of the Kato topology in terms of the adic
topology.

Lemma 7.1
.

When
p>1
, the map
\phi\colon({\mathfrak{f}}[[T]])^{\times}\to(\widehat{R})^{\times}
is an
isomorphism of topological abelian groups if we endow
{\mathfrak{f}}[[T]]\cong{\mathfrak{f}}\times{\mathfrak{f}}^{{\mathbb{N}}}
with the product topology of the adic topology of
{\mathfrak{f}}
and

({\mathfrak{f}}[[T]])^{\times}
with the subspace topology. In particular, the quotient
topology of the Kato topology on
({\widehat{R}}/{{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}})^{\times}
coincides with the
adic topology of
({{\mathfrak{f}}[T]}/{(T^{n})})^{\times}
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of
[
20
, § 7, Rem. 1, Lem. 3]
.
∎

Recall (e.g., see
[
44
, App. A]
) that for any commutative ring
A

and the set
\{1,\ldots,m\}
(where
m=\infty
is allowed),
the ring of big Witt-vectors of length
m
is the commutative ring
{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A)
which is

A^{m}
as a set and whose ring structure is uniquely determined by the condition that the
ghost map
{\rm gh_{m}}\colon{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A)\to A^{m}
is a ring homomorphism
with respect to the product ring structure on
A^{m}
. The ghost map is in fact a natural
transformation of functors between commutative rings. We write
{\mathbb{W}}_{\infty}(A)
as
{\mathbb{W}}(A)
.

Let
\Gamma(A):=(1+TA[[T]])^{\times}\subset(A[[T]])^{\times}
be the subgroup of the
group of units of the power series ring
A[[T]]
. Recall that every element of

\Gamma(A)
has a unique presentation of the form
f(T)={\underset{n\geq 1}{\prod}}(1-a_{n}T^{n})
with
a_{n}\in A
.
One knows (e.g., see
[
44
, App. A]
)
that as an abelian group with respect to its addition operation,
{\mathbb{W}}(A)
and

{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A)
have a simple description. That is, the maps

(7.1)

\nu_{A}\colon{\mathbb{W}}(A)\to\Gamma(A);\ \nu_{A}((a_{n}))={\underset{n\geq 1}{\prod}}(1-a_{n}T^{n}),\ \ and

(7.2)

\mu_{A}\colon\Gamma(A)\to{\mathbb{W}}(A),\ \ \mu_{A}({\underset{n\geq 1}{\prod}}(1-a_{n}T^{n}))=(a_{n}).

are group homomorphisms which are inverses to each other. These maps induce isomorphisms

(7.3)

\nu_{A}\colon{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}\Gamma_{m}(A):=\frac{(1+TA[[T]])^{\times}}{(1+T^{m+1}A[[T]])^{\times}};\ \ \mu_{A}\colon\Gamma_{m}(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A).

It follows from (
7.2
) that there are polynomials

p_{i}\in A[T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}]
such that

\mu_{A}
in (
7.3
) is
of the form
\mu_{A}(1+a_{1}T+\cdots+a_{m}T^{m})=(p_{1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}),\ldots,p_{m}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}))
. We shall let
{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A)=0
for
m\leq 0
.

Recall from §
6.2
that for any field
F
,
there is a unipotent linear algebraic group
{{\mathbb{W}}}_{m,F}
over
F

such that
{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A)={{\mathbb{W}}}_{m,F}(A)
for any
F
-algebra
A
. The map

\psi_{m,F}\colon{{\mathbb{W}}}_{m,F}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{m}_{F}
, given by
\psi_{m,F}((a_{i}))=(a_{i})

for
(a_{i})\in{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(A)
(
A
any
F
-algebra) is an isomorphism between
F
-schemes
(e.g., see
[
50
, Chap. V, § 3.13]
).
Note however that this is not an isomorphism of group schemes over
F
.
One can in fact write
{{\mathbb{W}}}_{m,F}={\rm Spec\,}(A_{m,F})
, where

(7.4)

A_{m,F}=\frac{F[X_{ij}|1\leq i,j\leq m+1]}{(X_{ij}\ \mbox{for}\ i>j,\ X_{ij}-1\ \mbox{for}\ i=j,\ X_{ij}-X_{i+1j+1}\ \mbox{for}\ i<j)}.

The isomorphism
\psi_{m,F}
is easily deduced from this description.

Suppose now that
F
is our local field
k
. For any finite field
extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
, the group scheme
{\mathbb{W}}_{m,k^{\prime}}
then induces the adic topology on the
abelian group
{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(k^{\prime})={\mathbb{W}}_{m,k^{\prime}}(k^{\prime})
.
Furthermore, the isomorphism
{\mathbb{W}}_{m,k^{\prime}}\cong{\mathbb{A}}^{m}_{k^{\prime}}
as
k^{\prime}
-schemes implies
that
{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(k^{\prime})\cong k^{\prime m}
as adic spaces, where the latter has the product of the
adic topology of
k^{\prime}
. Note here that the adic topology
of
{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(k^{\prime})
does not depend on whether it has been induced by the
k
-scheme
structure or by the
k^{\prime}
-scheme structure of
{\mathbb{W}}_{m,k^{\prime}}
(e.g., see

[
20
, § 7.2]
).
We shall henceforth consider
{\mathbb{W}}_{m,k^{\prime}}
as a unipotent algebraic group over
k^{\prime}

and
{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(k^{\prime})
as a topological abelian group with its adic topology.
Using the descriptions of Kato and adic topologies, we get the following.

Lemma 7.2
.

Assume
p>1
. Let
X
be a regular curve over
k
and
\pi_{x}
a uniformizer of the
local ring
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
at a closed point
x\in X
, then the map

\mu_{k(x)}\circ\phi^{-1}\colon\left(\frac{\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}}{(\pi^{m+1}_{x})}\right)^{\times}\to k(x)^{\times}\times{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(k(x))

is a continuous bijection between topological abelian groups for every
m\geq 0
,
where the left hand side is endowed with the quotient of the Kato topology.

Proof.

We can assume
m\geq 1
, else the statement is immediate from Lemma
7.1
.
Using Lemma
7.1
and (
7.2
),
we only need to show that

\mu_{k(x)}\colon\Gamma_{m}(k(x))\to{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(k(x))
is continuous
if
\Gamma_{m}(k(x))
is endowed with the subspace topology from

\left({k(x)[T]}/{(T^{m+1})}\right)^{\times}
. For this, we look at the diagram

(7.5)

\textstyle{\Gamma_{m}(k(x))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{k(x)}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{k(x)}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{W}}_{m}(k(x))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{k(x)}}
\textstyle{k(x)^{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{k(x)}}
\textstyle{k(x)^{m},}

where
\gamma_{k(x)}((a_{i}))=(p_{1}((a_{i})),\ldots,p_{m}((a_{i})))

(cf. (
7.3
)).
The arrow
\theta_{k(x)}
is the canonical bijection induced by the homeomorphism of
topological spaces

\alpha_{k(x)}\colon(k(x)[[T]])^{\times}\xrightarrow{\cong}k(x)^{\times}\times k(x)^{{\mathbb{N}}}
as in Lemma
7.1
. It is clear from the definition of various
maps that this diagram is commutative. Since
\psi_{k(x)}
is a homeomorphism,
it is enough to show that
\psi_{k(x)}\circ\mu_{k(x)}
is continuous.
Equivalently, we need to show that
\gamma_{k(x)}\circ\theta_{k(x)}
is
continuous.

Since
\alpha_{k(x)}
is a homeomorphism of topological spaces
(cf. Lemma
7.1
) and
\theta_{k(x)}
is the induced map
on the quotients with the quotient topologies (note that the projection

k(x)^{{\mathbb{N}}}\to k(x)^{m}
is a quotient map), it is a
homeomorphism. Since each component of
\gamma_{k(x)}
is a polynomial map,
it is clearly continuous. In particular,
\gamma_{k(x)}
is continuous.
It follows that
\gamma_{k(x)}\circ\theta_{k(x)}
is continuous.
∎

7.2.
Pontryagin duals of Chow groups

Let
k
be a local field of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
.
Let
(X,D)
be a modulus pair over
k
, where
X
is a geometrically
connected smooth projective curve over
k
. We let
D^{\dagger}=D_{\rm red}
.
We shall assume that
\emptyset\neq D^{\dagger}\subset X(k)
. We let

X^{o}=X\setminus D
.
We let
j\colon X^{o}\hookrightarrow X
and
t\colon D\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusions. We write

D=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}n_{i}[x_{i}]\in{\rm Div}(X)
.
If
U\subset X
is any Zariski dense open, then
U(k)\subset X(k)
is an adically
dense open subset (e.g., see
[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
). Since
X(k)\neq\emptyset
,
it easily follows that
U(k)
must be infinite. In particular,
X^{o}(k)
is infinite.
We fix a point
P\in X^{o}(k)
such that
{\rm alb}_{X|D}(P)=0
.
We have noted in §
6.3
that
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)
and
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)
are adic
topological abelian groups and the degree zero parts are their open subgroups.

Assume that
p>1
. Using the identification
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D)\cong{\underset{x\in D^{\dagger}}{\prod}}\left(\frac{\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}}{(\pi^{n_{x}}_{x})}\right)^{\times}

as an adic space, we conclude from Proposition
6.9
and
Lemma
7.2
that in (
5.1
),
the map
\partial_{X|D}
is continuous, for, it is the product of maps

\mu_{k(x)}\circ\phi^{-1}_{x}
over
x\in D^{\dagger}
followed by the
topological quotient
(k^{\times})^{r}\times(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}{\mathbb{W}}_{n_{i}-1}(k))\twoheadrightarrow(k^{\times})^{r-1}\times(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}{\mathbb{W}}_{n_{i}-1}(k))
. Since the map
\vartheta_{X|D}

and the inclusion
k^{\times}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D)
are clearly continuous,
(
5.1
) gives rise to a sequence of homomorphisms

(7.6)

0\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}\xrightarrow{(\vartheta_{X|D})^{\star}}{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}\xrightarrow{(\partial_{X|D})^{\star}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D))^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}

Lemma 7.3
.

(
7.6
) is a chain complex of abelian groups which is exact
at
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}
and
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
.

Proof.

The lemma is obvious if
\deg(D)=1
by Lemma
5.2
. We shall therefore
assume that
\deg(D)\geq 2
.
That (
7.6
) is a complex is a direct consequence of
(
5.1
). The injectivity of

(\vartheta_{X|D})^{\star}
follows again from (
5.1
).
To show the exactness at
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
, we can clearly replace

{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)
and
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)
by their degree zero subgroups.
We let
G=(k^{\times})^{r-1}\times(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}{\mathbb{W}}_{n_{i}-1}(k))
. We need to show that the sequence

(7.7)

0\to({\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0})^{\star}\xrightarrow{(\vartheta_{X|D})^{\star}}({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)_{0})^{\star}\to G^{\star}\to 0

is exact at the middle term.

To show the above exactness, we note that the sequence

(7.8)

0\to({\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{(\vartheta_{X|D})^{\vee}}({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)_{0})^{\vee}\to G^{\vee}\to 0

is clearly exact. Hence, given
a continuous character
\chi\in({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)_{0})^{\star}
whose restriction to

G
is zero, we get a unique
character
\chi^{\prime}\in({\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0})^{\vee}
such that
\chi=\chi^{\prime}\circ\vartheta_{X|D}
.
It remains to show that
\chi^{\prime}
is continuous with respect to the adic topology of

{\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0}
. But this is an easy consequence of Lemma
5.1
and
Corollary
6.11
.
∎

We now assume
p=1
and prove the analogous result.
We begin with the following.

Lemma 7.4
.

If
p=1
, we have
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D^{\dagger})^{\star}\cong({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star})_{\rm tor}\cong({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\vee})_{\rm tor}
. We also have

(7.9)

(({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D))^{\star})_{\rm tor}\cong({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D))^{\vee})_{\rm tor}\cong({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D^{\dagger}))^{\star}\cong({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D^{\dagger}))^{\vee})_{\rm tor},

where
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D)
and
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D^{\dagger})
are endowed with the adic
topology.

Proof.

The isomorphisms in (
7.9
) are
immediate consequences of
[
39
, Prop. II.5.7]
because

{\rm Ker}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D^{\dagger}))
is a divisible group.
To prove the first statement, we first assume
D=\emptyset
.
Since
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0}={\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)(k)
is a profinite abelian group by
Corollary
6.11
, it follows that
({\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0})^{\star}
is a torsion group.
Furthermore, the assumption
p=1
implies using the Kummer sequence that
{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/n

is discrete (and finite) for the quotient topology for every integer
n\geq 1
.
Since
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)\xrightarrow{n}{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)

is a smooth isogeny, it follows from
[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
that

{\rm CH}_{0}(X)\xrightarrow{n}{\rm CH}_{0}(X)
is an open map of adic spaces.
In particular,
{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}\to{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/n
is continuous.

Now, if
\chi\in{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}
, then
\chi({\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0})
is a finite cyclic group
of the type
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n
. This yields a commutative diagram of exact sequences

(7.10)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\chi}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since the image of
\widetilde{\chi}
must be finite, it follows that
\chi
has a finite
order. Conversely, if
\chi\in({\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\vee})_{\rm tor}
, then it factors through

{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/n
for some
n\geq 1
and it follows from
the previous paragraph that
\chi
is continuous on
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)
.

Suppose now that
D
is nonempty but reduced and let
\chi\in{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
.
We have seen above that the restriction of
\chi
to

(k^{\times})^{r-1}
has finite order. We thus get an integer
n\geq 1
and
a commutative diagram of exact sequences (cf. Proposition
6.9
)

(7.11)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(k^{\times})^{r-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\chi}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm CH}_{0}(X)
is a topological quotient by
Corollary
6.11
, we get that
\widetilde{\chi}
is continuous, and hence,
its image is finite. We conclude that the image of the middle vertical arrow is finite.

Conversely, if
\chi\in({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\vee})_{\rm tor}
, then it factors through

{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)}/n\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\chi}}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
for some
n\geq 1
.
We have shown above that

{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/n
is finite. It follows from the top row of (
7.11
) and

[
39
, Prop. II.5.7]
that
{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)}/n
is finite. On the other hand,
as
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)\xrightarrow{n}{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
is a smooth isogeny, it follows that

{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)}/n
is a finite and discrete quotient of
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)
. We conclude that

\chi
is continuous.

If
D
is not necessarily reduced, we look at the commutative diagram

(7.12)

\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D^{\dagger})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D^{\dagger})^{\vee})_{\rm tor}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star})_{\rm tor}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\vee})_{\rm tor},}

in which all arrows are the canonical inclusions. Since any element of

({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\vee})_{\rm tor}
must annihilate the kernel of

{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D_{\rm red})
, it follows
that the right vertical arrow in (
7.12
) is a bijection.
We have shown in the previous paragraph that the top horizontal arrow is a
bijection. A diagram chase shows that all arrows are bijections.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

If
p=1
and
D
is reduced, then (
5.1
) induces a chain complex
of abelian groups

(7.13)

0\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}\xrightarrow{(\vartheta_{X|D})^{\star}}{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}\xrightarrow{(\partial_{X|D})^{\star}}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D))^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}

which is exact at
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}
and
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
.

Proof.

At any rate, we do have an exact sequence

0\to({\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\vee})_{\rm tor}\xrightarrow{(\vartheta_{X|D})^{\vee}}({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\vee})_{\rm tor}\xrightarrow{(\partial_{X|D})^{\vee}}(({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(D))^{\vee})_{\rm tor}\to((k^{\times})^{\vee})_{\rm tor}.

The desired claim now follows by Lemma
7.4
.
∎

8.
Brauer group with modulus

The goal of this section is to define the Brauer group of a modulus pair and
prove some functorial properties. We begin by recalling Kato’s ramification
filtration which will play a fundamental role in our exposition.

8.1.
Étale motivic cohomology

Let
k
be a field of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
and let
X
be a Noetherian

k
-scheme.
If
n\in k^{\times}
is an integer and
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we let
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(r)
be the
étale sheaf on
X
defined as the usual Tate twist of the constant sheaf
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n

(e.g., see
[
35
, p. 163]
). If
p>1
and
n=p^{s}m
with
s\geq 0

and
p\nmid m
, we let
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(r)
be the object

{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(r)\oplus W_{s}\Omega^{r}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}}[-r]
as an object of
{\mathcal{D}}_{\text{\'{e}t}}(X)
.
We have the cup product pairing of the (hyper)cohomology of the form

H^{i}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(j))\times H^{i^{\prime}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(j^{\prime}))\to H^{i+i^{\prime}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(j+j^{\prime}))
.
For
q\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we let
H^{q}_{n}(X)
denote the étale cohomology group

H^{q}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(q-1))
. We let
H^{q}(X)={\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{q}_{n}(X)

with respect to the canonical transition maps
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(r)\xrightarrow{m}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{mn}(r)

(see (
2.6
) for their definition in positive characteristic).
If
X={\rm Spec\,}(A)
is affine, we write
H^{q}_{n}(X)
(resp.
H^{q}(X)
) as
H^{q}_{n}(A)

(resp.
H^{q}(A)
).

For
m,n\in k^{\times}
and

s\geq 1
, we have commutative diagrams of exact sequences

(8.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{\rm can}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{s}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}}/{p^{s}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{mn}}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{mn}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{s+1}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}}/{p^{s+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

(8.2)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{mn}}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{mn}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{s+1}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}}/{p^{s+1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{\rm can}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{s}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}}/{p^{s}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

of étale sheaves, where the diagrams on the right make sense when
p>1
.

The commutative diagrams in (
8.1
)
give rise to an exact sequence of ind-abelian groups

(8.3)

0\to\{{{\operatorname{Br}}(X)}/n\}\to\{H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(1))\}\to\{_{m}H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\}\to 0,

which are indexed by
{\mathbb{N}}
and whose transition maps
are induced by
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m\xrightarrow{n}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{mn}
.
Taking the limits and noting that
H^{i}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
is a torsion group for

i\geq 2
(e.g., see
[
4
, Lem. 3.5.3]
) when
X
is regular, we get the following.

Lemma 8.1
.

If
X
is regular, then the canonical map

H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\to H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is an easy application of the above discussion once we use an elementary fact that

A\otimes B=0
if
A
is a torsion abelian group and
B
is a divisible abelian
group.
∎

We also get the following folklore result which we shall use throughout this paper
without giving reference.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
A
be a equicharacteristic regular local ring. Then one has a canonical
isomorphism
H^{2}(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{Br}}(A)
.

For a local ring
A
over
k
, we have the
Norm residue map (e.g., see
[
13
, § 5.1]
)

(8.4)

{\rm NR}_{A}\colon{K^{M}_{i}(A)}/{n}\to H^{i}(A,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(i)).

Composing this with the cup product, we see that there is a canonical
bilinear pairing

(8.5)

\langle,\rangle\colon H^{i}(A,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(j))\times{K^{M}_{i^{\prime}}(A)}/{n}\to H^{i+i^{\prime}}(A,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(j+i^{\prime})).

If
A
is any equicharacteristic local integral domain with maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}_{A}

and quotient field
E
, we let
{\rm fil}_{0}{K}^{M}_{1}(E)=A^{\times}
and
{\rm fil}_{n}{K}^{M}_{1}(E)=(1+{\mathfrak{m}}^{n}_{A})
if
n\geq 1
.
For
i\geq 2
, we let
{\rm fil}_{n}{K}^{M}_{i}(E)
be the image of the
cup product map
{\rm fil}_{n}{K}^{M}_{1}(E)\otimes{K}^{M}_{i-1}(E)\to{K}^{M}_{i}(E)
.
We let
{\rm fil}_{n}{K}^{M}_{i}(E)={K}^{M}_{i}(E)
for
n<0
.
It is clear that
{\rm fil}_{\bullet}{K}^{M}_{i}(E)
is a decreasing filtration of

{K}^{M}_{i}(E)
. We shall call this ‘the logarithmic filtration’ of
{K}^{M}_{i}(E)
.

8.2.
Kato’s ramification filtration

In order to define the Brauer group with modulus, we need to recall Kato’s ramification
filtration. We let
L
be a Henselian discrete valuation field with the ring of
integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
, the maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}_{L}=(\pi_{L})
and the residue field
{\mathfrak{l}}

such that
{\rm char}(L)={\rm char}({\mathfrak{l}})=p\geq 0
. Let
\widehat{L}
denote the completion of
L
.
Recall the following from
[
23
, Cor. 2.5, Prop. 6.3]
, where we have shifted
Kato’s filtration one place to the right.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
q\geq 1
be an integer.

(1)

If
p=0
, we let
{\rm fil}_{0}H^{q}(L)=H^{q}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
and
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{q}(L)=H^{q}(L)
if
n\geq 1
.

(2)

If
p>0
and
n\geq 0
, we let
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{q}(L)
be the
subgroup of elements
\chi\in H^{q}(L)
such that

\langle\chi,1+\pi^{n}_{L}{\mathcal{O}}_{L^{\prime}}\rangle=0

for all Henselian discrete valuation
fields
L^{\prime}
such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{L^{\prime}}

and
{\mathfrak{m}}_{L^{\prime}}={\mathfrak{m}}_{L}{\mathcal{O}}_{L^{\prime}}
, where
1+\pi^{0}_{L}{\mathcal{O}}_{L^{\prime}}:={\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{L^{\prime}}
.

(3)

We let
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{q}(L)=0
for
n<0
.

It follows from
[
23
, Lem. 2.2]
that

(8.6)

H^{q}(L)={\underset{n\geq 0}{\bigcup}}{\rm fil}_{n}H^{q}(L).

We shall call
{\rm fil}_{\bullet}H^{q}(L):=\{{\rm fil}_{n}H^{q}(L)\}_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
,
the ramification filtration of
H^{q}(L)
.
Recall that for any
\chi\in H^{q}(L)
, the Swan conductor
{\rm sw}(\chi)
is the smallest
integer
n
such that
\chi\in{\rm fil}_{n}H^{q}(L)
.
The following result shows that one can characterize
{\rm fil}_{\bullet}H^{q}(L)
purely in terms
of characters of the Milnor
K
-groups of
L
in cases of our interest.

Lemma 8.4
.

Assume that
p>0
and
{\mathfrak{l}}
is a local field. Let
1\leq q\leq 2
and

n\geq 0
be integers. Then the following hold.

(1)

There are canonical isomorphisms

H^{3}(L)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{3}(\widehat{L})\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2}({\mathfrak{l}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

(2)

The canonical map
H^{q}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})\to H^{q}(L)
induced by the inclusion

{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\hookrightarrow L
fits into a split short exact sequence

0\to H^{q}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})\to{\rm fil}_{1}H^{q}(L)\to H^{q-1}({\mathfrak{l}})\to 0,

which is canonical for a given choice of
\pi_{L}
.

(3)

{\rm fil}_{0}H^{q}(L)=H^{q}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
.

(4)

For
n\geq 1
, an element

\chi\in H^{q}(L)
lies in
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{q}(L)
if and only if

\langle\chi,{\rm fil}_{n}{K}^{M}_{3-q}(L)\rangle=0
under
the pairing

H^{q}(L)\times{K}^{M}_{3-q}(L)\xrightarrow{\langle,\rangle}H^{3}(L)\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

Part (1) of the lemma is a special case of the general
isomorphism (
\forall\ q
)

(8.7)

H^{q}(L)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{q}(\widehat{L})

as shown in
[
24
, Lem. 21]
, and the well known case of
complete discrete valuation fields (see
[
21
, § 3.2, Prop. 1]
).
For
q=1
, the other parts of the lemma follow from

[
13
, Thm. 6.3]
. For
q=2
, part (2)
follows directly from
[
23
, Prop. 6.1]
.

To prove (3),
suppose first that
\chi\in{\rm fil}_{0}H^{2}(L)
. We consider the commutative diagram
of exact sequences

(8.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{1}H^{2}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{H^{1}({\mathfrak{l}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({\mathbb{Z}})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(L^{\times})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{L})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the vertical arrows are induced by (
8.5
).
Our hypothesis says that
\beta\circ\alpha(\chi)=0
. Since the right vertical
arrow is injective by the class field theory of local fields, it follows that

\chi\in H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
. Conversely, suppose that
\chi\in H^{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})
.
Then a diagram chase of (
8.8
) tells us that

\chi\in{\rm fil}_{1}H^{2}(L)
such that
\beta\circ\alpha(\chi)=0
.
But this is equivalent to saying that
\chi\in{\rm fil}_{0}H^{2}(L)
.
This proves (3).

We now assume
n\geq 1
and
q=2
.
In view of
[
23
, Prop. 6.3]
, we only need to show
that if
\chi\in H^{2}(L)
is a character such that
\langle\chi,{\rm fil}_{n}L^{\times}\rangle=0
,
then it lies in
{\rm fil}_{n}H^{2}(L)
. At any rate, we know that
\chi\in{\rm fil}_{m}H^{2}(L)
for some
m\gg 0
. We can assume that
m>n
(else we are done).
Our hypothesis implies that as a character of
L^{\times}
,
\chi
factors through

{L^{\times}}/{{\rm fil}_{n}L^{\times}}
.

We now note that the ring of integers

{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{L}}
of
\widehat{L}
is the
{\mathfrak{m}}_{L}
-adic completion
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
.
Since
{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{L}}/{{\rm fil}_{n}L^{\times}}\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{\widehat{L}}}/{{\rm fil}_{n}{\widehat{L}^{\times}}}
, it follows from the commutative diagram

(8.9)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{L}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{\widehat{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widehat{L}}^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{\widehat{L}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

of exact sequences that
{L^{\times}}/{{\rm fil}_{n}L^{\times}}\xrightarrow{\cong}{{\widehat{L}^{\times}}}/{{\rm fil}_{n}{\widehat{L}^{\times}}}
. Hence,
\chi
factors through

{{\widehat{L}^{\times}}}/{{\rm fil}_{n}{\widehat{L}^{\times}}}
as a character of
\widehat{L}^{\times}
.
We conclude from
[
23
, Prop. 6.3, Rem. 6.6]
that

\chi\in{\rm fil}_{n}H^{2}(\widehat{L})
.

By an iteration of the commutative diagram

(8.10)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{m-1}H^{2}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{m}H^{2}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\frac{{\rm fil}_{m}H^{2}(L)}{{\rm fil}_{m-1}H^{2}(L)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{m-1}H^{2}(\widehat{L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm fil}_{m}H^{2}(\widehat{L})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\frac{{\rm fil}_{m}H^{2}(\widehat{L})}{{\rm fil}_{m-1}H^{2}(\widehat{L})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

it remains to show that the right vertical arrow in this
diagram is injective.

We now look at the diagram

(8.11)

\textstyle{\frac{{\rm fil}_{m}H^{2}(L)}{{\rm fil}_{m-1}H^{2}(L)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm rsw}_{L}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\oplus\Omega^{1}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
