\textstyle{\frac{{\rm fil}_{m}H^{2}(\widehat{L})}{{\rm fil}_{m-1}H^{2}(\widehat{L})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm rsw}_{\widehat{L}}}
\textstyle{\Omega^{2}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\oplus\Omega^{1}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}.}

Since
\pi_{L}
is also a uniformizer of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widehat{L}}
, it follows from

[
23
, Thm. 0.1]
that this diagram is commutative and the
horizontal arrows are injective. It follows that the left vertical arrow is injective.
This concludes the proof.
∎

We shall use the following result of Kato to prove various functorial properties
of the Brauer and Picard groups of modulus pairs.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
{L^{\prime}}/L
be a finite extension of Henselian discrete valuation fields of
characteristic
p>0
. Let
e
denote the ramification index of
{L^{\prime}}/L
.
Let
{\mathfrak{l}}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}
)
denote the residue field of
L
(resp.
L^{\prime}
). Assume that
{\mathfrak{l}}
and
{\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}
are local
fields. Then we have the following.

(1)

There is a commutative diagram

(8.12)

\textstyle{H^{2}(L^{\prime})\times L^{\prime\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Cores}}
\textstyle{H^{3}(L^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{L^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Cores}}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Cores}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm inv}_{{\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{H^{2}(L)\times L^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Res}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{3}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{L}}
\textstyle{H^{2}({\mathfrak{l}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm inv}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.}

The same holds if we interchange
{\rm Cores}
and
{\rm Res}
on the left side of the cup
product maps.

(2)

{\rm Cores}({\rm fil}_{en}H^{2}(L^{\prime}))\subseteq{\rm fil}_{n}H^{2}(L)
for any
n\geq 0
.

(3)

{\rm Res}({\rm fil}_{n}H^{2}(L))\subseteq{\rm fil}_{en}H^{2}(L^{\prime})
for any
n\geq 0
.

Proof.

(1) follows from
[
21
, § 3.2, Lem. 1, Prop. 1(2)]
,
(2) follows from (1) using that
{\rm Res}({\rm fil}_{n}L^{\times})\subset{\rm fil}_{en}L^{\prime\times}

by definition, and (3) follows from (1) and
[
42
, Lem. 6.19]
.
∎

8.3.
The Brauer group with modulus

Let
k
be a local field with ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{k}
, maximal ideal

{\mathfrak{m}}=(\pi)
and residue field
{\mathfrak{f}}={{\mathcal{O}}_{k}}/{{\mathfrak{m}}}\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, where

q=p^{n}
for some prime
p\geq 2
and integer
n\geq 1
.
Let
X
be a connected regular quasi-projective scheme over
k
of dimension

d\geq 1
. Let
D\subset X
be an effective divisor (possibly empty) and

D^{\dagger}=D_{\rm red}
.
We write
D=\sum_{x\in X^{(1)}}n_{x}\overline{\{x\}}
. Clearly,
n_{x}=0
unless
x
is a
generic point of
D^{\dagger}
, and
D=0
if
D^{\dagger}
is empty. We let

{\rm Div}_{D^{\dagger}}(X)
be the set of all effective
divisors on
X
whose support is
D^{\dagger}
. This is a filtered set under
inclusion. We let
{\rm Div}(X)
denote the filtered set of all effective Cartier
(equivalently Weil) divisors on
X
.
We let
X^{o}=X\setminus D
. We let
j\colon X^{o}\hookrightarrow X
and
t\colon D\hookrightarrow X
be
the inclusions. We let
K
denote the function field of
X
.

For any
x\in X^{(1)}
, we let
K_{x}
denote the quotient field of

{\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x}
and
\widehat{K}_{x}
the quotient field of
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}
.
We shall say that an element
\chi\in H^{i}(K)
is unramified at a point
x\in X^{(1)}
if
the image of
\chi
under the canonical map
H^{i}(K)\to H^{i}(K_{x})
lies in the image of

H^{i}({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x})
. We shall say that
\chi
is unramified on an open subscheme
U\subseteq X
if it lies in the image of the canonical map
H^{i}(U)\to H^{i}(K)
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
U
be a connected Noetherian regular scheme with function field
K
.
Let
\chi\in{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
be unramified at all codimension one
points of
U
. Then
\chi
is unramified on
U
.

Proof.

This is an easy consequence of the purity theorem for Brauer group, see

[
4
, Thm. 3.7.7]
.
∎

Definition 8.7
.

We let
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)
denote the subgroup of
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
consisting of elements
\chi

such that for every
x\in X^{(1)}
, the image
\chi_{x}
of

\chi
under the canonical map
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)=H^{2}(K)\to H^{2}(K_{x})

lies in
{\rm fil}_{n_{x}}H^{2}(K_{x})
.

Lemma 8.8
.

We have the following relations between the subgroups of
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
.

(1)

For every pair of effective Cartier divisors
D\leq D^{\prime}
on
X
, one has

{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\subseteq{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)\subseteq{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D^{\prime})\subseteq{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\subseteq{\operatorname{Br}}(K).

(2)

{\underset{n\geq 1}{\varinjlim}}\ {\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|nD)\ \xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{D^{\prime}\in{\rm Div}_{D^{\dagger}}(X)}{\varinjlim}}{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o}).

(3)

{\underset{D^{\prime}\in{\rm Div}(X)}{\varinjlim}}{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{Br}}(K).

(4)

If
{\rm char}(k)=0
, then
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
for every

D\in{\rm Div}_{D^{\dagger}}(X)
.

Proof.

Part (1) follows easily from Lemmas
8.4
and

8.6
. To prove (2), let
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
. For every
irreducible component
D^{\dagger}_{i}
of
D^{\dagger}
, (
8.6
) says that
the image of
w
in
{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x_{i}})
lies in
{\rm fil}_{m_{i}}H^{2}(K_{x_{i}})
for some
m_{i}\geq 0
,
where
D^{\dagger}_{i}=\overline{\{x_{i}\}}
. We now take
D=\sum_{i}m_{i}D^{\dagger}_{i}\in{\rm Div}_{D^{\dagger}}(X)
. Then it is clear that
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)
.
Part (3) follows from (2) and
[
35
, Lem. III.1.16]
, and (4) follows
from the definition of
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)
.
∎

We shall now define the Brauer group of a modulus pair.
We let
{\mathcal{C}}(X)
denote the set of integral curves
on
X
and let
{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
denote the subset of
{\mathcal{C}}(X)
consisting of those curves
which are not contained in
D
. For any
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, we let
\nu\colon C_{n}\to X
denote the canonical map from the normalization of
C
and let
\nu^{*}(D)

denote the scheme theoretic Pull-back of
D
. We let
C^{o}_{n}=\nu^{-1}(X^{o})
.

Definition 8.9
.

We let
{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
denote the subgroup of
{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
consisting of elements
\chi

such that for every
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
, the Brauer class
\nu^{*}(\chi)\in{\operatorname{Br}}(C^{o}_{n})
lies in the subgroup
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(C_{n}|\nu^{*}(D))
.
The group
{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
will be called the Brauer group of the modulus pair
(X,D)
.

The Brauer group of modulus pairs has the following functorial properties first of
which is not clear for
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)
.
Recall that an admissible (resp. coadmissible) morphism of modulus pairs

f\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to(X,D)
is a morphism of schemes
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
such that

D^{\prime}\leq f^{*}(D)
(resp.
D^{\prime}\geq f^{*}(D)
) as Cartier divisors on
X^{\prime}
.
One says that
f
is strict if
D^{\prime}=f^{*}(D)
.

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
f\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to(X,D)
be a coadmissible morphism of modulus pairs over

k
. Then the pull-back on étale cohomology induces a homomorphism

f^{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\to{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})

such that
f^{*}(({\operatorname{Br}}(X|E))\subset{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|E^{\prime})
if
E\leq D
and
D^{\prime}\geq E^{\prime}\geq f^{*}(E)
.
If
g\colon(X^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\to(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is another coadmissible morphism of modulus
pairs, then
(f\circ g)^{*}=g^{*}\circ f^{*}
.

Proof.

We let
U^{\prime}=f^{-1}(X^{o})
so that
X^{\prime o}\subset U^{\prime}
. We then have the pull-back map

f^{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(U^{\prime})\subset{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime o})
. Suppose now that
\chi\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
and let
C^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})
. If the image of
C^{\prime}
under
f

is a closed point, then
this closed point must lie in
X^{o}
. In the latter case, it is clear that
the pull-back of
\chi
under the composite map
C^{\prime}_{n}\to U^{\prime}\to X^{o}
lies in

{\operatorname{Br}}(C^{\prime}_{n})\subset{\operatorname{Br}}(C^{\prime}_{n}|\nu^{\prime*}(D^{\prime}))={\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(C^{\prime}_{n}|\nu^{\prime*}(D^{\prime}))
.

Otherwise, the scheme theoretic image of
C^{\prime}
under
f
is a curve
C
which is
necessarily integral and lies in
{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
. Since
f\colon C^{\prime}\to C
is also
dominant, we get a commutative diagram

(8.13)

\textstyle{C^{\prime}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\textstyle{X.}

If we let
E=\nu^{*}(D)
and
E^{\prime}=\nu^{\prime*}(D^{\prime})
, then we get
E^{\prime}\geq g^{*}(E)
by our
assumption. In other words,
g\colon(C^{\prime}_{n},E^{\prime})\to(C_{n},E)
is a dominant morphism
of one-dimensional modulus pairs. We let
L
(resp.
L^{\prime}
) be the function field of

C
(resp.
C^{\prime}
).

We now let
\omega=\nu^{*}(\chi)
and let

x\in E^{\prime}
be a closed point. If
x\notin g^{-1}(E)
, then
g^{*}(\chi)\in{\rm fil}_{0}H^{2}(L^{\prime}_{x})
. If
x\in g^{-1}(E)
, we let
y=g(x)
. We then get an
inclusion of Henselian discrete valuation fields
L_{y}\hookrightarrow L^{\prime}_{x}
. It follows from
Definition
8.3
that this inclusion induces a map

{\operatorname{Br}}(L_{y})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(L^{\prime}_{x})
which preserves the ramification filtrations.
In particular, if
n_{y}
(resp.
n_{x}
) denotes the multiplicity of
E
(resp.
E^{\prime}
)
at
y
(resp.
x
), then we get
g^{*}(w_{y})\in{\rm fil}_{n_{y}}{\operatorname{Br}}(L^{\prime}_{x})\subset{\rm fil}_{n_{x}}{\operatorname{Br}}(L^{\prime}_{x})
, where the latter inclusion holds because
E^{\prime}\geq g^{*}(E)
.
It follows that
g^{*}(\omega)\in{\operatorname{Br}}(C^{\prime}_{n}|E^{\prime})
. We have thus shown that

f^{*}(\chi)\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})
. The inclusion
f^{*}(({\operatorname{Br}}(X|E))\subset{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|E^{\prime})
, as well
as the composition law, is clear from the definition of the pull-back map. This
concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 8.11
.

Let
f\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to(X,D)
be a finite morphism of modulus pairs over
k
.
Assume that
f
is
strict. Then the norm map
f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{\prime}})\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}
induces a homomorphism

f_{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)

such that
f_{*}(({\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|E^{\prime}))\subset{\operatorname{Br}}(X|E)
if
E\leq D
and
E^{\prime}=f^{*}(E)
.
If
g\colon(X^{\prime\prime},D^{\prime\prime})\to(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is another finite and strict morphism
of modulus pairs, then
(f\circ g)_{*}=f_{*}\circ g_{*}
.

Proof.

Since
f
is strict, we have
X^{\prime o}=f^{-1}(X^{o})
and the norm
f_{*}({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{\prime}})\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}
induces
f_{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime o})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
.
To show that
f_{*}
preserves the ramification filtration, we can assume that

\text{\rm dim}(X)=\text{\rm dim}(X^{\prime})=1
. We let
K^{\prime}
be the function field of
X^{\prime}
.

We let
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})
and
x\in D^{\dagger}
.
By
[
4
, Prop. 3.8.1, Lem. 3.8.6]
, there is a commutative diagram

(8.14)

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{{\underset{y\in f^{-1}(x)}{\prod}}\ {\operatorname{Br}}(A^{\prime}_{y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}={\rm Cores}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x}),}

where
{\underset{y\in f^{-1}(x)}{\prod}}A^{\prime}_{y}=X^{\prime o}\times_{X^{o}}{\rm Spec\,}(K_{x})

and the horizontal arrows are the pull-back maps. We fix
y\in f^{-1}(x)

and let
w_{y}
be the restriction of
w
to
{\operatorname{Br}}(A^{\prime}_{y})\cong{\operatorname{Br}}(K^{\prime}_{y})
.
It suffices to show that
{\rm Cores}_{{K^{\prime}_{y}}/{K_{x}}}(w_{y})\in{\rm fil}_{n_{x}}{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x})
.
But this follows from Lemma
8.5
because
n_{y}=n_{x}e_{y/x}
,
where
e_{y/x}
is the ramification index of
{K^{\prime}_{y}}/{K_{x}}
by the definition of

f^{*}(D)
.
∎

It is clear that
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)\cong{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
when
X
is a curve. For future study,
it will be of interest to know an answer to the following.

Question 8.12
.

Let
(X,D)
be a modulus pair over
k
. Is it true that
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm div}(X|D)\cong{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
?

9.
Brauer-Manin pairing for modulus pairs

In this section, we shall define an extension of the classical Brauer-Manin pairing
between the Picard and Brauer groups of smooth projective curves over local fields to
setting of relative Picard and Brauer groups of modulus pairs.
Throughout this section, we fix a local field
k
of exponential characteristic

p\geq 1
and a connected regular projective scheme
X
of dimension
d\geq 1
over
k
.
We also fix a divisor
D\subset X
and let
D^{\dagger}=D_{\rm red}
.
We let
j\colon X^{o}\hookrightarrow X
and
\iota\colon D\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusions,
where
X^{o}=X\setminus D
. We let
K
denote the function field of
X
.
All other notations will be those of §
8.3
.

9.1.
Idele class group

For any
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X)
, we let
k(C)
be the function field of

C
. Given
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
, we
let
k(C)_{\infty}={\underset{x\in\nu^{-1}(D^{\dagger})}{\bigoplus}}k(C)_{x}
, where

\nu\colon C_{n}\to X
is the canonical map from the normalization of
C
.
We let
k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}={\underset{x\in\nu^{-1}(D^{\dagger})}{\bigoplus}}k(C)^{\times}_{x},\ I(C_{n}|D)={\underset{x\in\nu^{-1}(D^{\dagger})}{\bigoplus}}(1+{\mathcal{I}}_{D}{\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{C_{n},x})
and

\widehat{I}(C_{n}|D)={\underset{x\in\nu^{-1}(D^{\dagger})}{\bigoplus}}(1+{\mathcal{I}}_{D}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n},x}})
,
where
{\mathcal{I}}_{D}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is the ideal sheaf defining
D
.
The inclusions
k(C)\hookrightarrow k(C)_{x}
induce a canonical map

\iota_{C}\colon k(C)^{\times}\to{k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}}/{I(C_{n}|D)}
.
We also have the map
\partial_{C_{n}}\colon k(C)^{\times}\xrightarrow{{\rm div}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(C_{n}^{o})\xrightarrow{\nu_{*}}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})
,
where
C^{o}_{n}=\nu^{-1}(X^{o})
.
We let
{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n},\infty}={\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n},\nu^{-1}(D^{\dagger})}
.

It is easy to check that the left square in the diagram

(9.1)

\textstyle{{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}{K}^{M}_{1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n},\infty},{\mathcal{I}}_{D}{\mathcal{O}}_{C_{n},\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm div}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}k(C)^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{({\rm div},\iota_{C})}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}}{I(C_{n}|D)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is commutative, where the left vertical arrow is the canonical inclusion,
the middle vertical arrow is the identity map of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})
and

{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\prime}
is the cokernel of the left horizontal arrow in
the bottom row. A straightforward application of the weak approximation theorem
(e.g., see
[
28
, Lem. 6.3]
) allows one to conclude that the left square
induces a natural isomorphism
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\prime}
.
In other words, there is a canonical exact sequence

(9.2)

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}k(C)^{\times}\xrightarrow{({\rm div},\iota_{C})}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}}{I(C_{n}|D)}\right)\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\to 0.

Remark 9.1
.

We remark that the canonical map
\frac{k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}}{I(C_{n}|D)}\to\frac{\widehat{k(C)}^{\times}_{\infty}}{\widehat{I}(C_{n}|D)}
is an isomorphism for
every
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
. In particular, the exact sequence (
9.2
)
remains unchanged if we replace
k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}
by
\widehat{k(C)}^{\times}_{\infty}

and
I(C_{n}|D)
by
\widehat{I}(C_{n}|D)
for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
. This can be easily verified.

Definition 9.2
.

The idele class group
C(X^{o})
is the cokernel of the map

{\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}k(C)^{\times}\xrightarrow{({\rm div},\iota_{C})}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}{\widehat{k(C)}^{\times}_{\infty}}\right).

One can show using
[
15
, Prop. 5.3]
that
C(X^{o})
depends only on

X^{o}
and not on
X
. Using Remark
9.1
,
it is clear that there is a canonical surjective morphism
of pro-abelian groups
\{C(X^{o})\}\twoheadrightarrow\{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\}_{D\in{\rm Div}_{D^{\dagger}}(X)}
.
Taking the limit, we get a homomorphism of abelian groups

(9.3)

\theta_{X^{o}}\colon C(X^{o})\to{\underset{D\in{\rm Div}_{D^{\dagger}}(X)}{\varprojlim}}\ {\rm CH}_{0}(X|D).

It is well known (e.g., see the proof of
[
15
, Prop. 5.3]
) that the sequence
(
9.2
) and the one in the definition of
C(X^{o})
are
covariantly functorial for a finite morphism of modulus pairs
f\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to(X,D)
. Hence, they induce push-forward maps
f_{*}\colon{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)

and
f_{*}\colon C(X^{\prime o})\to C(X^{o})
.
Furthermore, (
9.3
) is compatible with the push-forward maps.

9.2.
The pairing between Brauer and Chow groups with modulus

Let the notations be as above.
We shall now define a bilinear pairing between the Brauer group and the Chow group
of the modulus pair
(X,D)
. In the following sections, we shall prove several
properties of this pairing which will be key to the proofs of our main results.

Given a closed point
x\in X^{o}
with the inclusion

\iota_{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X^{o}
,
we have the pull-back map
\iota^{*}_{x}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(k(x))
. By composing with

{\rm inv}_{k(x)}
, we get a canonical map
{\iota}^{*}_{x}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
We thus get a bilinear pairing
{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\times{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
given by

\langle\omega,\sum_{i}n_{i}[x_{i}]\rangle=\sum_{i}n_{i}{\iota}^{*}_{x_{i}}(\omega)
.

Suppose now that
d=1
and
x\in D
, where
D=\sum_{x}n_{x}[x]
.
Since
K_{x}
is a Henselian discrete
valuation field whose residue field is a local field, the cup product of
(
8.5
) and Lemma
8.4
(1) (we note here that
Lemma
8.4
(1) holds in characteristic zero as well, see

[
21
, p. 311]
) together induce a
bilinear pairing
{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x})\times K^{\times}_{x}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
. By composing with
the canonical map
{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x})
, this yields a pairing

{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\times K^{\times}_{x}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Moreover, it follows from
Definition
8.3
that the induced pairing

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times K^{\times}_{x}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
uniquely factors through

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{K^{\times}_{x}}/{{\rm fil}_{n_{x}}K^{\times}_{x}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
Summing over the points of
D
, we get a pairing

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{K^{\times}_{\infty}}/{I(X|D)}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

It follows from the above discussion that for any
d\geq 1
, there
exist bilinear pairings

(9.4)

{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\times\left[{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}{k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}}\right)\right]\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}};\ \ \mbox{and}

(9.5)

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times\left[{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\underset{C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)}{\bigoplus}}\frac{k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}}{I(C_{n}|D)}\right)\right]\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

One checks (cf. Remark
9.1
) that the pairing
(
9.5
) remains unchanged if we replace

k(C)^{\times}_{\infty}
by
\widehat{k(C)}^{\times}_{\infty}
and
I(C_{n}|D)
by

\widehat{I}(C_{n}|D)
for
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
.

We recall the following reciprocity law due to Kato-Saito
[
26
]

when
d=1
.
Let
m\neq 0
be an integer. Using the localization sequence, together with the
purity theorem (due to Gabber when
p\nmid m
) and Corollary
4.6

when
m=p^{n}
), we get maps

(9.6)

H^{3}(K,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(2))\xrightarrow{\partial_{X}}{\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\bigoplus}}H^{2}(k(x),{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m(1))\xrightarrow{\sum_{x}{\rm inv}_{k(x)}}{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m.

Lemma 9.3
.

The composite map
(\sum_{x}{\rm inv}_{k(x)})\circ\partial_{X}
in (
9.6
) is zero.

Proof.

This is proven in
[
26
, § 5, p. 120]
.
∎

Proposition 9.4
.

The pairings (
9.4
) and (
9.5
) descend to bilinear maps
of abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\times C(X^{o})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}};\ \mbox{and}

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

We let
C\in{\mathcal{C}}(X|D)
and let
\nu\colon C_{n}\to X
be the canonical map from the
normalization of
C
. We write
E=\nu^{*}(D)
.
It follows from Proposition
8.10
and the remark below
(
9.2
) that there is a diagram

(9.7)

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu^{*}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(C_{n}|E)\times{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(C^{o}_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{*}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.}

It follows easily form the construction of (
9.5
) that this diagram is
commutative. Using this, and a similar commutative diagram
associated to the first pairing, it suffices to prove the
proposition when
d=1
. We shall thus assume that
X
is a curve.

We let
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
and let
\chi_{w}\colon{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus{K^{\times}_{\infty}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be the associated
character. We shall show that the composite map

(9.8)

K^{\times}\to{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus K^{\times}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\chi_{w}}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

is zero. This will automatically imply that the composite map

K^{\times}\to{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus({K^{\times}_{\infty}}/{I(X|D)})\xrightarrow{\chi_{w}}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

is zero if
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
.

We let
I(X):={\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\prod^{\prime}}}K^{\times}_{x}
denote the restricted
product with respect to the subgroups
({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x})^{\times}
. We consider the diagram

(9.9)

\textstyle{K^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{({\rm div},\iota_{X})}
\textstyle{I(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(H^{2}(K),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus{K^{\times}_{\infty}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{w}}
\textstyle{{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}({\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o}),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}),}

where
\alpha
is the canonical inclusion,
the bottom horizontal arrow is induced by the pairing (
9.8
)
and the right vertical arrow is induced by the canonical inclusion

{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
. To describe
\beta
, we write

I(X)=I(X^{o})\bigoplus K^{\times}_{\infty}
. We let
\beta
to be identity on

K^{\times}_{\infty}
and
\beta(a_{x})=v_{x}(a_{x})
if
a_{x}\in K^{\times}_{x}
with

x\in X^{o}
. This is well-defined because
v_{x}(({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x})^{\times})=0
.
It is clear that the left triangle commutes.

To define
\gamma
, we let
\chi\in H^{2}(K)
and
(a_{x})\in I(X)
. It follows
from Lemma
8.8
(3) that
\chi\in{\operatorname{Br}}(U)
for some dense
open subscheme
U\subset X^{o}
. We conclude by
[
46
, Thm. 2.7(4)]

that
\langle\chi,a_{x}\rangle=0
for all
x\in U
. In particular, the sum

\sum_{x\in X_{(0)}}\langle\chi,a_{x}\rangle
is finite. This shows that the cup product
of Lemma
8.4
(4) induces a pairing

(9.10)

H^{2}(K)\times I(X)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}},

which is clearly compatible with (
9.4
).
Letting
\gamma
denote the induced map
I(X)\to{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(H^{2}(K),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
,
it follows that the right square in (
9.9
) is commutative.
Hence, it suffices to show that
\gamma\circ\alpha=0
.

We let
a\in K^{\times},\ \chi\in H^{2}(K)
and write
b=\langle\chi,a\rangle\in H^{3}(K)
. We let
a_{x}
(resp.
\chi_{x}
) be the image of
a
(resp.
\chi
) in

K^{\times}_{x}
(resp.
H^{3}(K_{x})
). We need to show that

\sum_{x\in X_{(0)}}\langle\chi_{x},a_{x}\rangle=0
. Equivalently, we need to show that

(\sum_{x}{\rm inv}_{k(x)})\circ\partial_{X}(b)=0
in (
9.6
).
But this follows from Lemma
9.3
.
∎

The following is straightforward from the construction of the pairings
(
9.4
) and (
9.5
).

Corollary 9.5
.

There is a commutative diagram of bilinear pairings

(9.11)

\textstyle{``{{\underset{n}{\varinjlim}}}"{\operatorname{Br}}(X|nD)\times``{{\underset{n}{\varprojlim}}}"{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\hskip 11.38092pt\times\hskip 11.38092ptC(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}}

between ind and pro abelian groups.

The following corollary provides a quick proof of
the Brauer-Manin duality isomorphism
\beta_{X}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}({\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star})_{\rm tor}
of Lichtenbaum
[
32
]
and Saito
[
46
]
,
assuming that
\beta_{X}
is injective. Recall that the main difficulty in proving that

\beta_{X}
is an isomorphism lies in showing its surjectivity.

Corollary 9.6
.

Let
X
be a geometrically connected smooth projective curve over
k
.
Then the pairing of Proposition
9.4
(with
D=\emptyset
) induces
an isomorphism
\beta_{X}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}({\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star})_{\rm tor}
.

Proof.

We fix an integer
n\geq 1
and look at the diagram

(9.12)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/{p^{n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{p^{n}}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{p^{n}}}
\textstyle{{}_{p^{n}}{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{p^{n}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(_{p^{n}}{\operatorname{Br}}(X))^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/{p^{n}})^{\vee},}

where
\alpha_{p^{n}}
and
\gamma_{p^{n}}
are induced by the pairing
(
9.11
). The map
\beta_{p^{n}}
is induced by
the pairing of Theorem
4.7
.
From the definition of the torsion-by-profinite topology of

H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})
, one knows that the image of the map

{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/{p^{n}}\to H^{1}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X,{\operatorname{log}}})

in the top row (
9.12
)
is open (see (
3.9
) and §
3.2
).
In particular, the quotient topology of
{}_{p^{n}}{\operatorname{Br}}(X)
is discrete.
This shows that the bottom arrow of (
9.12
) is defined and
exact. It is an easy exercise that this diagram is commutative.

The middle arrow is bijective by Theorem
4.7
.
Since
\gamma_{p^{n}}
is known to be injective (cf. Lemma
12.1
), it follows
that
\alpha_{p^{n}}
is an isomorphism. Since

{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/{p^{n}}
is profinite
1
1

1

This is the only place where the
smoothness of
X
is used.
, it follows from Lemma
2.5
and
Pontryagin duality between profinite and discrete torsion groups that

\gamma_{p^{n}}
maps
{}_{p^{n}}{\operatorname{Br}}(X)
isomorphically onto
({{\rm CH}_{0}(X)}/{p^{n}})^{\star}
.
Taking the limit, we conclude that

\beta_{X}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\{p\}\xrightarrow{\cong}({\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star})\{p\}

is an isomorphism.
An identical argument (with Theorem
4.7

replaced by Saito-Tate duality, see
[
15
, Thm. 9.9]
)
shows that
\beta_{X}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\{p^{\prime}\}\xrightarrow{\cong}({\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star})\{p^{\prime}\}
.
is also bijective. This concludes the proof.
∎

We end this section with the following functorial property of the Brauer-Manin
pairings of modulus pairs.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
f\colon(X^{\prime},D)\to(X,D)
be a finite and strict morphism between modulus pairs,
where
X
and
X^{\prime}
are connected regular projective curves over
k
. Then we have
a commutative diagram

(9.13)

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\times{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.}

Proof.

We first note that
f^{*}
is the well-known flat pull-back map between
the Chow groups with modulus and
f_{*}
is defined by Proposition
8.11
.
To show the commutativity of
(
9.13
), we fix a Brauer class
w^{\prime}\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})
and let

w=f_{*}(w^{\prime})
. We need to show that the right side triangle in the diagram

(9.14)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{w}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{\prime o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{w^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}}

is commutative.
Since the left square is commutative and its top horizontal arrow is surjective,
it suffices to show that the outer trapezium in (
9.14
) is commutative
when evaluated on every free generator of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})
.

Let
x\in X^{o}
be a closed point and let
f^{*}([x])=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}n_{i}[y_{i}]
. Let
\iota_{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X^{o}

and
\iota_{y_{i}}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(y_{i}))\hookrightarrow X^{\prime o}
be the inclusion maps.
By the definition of the Brauer-Manin pairing in §
9.2
, we need to show
that
{\rm inv}_{k(x)}(\iota^{*}_{x}(w))=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}n_{i}{\rm inv}_{k_{y_{i}}}(\iota^{*}_{y_{i}}(w^{\prime}))
. But this follows by a combination of

[
4
, Prop. 3.8.1, Lem. 3.8.6]
and
[
21
, § 3.2, Prop. 1(2)]
.
∎

10.
Local vs. global duality for
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
on compact curve

In this section, we shall establish a duality for some cohomology groups of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
on
a smooth projective curve over a local field and show that it is
compatible with the duality à la Kato
[
21
]
for the local cohomology of

{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
at closed points of the curve. This compatibility
will be another key step in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

10.1.
Local duality for
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}

Let
R
be a equicharacteristic Henselian discrete valuation ring whose residue field

{\mathfrak{f}}
is a local field of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
.
Let
L
denote the quotient field of
R
. Let
x={\rm Spec\,}({\mathfrak{f}})
denote the
closed point of
W={\rm Spec\,}(R)
. We let
\widehat{W}={\rm Spec\,}(\widehat{R})
.
We choose a common uniformizer
\pi
of
R
and
\widehat{R}
,
and let
W_{n}={\rm Spec\,}(R/{(\pi^{n})})
for
n\geq 1
.
The following result is due to Kato
[
21
, § 3.5]
.

Theorem 10.1
.

There is a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}(L)\times(L^{\times})^{\rm pf}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}},

where
{\operatorname{Br}}(L)
and
L^{\times}
are endowed with the discrete and the Kato topologies,
respectively.

We remark that Kato proved this result for
\widehat{L}
. However, it is easily checked
that the canonical maps
{\operatorname{Br}}(L)\to{\operatorname{Br}}(\widehat{L})
and
({L}^{\times})^{\rm pf}\to(\widehat{L}^{\times})^{\rm pf}
are topological isomorphisms.

Let
\gamma_{L}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(L)\to(L^{\times})^{\star}
denote the map induced by the
above pairing. It follows from Theorem
10.1

(see
[
46
, Rem. 2.14]
) that there is a commutative diagram of exact
sequences

(10.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{R}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{L}}
\textstyle{H^{3}_{{\text{\'{e}t}},x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{x}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(v_{L})^{\star}}
\textstyle{(L^{\times})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(R^{\times})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the duals
(L^{\times})^{\star}
and
(R^{\times})^{\star}
are considered with respect to the
Kato topology.

In the above diagram,
\gamma_{R}
is an isomorphism because it is the composition

{\operatorname{Br}}(R)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{Br}}({\mathfrak{f}})\xrightarrow{{\rm inv}_{\mathfrak{f}}}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}={{\mathbb{Z}}}^{\star}
.

\gamma_{L}
is an isomorphism if
p>1
by
[
21
, § 3.5, Rem. 4]
.
It maps
{\operatorname{Br}}(L)
isomorphically onto the subgroup
((L^{\times})^{\star})_{\rm tor}
if
p=1
.
It follows that
\gamma_{x}
is an isomorphism if
p>1
, and maps

H^{3}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})
isomorphically onto
((R^{\times})^{\star})_{\rm tor}
if
p=1
.
Recall that each
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(W_{n})
is endowed with the adic topology.

Lemma 10.2
.

For
R
as above, we have the following.

(1)

There exists a unique isomorphism
\gamma_{x}\colon H^{3}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\xrightarrow{\cong}((R^{\times})^{\star})_{\rm tor}
such that (
10.1
) is a
commutative diagram.

(2)

If
p>1
, each of the groups
(R^{\times})^{\star},\ (\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}
and

({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}({W_{n}}))^{\star}
(
n\geq 1
) is torsion.

(3)

The canonical maps
H^{3}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\to H^{3}_{x}(\widehat{W},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{\widehat{W}})

and
(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}\to(R^{\times})^{\star}
are isomorphisms.

(4)

If
p>1
, one has a canonical isomorphism

(10.2)

\gamma_{x}\colon H^{3}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\varinjlim}_{n}\ ({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}(W_{n}))^{\star},

where the duals on the right hand side are taken with respect to the adic topology.

(5)

If
p=1
, the canonical map
({\mathfrak{f}}^{\times})^{\star}\to(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}

induces an isomorphism
({\mathfrak{f}}^{\times})^{\star}\cong((\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star})_{\rm tor}
.

Proof.

To prove (2), note that the map
({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}({W_{n}}))^{\star}\hookrightarrow(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}

is injective for all
n\geq 1
. Using the bottom row of (
10.1
),
it suffices therefore to show that
(L^{\times})^{\star}
and
(\widehat{L}^{\times})^{\star}
are
torsion groups. Using the definition of Kato topology, it is an easy exercise that the
canonical map
(\widehat{L}^{\times})^{\star}\to(L^{\times})^{\star}
is an isomorphism. But

[
21
, § 3.5, Rem. 4]
says that
(\widehat{L}^{\times})^{\star}
is a torsion group
if
p>1
. The statement (1) is already shown above.
The statement (3) follows easily by comparing (
10.1
) for
R
and

\widehat{R}
and using the isomorphism
(\widehat{L}^{\times})^{\star}\xrightarrow{\cong}(L^{\times})^{\star}
. To show (4),
we note that the map
{R}/{(\pi^{n})}\to{\widehat{R}}/{(\pi^{n})}
is bijective
for
n\geq 1
. Furthermore, the map

\widehat{R}^{\times}\to{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {\mathcal{O}}^{\times}({W_{n}})

is an isomorphism of topological abelian groups by Lemma
7.1
.
It is an elementary checking (cf. Lemma
2.6
) that the induced map

{\varinjlim}_{n}\ ({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}({W_{n}}))^{\star}\to(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}

is bijective.

To prove (5), we can replace
(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}
by
(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\vee}

since
\widehat{R}^{\times}
is a discrete group. By
Lemma
7.4
, we can also replace
({\mathfrak{f}}^{\times})^{\star}
by

(({\mathfrak{f}}^{\times})^{\vee})_{\rm tor}
. We are now done because it is clear that

({\mathfrak{f}}^{\times})^{\vee}\to(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\vee}
is injective, and it is surjective at the
level of torsion subgroups by
[
15
, Lem. 5.9]
. This concludes the proof.
∎

10.2.
Global duality for
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}

Let
k
be a local field of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
and let
X
be a
smooth and geometrically connected projective curve over
k
.
Let
K
denote the function field of

X
. The subject of this subsection is the construction of a perfect duality
between
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
and
H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
which is compatible with
the local duality of §
10.1
.

One can deduce from the
Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence that there is an isomorphism

H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\cong H^{2}(k,{\rm Pic}(X_{s}))
, where
X_{s}
is the base change of
X

by
k_{s}
. Using the decomposition
{\rm Pic}(X_{s})\cong{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{s})\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}
as

G_{k}
-module and the vanishing
H^{2}(k,{\rm Pic}^{0}(X_{s}))=0

(see
[
37
, Thm. 7.8]
), one deduces an isomorphism

\nu_{X}\colon H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\cong(G_{k})^{\star}\cong(k^{\times})^{\star}
.
However, this isomorphism does not serve our purpose. The reason is that it is
not known if
\nu_{X}
is compatible with the local duality isomorphism
\gamma_{x}
in
Lemma
10.2
for a closed point
x\in X
, in positive characteristic.
As mentioned above, this compatibility is very crucial for proving our main results.
The compatibility of
\nu_{X}
with the local duality seems like a
challenging independent problem. The main obstacle in solving this is the failure
of purity for
H^{2}_{x}(X,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{X.{\operatorname{log}}})
.

To serve our purpose, we shall construct a different pairing between

H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
and
H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
. We shall show that this pairing is
perfect and is compatible with the local duality isomorphism
\gamma_{x}
for

x\in X_{(0)}
. We do not have any guess whether the two duality maps coincide.
We let
H^{i}(X,\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(j)):={\varprojlim}_{n}\ H^{i}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(j))
,
where the limit is taken over
{\mathbb{N}}
. We begin with the following.

Lemma 10.3
.

There exists a canonical bilinear pairing

(10.3)

H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\times H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.1
, we can replace
H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
by

H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))
. Using (
4.17
) and the analogous diagram
for the sheaves
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}(r)
(with
n
prime to
p
), we get a bilinear pairing
between ind-abelian and pro-abelian groups

``{{\underset{n}{\varinjlim}}}"\ H^{i}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(j))\times``{{\underset{n}{\varprojlim}}}"\ H^{i^{\prime}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(j^{\prime}))\to``{{\underset{n}{\varinjlim}}}"\ H^{i+i^{\prime}}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(j+j^{\prime})).

Taking the limits, we conclude by the Saito-Tate duality

[
47
]
(see also
[
15
, Thm. 9.9]
) in the prime-to-
p
case
and Corollary
4.8
in the
p
-primary case
that there is a bilinear pairing between abelian groups

(10.4)

H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\times H^{1}(X,\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Composing this with the canonical maps (see (
8.1
))

(10.5)

H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\to{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/n\hookrightarrow H^{1}(X,\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1)),

we get the desired pairing.
∎

In the remainder of this subsection, our goal is to prove the perfectness of
(
10.3
) with respect to the discrete topology on

H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
and the adic topology on
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\cong k^{\times}
.
We begin by noting that the diagrams in (
8.2
) give rise to an exact
sequence of pro-abelian groups

(10.6)

0\to\{{H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/{n}\}\to\{H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\}\to\{_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\}\to 0,

whose transition maps are induced by the canonical surjections

{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{mn}\twoheadrightarrow{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{n}
. Taking the limits, we get an exact sequence

(10.7)

0\to{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/n\to H^{1}(X,\widehat{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\to{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {}_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\to 0.

Applying the dual functor in the category of abelian groups, we get an
exact sequence

(10.8)

0\to{\varinjlim}_{n}\ (_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X))^{\vee}\to{\varinjlim}_{n}\ (H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1)))^{\vee}\to{\varinjlim}_{n}\ ({H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/n)^{\vee}\to 0.

Lemma 10.4
.

We have
{\underset{n}{\varprojlim}}\ _{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)={\underset{n}{\varinjlim}}\ (_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X))^{\vee}=0
.

Proof.

One knows that there is a canonical inclusion

{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {}_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\hookrightarrow{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {}_{n}J_{X}(k)
, where
J_{X}

is the Jacobian variety of
X
(e.g., see
[
36
, Rem. 1.5]
).
On the other hand,
J_{X}(k)
is a profinite abelian group
(see the proof of Corollary
6.11
) and
{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {}_{n}J_{X}(k)

coincides with the Tate module of
J_{X}(k)
. It follows that

{\varprojlim}_{n}\ {}_{n}J_{X}(k)=0
as the Tate module of a profinite abelian
group is zero.

To show that
{\varinjlim}_{n}\ (_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X))^{\vee}
is zero, it suffices to
show the stronger assertion that the pro-abelian group

\{_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\}
is zero as it would imply that the ind-abelian group

\{(_{n}{\rm Pic}^{0}(X))^{\vee}\}
is zero.
But this follows from our first assertion and the well known fact that a
pro-abelian group satisfying the Mittag-Leffler condition is zero if and only if its
limit is zero (e.g., see
[
54
, Tag 07KV, Lem. 15.86.13]
).
∎

Lemma 10.5
.

The map
\delta_{X}\colon H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\to H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})^{\vee}
,
induced by (
10.3
), is injective.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.1
and (
10.5
),
\delta_{X}
is the same as
the composite map

H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\to{\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))^{\vee}\to{\varinjlim}_{n}\ ({H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/n)^{\vee}\to H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})^{\vee}.

The first arrow in this sequence is injective in the
p
-primary case by
Theorem
4.7

and isomorphism in the prime-to-
p
case by
[
15
, Thm. 9.9]
.
The second arrow is injective by (
10.8
) and Lemma
10.4
.
The third arrow is easily seen to be injective. This concludes the proof.
∎

The following shows the compatibility between the local and global duality maps.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
x\in X_{(0)},\ R={\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x}
and let the notations be as in
Lemma
10.2
. Then the diagram

(10.9)

\textstyle{H^{3}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{x}}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{X}}
\textstyle{(R^{\times})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}))^{\vee}}

is commutative if we let the top horizontal arrow be the forget support map and
the bottom horizontal arrow be the dual of the canonical pull-back map

H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\to R^{\times}
.

Proof.

Using (
10.1
), the lemma is equivalent to showing that the diagram

(10.10)

\textstyle{H^{2}(K_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{K_{x}}}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{X}}
\textstyle{(K^{\times}_{x})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{x}}
\textstyle{(H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}))^{\vee}}

is commutative, where
\phi_{x}
is induced by the canonical pull-back map.
To show this, we can replace
H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
by

H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))
using Lemma
8.1
. It also suffices to
prove the commutativity after replacing
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
by
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n
for all
n\geq 1
.

We now fix an integer
n\geq 1
. We let
\alpha\in H^{2}(K_{x},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))
and

\beta\in H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
be arbitrary elements. We look at the diagram

(10.11)

\textstyle{H^{2}(K_{x},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\times H^{1}(K_{x},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{3}(K_{x},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{x}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{H^{3}_{x}(W,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\times H^{1}(W,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{4}_{x}(W,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H^{3}_{x}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\times H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{4}_{x}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon_{x}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\times H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\cup}
\textstyle{H^{4}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{X}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\times{H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/n\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{X}}
\textstyle{H^{4}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{{\operatorname{id}}}
\scriptstyle{{\rm Tr}_{X}}

where
\psi_{X}
is the canonical map (see (
10.6
)).

All squares on the left are induced by the cup products, pull-back and the boundary
maps in étale cohomology. In particular, these squares are known to be
commutative (e.g., see (
4.15
) for the commutativity of the top
square on the left).
The upper square on the right is commutative by the definition of
{\rm Tr}_{x}

(see Corollary
4.6
and note that this also holds when
p\nmid n
).
The lower square on the right commutes by (
4.8
) when

n=p^{n}
and by
[
15
, Lem. 9.7]
when
p\nmid n
.
The bottom right triangle clearly commutes.

We let
\theta\colon H^{2}(K_{x},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))\to H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))
be the
composition of all vertical arrows going down on the extreme left of
(
10.11
) and let
\theta^{\prime}\colon{H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})}/n\to H^{1}(K_{x},{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))
be the composition of all vertical arrows going up
in the middle of (
10.11
). It follows then by a diagram chase that

{\rm Tr}_{x}(\alpha\cup\theta^{\prime}(\beta))={\rm Tr}_{X}(\theta(\alpha)\cup\psi_{X}(\beta))
.
On the other hand, it is straightforward to check that

\langle\phi_{x}\circ\gamma_{K_{x}}(\alpha),\beta\rangle={\rm Tr}_{x}(\alpha\cup\theta^{\prime}(\beta))\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \langle\delta_{X}\circ\partial_{x}(\alpha),\beta\rangle={\rm Tr}_{X}(\theta(\alpha)\cup\psi_{X}(\beta)).

This proves the commutativity of (
10.10
) and concludes the proof of
the lemma.
∎

We shall also need the following independent result for proving
the perfectness of (
10.3
) as well as in the proof of
Lemma
11.3
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
{k^{\prime}}/{k}
be a finite field extension of
k
. Then the inclusion

k^{\times}\hookrightarrow k^{\prime\times}
induces a surjective map
(k^{\prime\times})^{\star}\twoheadrightarrow(k^{\times})^{\star}
when the duals are taken with respect to the adic topologies.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in(k^{\times})^{\star}
.
Since the inclusion
k^{\times}\hookrightarrow k^{\prime\times}
is continuous and its
image is closed (e.g., see
[
20
, § 7.2]
or

[
39
, Prop. II.5.7]
) with respect to the adic
topologies, it follows from
[
9
, Cor. 4.42]
that there exists a
continuous homomorphism
\chi^{\prime}\colon k^{\prime\times}\to{\mathbb{T}}
whose restriction to
k^{\times}

is
\chi
. It remains to show that
\chi^{\prime}
has finite order.
Since
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k^{\prime}}
is profinite, it is clear that
\chi^{\prime}
has finite order when
we restrict it to
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k^{\prime}}
. We let
G=(k^{\times})\cdot({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k^{\prime}})\subset k^{\prime\times}
. Then
G
is a closed subgroup of
k^{\prime\times}
and
\chi^{\prime}
has finite order
(say,
m
) on
G
.

We now look at the commutative diagram of exact sequences

(10.12)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k^{\prime\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{e}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
e
is the ramification index of
{k^{\prime}}/{k}
.
It follows that
\chi^{\prime}(k^{\prime\times})
is finite. This concludes the proof.
∎

We can now prove the main result of §
10
.

Theorem 10.8
.

The bilinear pairing of (
10.3
) induces a perfect pairing of
topological abelian groups

(10.13)

\delta_{X}\colon H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\times H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})^{\rm pf}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

We can identify
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
with
k^{\times}
and

H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
with
H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))
(cf. Lemma
8.1
).
In the first step, we show that
\delta_{X}\colon H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\to(k^{\times})^{\vee}
factors through
(k^{\times})^{\star}
. Since
(k^{\times})^{\star}\cong{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(k^{\times},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\{p^{\prime}\})\bigoplus{\rm Hom}_{\mathbf{Tab}}(k^{\times},{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}})
and
H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
is a torsion
group, it suffices to consider the prime-to-
p
and
p
-primary cases
separately.

On the prime-to-
p
torsion subgroup,
\delta_{X}
is identified with the composite map

(10.14)

H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\{p^{\prime}\}\to{\underset{n\in k^{\times}}{\varinjlim}}\ (H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))^{\vee}\to{\underset{n\in k^{\times}}{\varinjlim}}\ ({k^{\times}}/n)^{\vee}\to(k^{\times})^{\vee}.

Since the groups
H^{i}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(j))
are finite for
n\in k^{\times}

(e.g., see
[
15
, Thm. 9.9]
),
we have
H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))^{\star}\cong H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))^{\vee}
.
Since
{k^{\times}}/n
is also discrete (and finite),
it follows that
({k^{\times}}/n)^{\star}\cong({k^{\times}}/n)^{\vee}
for every
n\geq 1
. Hence, the above sequence of maps has a
factorization

(10.15)

H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\{p^{\prime}\}\to{\underset{n\in k^{\times}}{\varinjlim}}\ H^{1}(X,{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n(1))^{\star}\to{\underset{n\in k^{\times}}{\varinjlim}}\ ({k^{\times}}/n)^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}.

To prove the
p
-primary case, we can assume
p>1
. We
choose a closed point
x\in X
. We follow the
notations of Lemma
10.2
and look at the commutative diagram

(10.16)

\textstyle{H^{3}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\{p\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{x}}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\{p\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{X}}
\textstyle{{\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{0}(W_{n},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W_{n}})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(k^{\times})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(k^{\times})^{\vee}.}

Note that
\delta_{X}
is injective by Lemma
10.5
.
It is clear that the map
H^{0}(W_{n},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W_{n}})^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\vee}

factors through
(k^{\times})^{\star}
for every
n\geq 1
. In particular, the bottom
left arrow is defined. Moreover, the outer trapezium commutes by Lemma
10.6
.
To show that the image of
\delta_{X}
lies in
(k^{\times})^{\star}
, it suffices therefore
to show that the top horizontal arrow is surjective.

To show the last claim, we set
U=X\setminus\{x\}
. We now recall that

H^{i}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{Z})
is a torsion group for all
i\geq 2
if
Z\in\{X,U\}
.
Using the support cohomology exact sequence, this implies that

H^{i}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\cong H^{i}_{x}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
is a torsion group for

i\geq 3
. (the case of interest
i=3
also follows from
Lemma
10.2
). In particular, the sequence

H^{3}_{x}(W,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W})\{p\}\to H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\{p\}\to H^{3}(U,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{U})\{p\}

is exact.

On the other hand, there is a surjection

H^{2}(U,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{U,{\operatorname{log}}})\twoheadrightarrow~_{p^{n}}H^{3}(U,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{U})
for any
n\geq 1
, as
follows from (
8.1
). It suffices therefore to show that

H^{2}(U,W_{n}\Omega^{1}_{U,{\operatorname{log}}})=0
for all
n\geq 1
.
For
n=1
, note that in the exact sequence

0\to\Omega^{1}_{U,{\operatorname{log}}}\to\Omega^{1}_{U}\xrightarrow{\pi-\overline{F}}\frac{\Omega^{1}_{U}}{d{\mathcal{O}}_{U}}\to 0

(see (
3.1
)), the middle and the term on the right are coherent

{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
-modules. In particular, their cohomological dimension is
zero as
U
is affine. It follows that
H^{2}(U,\Omega^{1}_{U,{\operatorname{log}}})=0
.
The general case follows by induction on
n
using the cohomology sequence
associated to (
2.6
).

We shall now show that
\delta_{X}\colon H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\to(k^{\times})^{\star}

is bijective. It is injective by Lemma
10.5
.
To prove its surjectivity between the prime-to-
p
torsion subgroups, note that
all arrows in (
10.15
) are isomorphisms (the first arrow
is an isomorphism by the Saito-Tate duality, see
[
15
, Thm. 9.9]
).
To prove the surjectivity of
\delta_{X}
between the
p
-primary torsion subgroups,
note that this map is the same as the dotted arrow in (
10.16
).
The map
\gamma_{x}
is bijective by Lemma
10.2
(4). It suffices therefore
to show that the map
{\varinjlim}_{n}\ H^{0}(W_{n},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{W_{n}})^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}

is surjective. Equivalently, the map
(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}

is surjective, where recall that
R={\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x}
.
But this latter map is induced by the canonical inclusion

k^{\times}\hookrightarrow\widehat{R}^{\times}
. We are now done because the composite map

(k(x)^{\times})^{\star}\to(\widehat{R}^{\times})^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}
is surjective by
Lemma
10.7
.

To prove the perfectness of (
10.13
), we let
F=(k^{\times})^{\rm pf}
.
We now note that
the canonical map
F^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}
is an isomorphism, as one
easily checks using
[
39
, Prop. II.5.7]
and the profiniteness of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k}
.
It follows that (
10.3
) gives rise to (
10.13
)
and the resulting map
\delta_{X}\colon H^{3}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}(1))\to F^{\star}
is an
isomorphism.
Since this map is clearly continuous and
F
is profinite, it follows that
(
10.13
) is a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 10.9
.

We showed in the proof of Theorem
10.8
that
H^{3}(U,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{U})\{p\}=0
if
U
is the complement of a closed point in
X
. But the
proof actually shows that
H^{i}(U,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{U})\{p\}=0
if
U
is
any affine open in
X
and
i\geq 3
is any integer. This observation will be used
in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Corollary 10.10
.

For any nonempty effective Cartier divisor
D\subset X
, the forget support map

H^{3}_{D}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\to H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})
is surjective.

Proof.

Using Lemmas
10.2
,
10.6
and Theorem
10.8
,
it suffices to show that the map
(k(x)^{\times})^{\star}\to(k^{\times})^{\star}
is
surjective for any
x\in D
. But this follows from Lemma
10.7
.
∎

11.
Continuity of Brauer-Manin pairing

Let
k
be a local field of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
.
The goal of this section is to show that the Brauer-Manin pairing for
a 1-dimensional modulus pair
(X,D)
over
k
is continuous with respect to the
discrete topology of
{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
and the adic topology of
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
.
This is an important step in the proofs of the main results. We shall also prove few
more properties of this pairing which will be used in the proof of Theorem
1.6
.
We begin with the following general statement about regular (but not necessarily smooth)
curves.

Lemma 11.1
.

Let
X
be a connected and regular projective curve over
k
and let
D\subset X
be
a divisor with the complement
X^{o}
. Then the map
\theta_{X^{o}}\colon C(X^{o})\to{\underset{D^{\prime}\in{\rm Div}_{D_{\rm red}}(X)}{\varprojlim}}\ {\rm CH}_{0}(X|D^{\prime})
is bijective.
In particular, the map
C(X^{o})\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D^{\prime})
is surjective for every

D^{\prime}\in{\rm Div}_{D_{\rm red}}(X)
.

Proof.

We can replace the right hand side by
{\varprojlim}_{n}{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)
.
Using Remark
9.1
, we can replace
{k(X)}^{\times}_{\infty}
(resp.

I(X|nD)
) by
\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{\infty}
(resp.
\widehat{I}(X|nD)
) in
(
9.2
).
It suffices now to show the stronger statement that the canonical map

{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus{\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{\infty}}\to{\varprojlim}_{n}\left({\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\frac{\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{\infty}}{\widehat{I}(X|nD)}\right)

is bijective. It is clear that the right hand side of the latter map is

{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\varprojlim}_{n}\frac{\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{\infty}}{\widehat{I}(X|nD)}\right)
.
It remains therefore to show that for every
x\in D
, the map

\widehat{k(X)}_{x}^{\times}\to{\varprojlim}_{n}\frac{\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{x}}{{\rm fil}_{n}\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{x}}

is bijective. But this can be easily checked using the completeness of

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}
and the strict exact sequence of pro-abelian groups

0\to\left\{\frac{\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}^{\times}}{{\rm fil}_{n}\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{x}}\right\}_{n}\to\left\{\frac{\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{x}}{{\rm fil}_{n}\widehat{k(X)}^{\times}_{x}}\right\}_{n}\xrightarrow{v_{x}}{\mathbb{Z}}\to 0.

To see that
C(X^{o})\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D^{\prime})
is surjective, we only have to note that

\{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD^{\prime}\}_{n}
is an inverse system indexed by
{\mathbb{N}}
with surjective transition maps
whose limit is
C(X^{o})
.
∎

We now let
X
be a smooth projective geometrically integral curve
over
k
and
D\subset X
an effective divisor with support
D^{\dagger}
.
We write
D={\underset{x\in D^{\dagger}}{\sum}}n_{x}[x]\in{\rm Div}(X)
. Let

j\colon X^{o}=X\setminus D\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion. We shall consider

C(X^{o})=\varprojlim_{n}{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)
as a topological abelian group, endowed with the
inverse limit of the adic topologies of
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)
using Lemma
11.1
.

Proposition 11.2
.

The pairings

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\times{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}},\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ {\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\times C(X^{o})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

are continuous.

Proof.

Let
\beta_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\vee}
and
\beta_{X^{o}}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to C(X^{o})^{\vee}
be the induced homomorphisms.
As mentioned in §
4.2
, the proposition is equivalent to
the statement that the image of
\beta_{X|D}
(resp.
\beta_{X^{o}}
) lies in

{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
(resp.
C(X^{o})^{\star}
). Note that this uses the discreteness of

{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
.
Since
\varinjlim_{n}{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)^{\star}\xrightarrow{\cong}C(X^{o})^{\star}
,
it suffices to show that
\beta_{X|D}({\operatorname{Br}}(X|D))\subset{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
.
To prove the latter statement, we
fix
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
and let
\chi_{w}\colon{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be the
induced character.

Assume first that
p=1
. As
nw=0
for some
n\geq 1
, the map

\chi_{w}\colon{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
must factor through
{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)}/n
.
On the other hand,
{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)}/n
is a finite discrete group,
as one easily checks using (
5.1
),
[
39
, Prop. II.5.7]

and the Kummer sequence.
In particular, the map
\chi_{w}\colon{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)}/n\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is continuous.
It follows that
\beta_{X|D}(w)\in{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
.
We shall now assume in the rest of the proof that
p>1
.

We begin by proving the special case of the proposition when
D
and

X^{o}(k)
are not empty. We fix a closed point
P\in X^{o}(k)
.
As
\chi_{w}
is a group homomorphism, it will be enough to show that
it is continuous at some point of
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
.
We now note that the composition

\chi^{1}_{w}\colon X^{o}(k)\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X|D)\xrightarrow{\chi_{w}}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

(see (
9.14
)) is given by
\chi^{1}_{w}(x)={\rm inv}_{k(x)}(\iota^{*}_{x}(w))
, where

\iota_{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X^{o}
is the inclusion. It follows from

[
4
, Prop. 10.5.2]
(see also
[
41
, Prop. 8.2.9]
)
that
\chi^{1}_{w}
is continuous. In particular, the map
\chi^{r}_{w}\colon(X^{o}(k))^{r}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
, given by
\chi^{r}_{w}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\sum}}}\chi^{1}_{w}(x_{i})
, is continuous for every
r\geq 1
.

We let
{\rm alb}_{X|D}\colon X^{o}(k)\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)
be the albanese map,
given by
{\rm alb}_{X|D}(x)=[x]-[P]
(cf. (
6.2
)).
It follows from
[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
and Theorem
6.6
that for all

r\gg 0
, there exists a Zariski open subscheme
U\subset(X^{o})^{r}

such that the composite map
\phi^{r}_{X|D}\colon U(k)\hookrightarrow(X^{o}(k))^{r}\to{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X|D)(k)={\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)
between the adic spaces is open and has a dense image.
We now look at the commutative diagram

(11.1)

\textstyle{U(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi^{r}_{X|D}}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{r}_{w}}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{w}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-r\chi_{w}(P)}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.}

We showed above that the left vertical arrow is continuous. Since the bottom
horizontal arrow is clearly continuous, it follows that
\chi_{w}\circ\phi^{r}_{X|D}

is continuous. Since the image of
\phi^{r}_{X|D}
is open dense and
the map
U(k)\to\phi^{r}_{X|D}(U(k))
is open, it follows that

\chi_{w}
is continuous on the nonempty open subset
\phi^{r}_{X|D}(U(k))
.
This proves the proposition when
X^{o}(k)
and
D
are not empty.

We assume next that
D\neq\emptyset
but
X(k)=\emptyset
. We let

G^{\star}(X^{o})={\varinjlim}_{n}\ {\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)^{\star}
and

G^{\vee}(X^{o})={\varinjlim}_{n}\ {\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)^{\vee}
.
Recall from Lemma
8.8
that
{\varinjlim}_{n}{\operatorname{Br}}(X|nD)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
. Suppose we know that the map
\beta_{X^{o}}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to G^{\vee}(X^{o})

factors through
G^{\star}(X^{o})
. Then it follows from (
9.11
) that

\beta_{X|D}(\chi)\in G^{\star}(X^{o})\cap{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\vee}
. In particular, the composite
map
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\xrightarrow{\chi_{w}}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is
continuous for
n\gg 0
. This implies that
\chi_{w}
is continuous because
the first arrow is a quotient map of adic spaces by Corollary
6.10
and

[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
. It remains therefore to show that

\beta_{X^{o}}({\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o}))\in G^{\star}(X^{o})
.

We fix an element
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
and let
\chi_{w}=\beta_{X^{o}}(w)
. By

[
59
, Thm. 4.3.1]
(see also
[
11
, Thm. 2.2]
),
we can find a finite field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
and a geometrically connected
smooth projective curve
Y
over
k^{\prime}
together with a finite morphism

g\colon Y\to X_{k^{\prime}}
such that the degrees of
{k^{\prime}}/k
and
g

are powers of
p
and
Y
admits a strict semi-stable reduction over
k^{\prime}
. That is,
there exists a projective and flat morphism
\phi\colon{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\rm Spec\,}({\mathcal{O}}_{k^{\prime}})

such that
{\mathcal{Y}}
is regular whose generic fiber is
Y
and whose (scheme theoretic)
closed fiber
Y_{s}
is a reduced curve with only double point singularities.

We let
Y^{o}
(resp.
E^{\dagger}
) be the inverse image of
X^{o}
(resp.
D^{\dagger}
)
under the composite map
Y\to X_{k^{\prime}}\to X
and let
{\mathcal{Y}}^{o}
be the complement
of the closure of
E^{\dagger}
in
{\mathcal{Y}}
.
By an application of Weil conjectures over the residue field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
of
k^{\prime}
, we can
find a closed point
P_{s}\in{\mathcal{Y}}^{o}\cap(Y_{s})_{\rm reg}
whose degree over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is a
power of
p
. It is easy to see that there exists a closed point
P\in Y^{o}

whose degree over
k^{\prime}
is same as that of
P_{s}
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. We let

F=k^{\prime}(P)
and let
X^{\prime}=Y_{F}
. Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be the composite finite
surjective map. We let
X^{\prime o}=f^{-1}(X^{o})
and
E=f^{*}(D)
.
It follows then that
X^{\prime}
is a smooth and geometrically connected curve over
the local field
F
such that
P\in X^{\prime o}(F)
and
\deg(f)=p^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
.

We have the canonical maps
{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\xrightarrow{f^{*}}{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime o})\xrightarrow{f_{*}}{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
whose composition is multiplication by
p^{n}
(e.g., see

[
4
, § 3.8]
). In particular,
{\rm Coker}(f_{*})
is torsion of exponent
p^{n}
.
On the other hand,
{\rm Coker}(f_{*})
is a
p
-divisible group by
[
4
, Thm. 3.2.3]
.
It follows that
f_{*}
is surjective. In particular,
w=f_{*}(w^{\prime})
for some

w^{\prime}\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime o})
. We can assume that
w^{\prime}\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|nE)
for some
n\gg 0
.
Since
X^{\prime o}(F)\neq\emptyset
, the previous case of the
lemma shows that
\beta_{X^{\prime}|nE}(w^{\prime})\in{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{\prime}|nE^{\prime})^{\star}
. We let

\chi_{w^{\prime}}=\beta_{X^{\prime}|nE}(w^{\prime})
.

We now look at the diagram

(11.2)

\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{w}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{\prime}|nE)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{w^{\prime}}}

It follows from Lemma
9.7
that this diagram is commutative.
Since
f^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|nD)\to{\mathbf{Pic}}(X^{\prime}|nE)
is a morphism between locally
of finite type
k
-schemes by Theorem
5.6
, it follows from

[
5
, Prop. 5.4]
that the left vertical arrow is
continuous. We deduce that
\chi_{w}
is continuous.
This concludes the proof when
D\neq\emptyset
.

To prove the proposition for
(X,\emptyset)
, we choose a nonempty effective
divisor
D\subset X
and let
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\subset{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
.
We have shown above that the composite
map
{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm CH}_{0}(X)\xrightarrow{\chi_{w}}{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is continuous.
On the other hand, it follows from Corollary
6.10

and
[
4
, Thm. 10.5.1]
that the first arrow is a topological quotient map.
We deduce that
\chi_{w}
is continuous.
∎

Lemma 11.3
.

Let
{k^{\prime}}/k
be a finite field extension. Let
X^{\prime}=X_{k^{\prime}},\ D^{\prime}=D_{k^{\prime}}
and

X^{\prime o}=X^{\prime}\setminus D^{\prime}
. Let
f\colon X^{\prime}\to X
be the projection map.
Then the following hold.

(1)

The map
f^{*}\colon{\rm Pic}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})
is a closed embedding of
adic spaces.

(2)

f^{*}
induces a surjective homomorphism

(f^{*})^{\star}\colon{\rm Pic}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})^{\star}\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X|D)^{\star}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.6
, the pull-back map
f^{*}\colon{\rm Pic}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})

is the canonical inclusion
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)(k)\subset{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)(k^{\prime})
. To prove (1), we
therefore need to show that the inclusion
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)(k)\subset{\mathbf{Pic}}(X|D)(k^{\prime})
of
adic spaces is closed. But this follows from
[
5
, Prop. 5.11(3)]
since the
inclusion
k\subset k^{\prime}
is easily seen to be closed (e.g., see

[
39
, Prop. II.5.7]
).

Since
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is a divisible group, one easily reduces the proof of (2) to showing
that the map
(f^{*})^{\star}\colon{\rm Pic}^{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})^{\star}\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)^{\star}
is surjective.
Let
\chi\colon{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be a continuous character.
By part (1) of the lemma and
[
9
, Cor. 4.42]
,
\chi
extends to a continuous
character
\chi^{\prime}\colon{\rm Pic}^{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\to{\mathbb{T}}
. It remains to show that the image of

\chi^{\prime}
lies in
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
We write
F=F_{1}\times F_{2}
, where
F_{1}
is the cokernel of the diagonal inclusion

k^{\times}\hookrightarrow\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}k(x_{i})^{\times}
and

F_{2}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}{\mathbb{W}}_{n_{i}-1}(k(x_{i}))
.
We then note using
(
5.1
) and the proof of Lemma
7.1
(see also
Proposition
6.9
) that there is an exact sequence of topological groups

(11.3)

0\to F\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(X|D)\to{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)\to 0.

We claim that
\chi(F)
is finite.

Since
F_{2}
is a torsion group of bounded exponent,
\chi(F_{2})
must be finite.
For every
1\leq i\leq r
, we have an exact sequence of topological groups

(11.4)

0\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k(x_{i})}\to k(x_{i})^{\times}\to{\mathbb{Z}}\to 0.

Since
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k(x_{i})}
is a profinite group, its image under
\chi
must be finite.
In particular, it is a cyclic subgroup of
{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
of the type
{{\mathbb{Z}}}/n
for
some integer
n\geq 1
. We now look at the commutative diagram of exact sequences

(11.5)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k(x_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k(x_{i})^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{Z}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\chi({\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{k(x_{i})})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{n}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Since the image of the right vertical arrow must be finite, it follows that

\chi(k(x_{i})^{\times})
is finite. Summing over
1\leq i\leq r
, we get that

\chi(F_{1})
is finite. This proves the claim.

We let
D^{\prime}_{\rm red}=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\}
and
D^{\prime}=\sum_{i}m_{i}[y_{i}]
.
We let
F^{\prime}=F^{\prime}_{1}\times F^{\prime}_{2}
, where

F^{\prime}_{1}
is the cokernel of the diagonal inclusion

k^{\prime\times}\hookrightarrow\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}k(y_{i})^{\times}
and

F^{\prime}_{2}=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}{\mathbb{W}}_{m_{i}-1}(k(y_{i}))
.
We then have an exact sequence similar to (
11.3
).
Since
F^{\prime}_{2}
is a torsion group of bounded exponent,
\chi^{\prime}(F_{2})
must be finite.
On the other hand, each
\chi^{\prime}(k(y_{i})^{\times})
is finite by Lemma
10.7
.
Summing over
1\leq i\leq s
, we get that
\chi^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{1})
is finite. We deduce that

\chi^{\prime}(F^{\prime})
is finite.

Finally, we look at the commutative diagram of exact sequences

(11.6)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}^{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\chi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\rm Pic}^{0}(X^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{{\mathbb{Z}}}/{m^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{T}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

It follows from Corollary
6.10
that

\alpha
is a quotient map. Since
m^{\prime}\circ\chi^{\prime}
is continuous, it follows that

\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}
is a continuous homomorphism. On the other hand,
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X^{\prime})
is
profinite. It follows that the image of
\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}
is finite. We conclude that the
image of
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})
under
\chi^{\prime}
is finite.
We have thus shown that
\chi^{\prime}\in{\rm Pic}^{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})^{\star}
.
This proves (2) and concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

12.
Perfectness of Brauer-Manin pairing for modulus pairs

The goal of this section is to prove the perfectness of the Brauer-Manin pairing for
a one-dimensional modulus pair. We shall also prove Theorem
1.1
using the
perfectness of the Brauer-Manin pairing.
We let
k
be a local field of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
. Let
X
be a
geometrically integral smooth projective curve over
k
and let
D\subset X
be an
effective divisor. We let
j\colon X^{o}\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion of the complement of

D
. We write
D=\sum_{i}n_{i}[x_{i}]
, where
D^{\dagger}=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\}
is the
support of
D
with reduced closed subscheme structure.
We let
K
denote the function field of
X
.
For
x\in X_{(0)}
, we let
X_{x}={\rm Spec\,}({\mathcal{O}}_{X,x})
and
X^{h}_{x}={\rm Spec\,}({\mathcal{O}}^{h}_{X,x})
.

12.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

By Proposition
11.2
, the Brauer-Manin pairing with modulus
(cf. Proposition
9.4
) induces continuous homomorphisms

\beta_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
and
\beta_{X^{o}}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to C(X^{o})^{\star}
. We begin by showing the injectivity of these homomorphisms.

Lemma 12.1
.

\beta_{X|D}
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

Let
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
be such that
\chi_{w}:=\beta_{X|D}(w)=0
.
For
x\in X_{(0)}
, let
