\iota_{x}\colon{\rm Spec\,}(k(x))\hookrightarrow X
be the
inclusion. We let
w_{x}
be the image of
w
under the canonical composite map

{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\to{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x})
. Let
\chi^{1}_{w}\colon{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
and

\chi^{2}_{w}\colon{K^{\times}_{\infty}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be the two components of
\chi_{w}

(see §
9.2
). Our assumption implies that
\chi^{i}_{w}=0
for
i=1,2
.

Suppose
x\in D
. Since
\chi^{2}_{w}=0
, it follows from

[
20
, § 6, Thm. 1]
that
w_{x}=0
.
The commutative diagram of exact sequences

(12.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3}_{x}(X_{x},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X_{x}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{h}_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{3}_{x}(X^{h}_{x},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{h}_{x}})}

implies that as an element of
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
, the Brauer class
w
lies in
{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{x})

and it dies in
{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{h}_{x})
. In particular,
\iota^{*}_{x}(w)=0
.
Since
\chi^{1}_{w}=0
, we also have
\iota^{*}_{x}(w)=0
for every
x\in X^{o}_{(0)}
.
It follows that
w
is a class in
{\operatorname{Br}}(X)
such that
\iota^{*}_{x}(w)=0
for

x\in X_{(0)}
. We conclude from
[
46
, Thm. 9.2]
that
w=0
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 12.2
.

We have the following.

(1)

If
p>1
, the map
\beta_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}

is an isomorphism.

(2)

If
p=1
, the map
\beta_{X|D}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\to({\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star})_{\rm tor}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

In view of Lemma
12.1
, we only need to show that
\beta_{X|D}
is surjective.
We first prove this surjectivity under the assumption that
D^{\dagger}\subset X(k)
.
It follows from Lemma
8.8
that

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D^{\dagger})={\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)={\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
when
p=1
.
Using Lemma
7.4
, we can assume in this case that
D
is reduced.
Hence,
D
will assumed to be reduced (and
n=1
) in the following argument
when
p=1
.

We let
G^{\star}(X^{o})={\varinjlim}_{n}\ {\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)^{\star}
.
We shall first show that the map
\beta_{X^{o}}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\to G^{\star}(X^{o})

is surjective. We consider the diagram

(12.2)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X^{o}}}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in D}{\oplus}}H^{3}_{x}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{X|D}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X|D}}
\textstyle{H^{3}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{X}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\textstyle{G^{\star}(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in D}{\oplus}}H^{0}(X^{h}_{x},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{h}_{x}})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{(\iota^{*}_{D})}^{\star}}
\textstyle{(k^{\times})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where
\gamma_{X|D}
is the direct sum of maps
\gamma_{x}
given by
Lemma
10.2
and
\delta_{X}
is given by Lemma
10.5
.
The top row is the localization sequence for the étale sheaf
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X}

and hence is exact except that we need to explain the surjectivity of
\eta_{X|D}
.
But this follows from Corollary
10.10
.
The bottom row is a complex which is exact at
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}
and
G^{\star}(X^{o})

by Lemmas
7.3
,
7.5
and

10.2
.

The left square in (
12.2
) is commutative by
Corollary
9.5
and the right square is commutative by
Lemma
10.6
. To show the commutativity of the middle square,
we look at the diagram

(12.3)

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X|nD)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X|nD}}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in D}{\oplus}}{\operatorname{Br}}(K_{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{x}\gamma_{K_{x}}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|nD)^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{x\in D}{\oplus}}(K^{\times}_{x})^{\star}}

for
n\geq 1
, where the dual of
K^{\times}_{x}
is taken with respect to its
Kato topology. This diagram is commutative by the definition of
\beta_{X|D}
, where

\gamma_{K_{x}}
is as in (
10.1
). When we restrict
\beta_{X^{o}}
to

{\operatorname{Br}}(X|nD)
, the middle square in (
12.2
) commutes because
it is the composition of (
12.3
) with the sum (over
x\in D
)
of right squares in (
10.1
). Taking the limit over
n\geq 1
,
we see that the middle square in (
12.2
) commutes.

To show that
\beta_{X^{o}}
is an isomorphism, we note that
\beta_{X}
is an isomorphism
by
[
32
, Thm. 4]
and
[
46
, Thm. 9.2]
.
The arrow
\gamma_{X|D}
is an isomorphism by Lemma
10.2
, and

\delta_{X}
is an isomorphism by Theorem
10.8
.
An easy diagram chase shows that the bottom row of (
12.2
) is
exact and
\beta_{X^{o}}
is an isomorphism. This also proves that

\beta_{X|D}
is surjective when
p=1
.
To pass from
\beta_{X^{o}}
to
\beta_{X|D}
when
p>1
, we fix a character

\chi\in{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}
. It follows from the surjectivity of
\beta_{X^{o}}
that
there exists a class
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})={\underset{n}{\bigcup}}\ {\operatorname{Br}}(X|nD)
such that

\chi=\chi_{w}:=\beta_{X^{o}}(w)
. It follows from (
9.2
) and
the definition of
\beta_{X|D}
that the map

\chi_{w}\colon K^{\times}_{\infty}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
annihilates
I(X|D)
.
Using Definition
8.9
, this forces
w
to lie in
{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)
. In this case,
we must have
\chi=\beta_{X|D}(w)
. This finishes the proof of surjectivity of

\beta_{X|D}
when
D^{\dagger}\subset X(k)
.

To prove the general case, we choose a finite field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k

such that
{\rm Supp}(D_{k^{\prime}})\subset X(k^{\prime})
. We let
X^{\prime}=X_{k^{\prime}}
and
D^{\prime}=D_{k^{\prime}}
.
We let
f\colon(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to(X,D)
be the projection and consider the diagram

(12.4)

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X^{\prime}|D^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{\prime}|D^{\prime})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f^{*})^{\star}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X|D}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X|D)^{\star}.}

This diagram is commutative by Lemma
9.7
. We have shown above that

\beta_{X^{\prime}|D^{\prime}}
is surjective, and Lemma
11.3
says that
the right vertical arrow is surjective. It follows that
\beta_{X|D}
is
surjective. This concludes the proof.
∎

End of the proof of Theorem
1.6
:
Combine Lemmas
2.5
,
8.8
,
12.1
,

12.2
and Proposition
11.2
.

\hfill\qed

As an application of Theorem
1.6
, we get the following result about the
norm map between the Brauer groups of regular curves over local fields.

Theorem 12.3
.

Let
X
be a geometrically connected regular quasi-projective curve over a local
field
k
and let
k^{\prime}
be a finite field extension of
k
. Let
f\colon X_{k^{\prime}}\to X

be the projection. Assume that
X
is either smooth or affine.
Then the map
f_{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{k^{\prime}})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(X)
is surjective.

Proof.

We choose an open embedding
j\colon X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
, where
\overline{X}
is a connected
regular projective curve. Then
\overline{X}
is necessarily geometrically connected.
We let
D=\overline{X}\setminus X
with the reduced closed subscheme structure.
We let
\overline{X}^{\prime}=\overline{X}_{k^{\prime}},\ X^{\prime}=X_{k^{\prime}}
and
D^{\prime}=D_{k^{\prime}}
. If
\overline{X}
is smooth
(e.g., when
p=1
), we conclude the proof by combining Proposition
8.11
,
Lemma
11.3
and Theorem
1.6
. We shall
now assume that
p>1
and
X
is affine.

If
{k^{\prime}}/k
is purely inseparable, then

{\rm Coker}(f_{*})
is annihilated by some power of
p

(e.g., see
[
4
, § 3.8]
). On the other hand,
{\rm Coker}(f_{*})
is
p
-divisible
by
[
4
, Thm. 3.2.3]
. It follows that
{\rm Coker}(f_{*})=0
.
In general, we can get a factorization
X_{k^{\prime}}\to X_{k^{\prime\prime}}\to X
, where

{k^{\prime\prime}}/k
is separable and
{k^{\prime}}/{k^{\prime\prime}}
is purely inseparable.
Since
X_{k^{\prime\prime}}
is a geometrically connected regular affine curve,
the map
{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{k^{\prime}})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{k^{\prime\prime}})
is surjective.
We can thus assume that
{k^{\prime}}/k
is separable.

As in the proof of Proposition
11.2
, we can find a finite field extension

l/k
, a geometrically connected smooth projective curve
\overline{Y}
over
l
, and a
finite surjective morphism of
k
-schemes
g\colon\overline{Y}\to\overline{X}
such that

\deg(g)
and
[l:k]
are some powers of
p
. We let
Y=g^{-1}(X)
and

E=g^{*}(D)
. We let
A=l\otimes_{k}k^{\prime}
and
\overline{Y}_{A}=\overline{Y}\times_{{\rm Spec\,}(l)}{\rm Spec\,}(A)
. Since
{k^{\prime}}/k
is separable, we see that

A=\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}l_{i}
, where each
{l_{i}}/l
is a finite
separable field extension.

We consider the diagrams

(12.5)

\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\prod}}}\overline{Y}_{l_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\overline{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle r}}{{\underset{i=1}{\oplus}}}{\operatorname{Br}}({Y}_{l_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}({X}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\overline{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\overline{X}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}({Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{*}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X),}

where the left square is Cartesian whose all arrows are finite.
The right square is commutative by Proposition
8.11
.

We now proceed as follows. Since
\overline{Y}
is a geometrically connected smooth projective
curve over
l
, we have argued previously that
h_{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(Y_{l_{i}})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(Y)
is
surjective for each
i
. Since
\deg(g)=p^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
, we have also
seen previously that
g_{*}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}(Y)\to{\operatorname{Br}}(X)
is surjective. In particular,

f_{*}\circ g^{\prime}_{*}=g_{*}\circ h_{*}
is surjective. It follows that
f_{*}
is surjective.
This concludes the proof.
∎

12.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

We let the notations and assumptions be as stated in the beginning of
§
12
. We shall divide the proof of Theorem
1.1
into several
cases. We begin with the following case.
Recall that
H^{q}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})={\varprojlim}_{n}H^{q}(X,{\mathbb{G}}_{m,(X,nD)})\cong{\varprojlim}_{n}H^{q}(X,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(X,nD)})
.

Lemma 12.4
.

One has
H^{0}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})=H^{i}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})=0
for all

i\geq 3
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
H^{0}(X,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(X,nD)})=0
for every

n\geq 1
. In particular,
H^{0}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})=0
. To show
that
H^{i}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})=0
for
i\geq 3
is equivalent to show that

H^{i}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})_{\rm tor}=0
for all
i\geq 3
(e.g., see
[
4
, Lem. 3.5.3]
).
It already follows from Remark
10.9
that

H^{i}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\{p\}=0
for all
i\geq 3
.

We now show the prime-to-
p
case.
For
i\geq 4
, we have a surjection
H^{i}(X^{o},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\{p^{\prime}\}(1))\twoheadrightarrow H^{i}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\{p^{\prime}\}
. On the other hand, the term on the left of this
surjection is zero because
cd(X^{o})\leq 3
. For
i=3
, note that there is an
inclusion
H^{3}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\{p^{\prime}\}\hookrightarrow H^{4}_{D}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\{p^{\prime}\}
by
Corollary
10.10
.
On the other hand, there is a canonical surjection

H^{4}_{D}(X,{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\{p^{\prime}\}(1))\twoheadrightarrow H^{4}_{D}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X})\{p^{\prime}\}
.
Using Gabber’s purity, the term on the left of this surjection
is isomorphic to
{\underset{x\in D}{\bigoplus}}H^{2}(k(x),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\{p^{\prime}\})
.

We are thus reduced to showing that
H^{2}(k^{\prime},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\{p^{\prime}\})=0
for
i\geq 2
if

{k^{\prime}}/k
is any finite field extension. By
[
15
, Thm. 9.9]
, we have an isomorphism

H^{2}(k^{\prime},{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}\{p^{\prime}\})\cong{\varinjlim}_{p\nmid n}{\rm Hom}_{{\mathbf{Ab}}}(\mu_{n}(k^{\prime}),{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})
, where the limit is taken with respect to
the maps
\mu_{mn}(k^{\prime})\xrightarrow{m}\mu_{n}(k^{\prime})
.
But this limit is zero since
({k^{\prime}}^{\times})\{p^{\prime}\}
is finite.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Recall that there is an exact sequence

(12.6)

{{\mathbb{Z}}}^{r}\to{\rm CH}_{0}(X)\xrightarrow{j^{*}}{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})\to 0,

where the first arrow is the sum of cycle class maps for points in
D
.
We endow
{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})
with the quotient of the adic topology of
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)

via this exact sequence. Note that this topology of
{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})
is
independent of any choice of a regular compactification of
X^{o}
.
We shall consider
H^{2}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})
to be a discrete abelian group.

Lemma 12.5
.

There is a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups

{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})^{\rm pf}\times H^{2}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

We look at the diagram

(12.7)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{2}_{cc}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X}}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(D^{\dagger})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{D^{\dagger}}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j^{*})^{\star}}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{({{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}})^{r},}

where the right vertical arrow is the sum (over
x\in D^{\dagger}
)
of the maps
{\rm inv}_{k(x)}
.
It follows from the exactness of the top row that the canonical map

H^{2}_{cc}(X,{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\to H^{2}(X,{\mathcal{K}}^{M}_{1,(X,D^{\dagger})})
is an
isomorphism.

It is immediate from the construction of the Brauer-Manin pairing (
9.5
)
(with
D=\emptyset
) that the right square in (
12.7
) is commutative.
The middle and the right vertical arrows are isomorphisms. It follows that there
is a unique isomorphism
\beta^{1}_{X^{o}}\colon H^{2}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})^{\star}
such that (
12.7
) is commutative.
Since
\beta^{1}_{X^{o}}
is automatically continuous, we get
a continuous pairing
{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})^{\rm pf}\times H^{2}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X^{o}})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
. Since
{\rm CH}_{0}(X)^{\star}
is a torsion group,
{\rm CH}_{0}(X^{o})^{\star}
must also be
torsion. We can now apply Lemma
2.5
and the Pontryagin duality
between profinite and discrete torsion groups to conclude the proof.
∎

End of the proof of Theorem
1.1
:
Combine Theorem
1.6
with Lemmas
2.5
,
8.8
,

12.4
and Lemma
12.5
.

\hfill\qed

Corollary 12.6
.

There is a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})\times C(X^{o})^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

Combine Theorem
1.1
and Lemma
11.1
.
∎

12.3.
Duality for the cohomology of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m,K}

Let the notations and assumptions be as stated in the beginning of
§
12
. Recall that
K
denotes the function field of
X
.
We let
H^{q}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m}):={\underset{D\in{\rm Div}(X)}{\varprojlim}}H^{q}(X,{\mathbb{G}}_{m,(X,D)})
. We let
C(K)
be the cokernel of the canonical map

K^{\times}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\widehat{I}(X):={\underset{x\in X_{(0)}}{\prod^{\prime}}}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}
, where
\alpha
is the canonical inclusion (cf. (
9.9
)).
We endow
H^{1}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})
with the inverse limit of the adic topologies
of
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
for
D\in{\rm Div}(X)
, and
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
with the discrete topology.

To prove the duality theorem for the étale cohomology of

{\mathbb{G}}_{m,K}
, we need the following.

Lemma 12.7
.

For every effective divisor
D\subset X
with complement
X^{o}
, the canonical maps

H^{1}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\to H^{1}_{cc}(X^{o},{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\to H^{1}(X,{\mathbb{G}}_{m,(X,D)})
are surjective.

Proof.

Using Lemma
11.1
, it suffices to show that for every reduced effective
divisor
D\subset X
with
X^{o}=X\setminus D
, the map

C(K)\to C(X^{o})
is surjective, and the map
C(K)\to{\varprojlim}_{D}C(X^{o})
is
bijective, where the limit is over all reduced effective divisors
D\subset X
.

We have a commutative diagram of exact sequences

(12.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\widehat{I}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{D}}
\textstyle{C(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{D}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{D}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\oplus B(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where we let
B(D):=\widehat{K}^{\times}_{\infty}={\prod}_{x\in D}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}
.
We let
A(D)={\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\oplus B(D)
.
It is straightforward to check that each
\beta_{D}
is surjective.
In particular, each transition map of the cofiltered system
\{A(D)\}

is surjective. It remains to show that the map

\beta:={\varprojlim}_{D}\beta_{D}\colon\widehat{I}(X)\to{\varprojlim}_{D}A(D)
is
bijective.

It is easy to see that
{\prod}_{x\in X_{(0)}}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\varprojlim}_{D}B(D)
.
We let
\pi_{D}\colon A(D)\to B(D)
denote the projection.
For
D^{\prime}>D
, the transition map
\Phi_{D^{\prime}>D}\colon A(D^{\prime})\to A(D)

has the property that if
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in A(D^{\prime})
with

a_{1}\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X\setminus D^{\prime}),\ a_{2}\in{\prod}_{x\in D}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}
and

a_{3}\in{\prod}_{x\in D^{\prime}\setminus D}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}
, then

(12.9)

\Phi_{D^{\prime}>D}(a_{1})=a_{1},\ \Phi_{D^{\prime}>D}(a_{2})=a_{2}\ \mbox{and}\ \Phi_{D^{\prime}>D}(a_{3})={\prod}_{x\in D^{\prime}\setminus D}v_{x}(a_{3}).

It follows that
\pi_{D}\circ\Phi_{D^{\prime}>D}=\Phi_{D^{\prime}>D}\circ\pi_{D^{\prime}}
.
That is, the projection
\{\pi_{D}\}\colon\{A(D)\}\to\{B(D)\}

is a strict morphism of pro-abelian groups. Taking the limits, we get a
commutative diagram

(12.10)

\textstyle{{\varprojlim}_{D}A(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{\prod}_{x\in X_{(0)}}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}}
\textstyle{\widehat{I}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\prime}}

where
\beta^{\prime}
is the canonical inclusion

\widehat{I}(X)\hookrightarrow{\prod}_{x\in X_{(0)}}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}
. In particular,
\beta
is
injective.

We claim that
\pi
is injective. Indeed, if
(a_{D})\in{\varprojlim}_{D}A(D)

is an element indexed by reduced divisors
D\subset X

with
(a_{D})=(a_{0},a_{1})
(where
a_{0}\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X\setminus D)
and
a_{1}\in B(D)
)
such that
\pi((a_{D}))=0
, then we must have
a_{1}=0
for each reduced divisor

D\subset X
. That is,
(a_{D})\in{\varprojlim}_{D}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X\setminus D)={\bigcap}_{D}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X\setminus D)
. The claim now follows because it is not hard to
see that
{\bigcap}_{D}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X\setminus D)=0
.

To prove that
\beta
is surjective, let
(a_{D})\in{\varprojlim}_{D}A(D)\subset{\prod}_{x\in X_{(0)}}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x}
. Let
b=\pi((a_{D}))
and write

b=(b_{x})_{x\in X_{(0)}}
. If
v_{x}(b_{x})\neq 0
for infinitely many

x\in X_{(0)}
, then it follows from (
12.9
) that
the projection map
{\varprojlim}_{D}A(D)\to A(D)
is not defined at
b
for any
reduced divisor
D\subset X
. But this is absurd. We conclude that

b=\pi((a_{D}))\in\widehat{I}(X)
. This shows that
\beta
is surjective,
and concludes the proof.
∎

Using Lemma
12.7
, we endow
C(K)
with the inverse limit of the
adic topologies of
{\rm Pic}(X|D)
for
D\in{\rm Div}(X)
.
We can now prove the duality theorem for the cohomology of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m,K}
.

Theorem 12.8
.

For every integer
q\neq 0
, there is a bilinear pairing

H^{q}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\times H^{3-q}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

which induces perfect pairings of topological abelian groups

H^{1}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})^{{\rm pf}}\times H^{2}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}},

H^{2}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\times H^{1}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}

and

H^{3}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})\times H^{0}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

The existence of the pairing is an immediate consequence of
Theorem
1.1
and a limit argument (over
D\in{\rm Div}(X)
).
The perfectness of the third pairing follows from Lemma
12.4

using a limit argument. The perfectness of the second pairing follows
by applying Theorem
1.6
, Lemmas
2.5
,

2.6
,
8.8
and
12.7
,
and taking limit over
{\rm Div}(X)
.
To prove the perfectness of the first pairing, we only need to show that

H^{2}_{cc}(K,{\mathbb{G}}_{m})=0
. But this follows by taking limit over
{\rm Div}(X)

of the top exact sequence in (
12.7
) and observing that
the map
{\operatorname{Br}}(X)\to{\prod}_{x\in X_{(0)}}{\operatorname{Br}}(k(x))\cong{\varprojlim}_{D}{\operatorname{Br}}(D_{\rm red})
is
injective by Lemma
12.1
(with
D=\emptyset
).
∎

By (
9.9
), we have a bilinear pairing

{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\times\widehat{I}(X)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
, and the proof of Proposition
9.4

shows that this factors through
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\times C(K)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
The following result is the global version of
[
46
, Thm. 2.10, Cor. 2.12]

and provides an explicit description of
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)
.

Corollary 12.9
.

The above pairing induces a perfect pairing of topological abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\times C(K)^{{\rm pf}}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

In particular,
{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\xrightarrow{\cong}(C(K)^{\star})_{\rm tor}
.

13.
Brauer-Manin pairing for singular curves

In this section, we shall apply Theorem
1.6
to extend the Brauer-Manin
pairing for smooth projective curves à la Lichtenbaum-Saito to singular curves
over local fields.
To achieve this, we shall introduce a refined version of the Brauer group for
singular varieties. We shall then show that this refined group is directly related
to the Picard group for singular curves.
We fix a local field
k
of exponential characteristic
p\geq 1
.

13.1.
Levine-Weibel Brauer group of singular varieties

Before we introduce the refined Brauer group for singular quasi-projective varieties,
we recall some known facts about the completions of local
rings. For any semilocal ring
R
, let
\widehat{R}
denote the completion of
R
with
respect to its Jacobson radical.
Let
A
be a local integral domain which is essentially of finite type over a
field and let
B
denote the integral closure of
A
. We let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{A}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{m}}_{B}
)
denote the maximal ideal of
A
(resp. the Jacobson radical of
B
).
It is an elementary fact (e.g., see
[
1
, Chap. 10]
)
that
\widehat{A}\hookrightarrow\widehat{B}\cong B\otimes_{A}\widehat{A}\cong\prod_{i}\widehat{B_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}}
,
where the product is over all maximal ideals of
B
.
Furthermore,
\widehat{B}
coincides with the normalization of
\widehat{A}
if
\text{\rm dim}(A)=1
. In
particular, it is a product of discrete valuation rings
\widehat{B_{{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}}}
.
In the latter case, it is also easy to see using the faithfully flatness property of

A\hookrightarrow\widehat{A}
that
\widehat{A}\cap K=A
, where
K
is the quotient field of
A
.

We let
X
be an integral quasi-projective curve over
k
. Let
\pi\colon{X^{\prime}}\to X
be the normalization of
X
and let
K
denote the function field of
X
.
For every closed point
x\in X

and every
y\in\pi^{-1}(x)
, let
U^{0}_{x,y}\subset\widehat{K}_{y}
be the image of

(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}})^{\times}
under the composite map

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}\hookrightarrow\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime},y}}\hookrightarrow\widehat{K}_{y}
.

Definition 13.1
.

We let
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
be the subgroup of
{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{\rm reg})
consisting of elements
w

such that for every singular point
x\in X
and every
y\in\pi^{-1}(x)
, the
image of
w
under the canonical map
{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{\rm reg})\hookrightarrow{\operatorname{Br}}(K)\to{\operatorname{Br}}(\widehat{K}_{y})
has
the property that the associated character (under Kato’s pairing)

\chi_{w}\colon\widehat{K}^{\times}_{y}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
annihilates
U^{0}_{x,y}
.

Let
X
be an integral quasi-projective variety over
k
.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm l.c.i.\!}(X)
be the set of all integral curves
C\subset X

such that
C
is not contained in
X_{\rm sing}
and the inclusion
C\hookrightarrow X
is a local
complete intersection at every point of
C\cap X_{\rm sing}
.

Definition 13.2
.

We let
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
be the subgroup of
{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{\rm reg})
consisting of elements
w

such that for every
C\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\rm l.c.i.\!}(X),\ x\in X_{\rm sing}\cap C

and
y\in\nu^{-1}(x)
, the image of
w
under the canonical composite map

{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{\rm reg})\to{\operatorname{Br}}(\nu^{-1}(X_{\rm reg}))\to{\operatorname{Br}}(\widehat{k(C)}_{y})
has
the property that the associated character (under Kato’s pairing)

\chi_{w}\colon\widehat{k(C)}^{\times}_{y}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
annihilates
U^{0}_{x,y}
,
where
\nu\colon C_{n}\to X
is the canonical map.

It easily follows from the above definitions that
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)\subset{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(U)

if
U\subset X
is open. This is in contrast with the classical Brauer group of
singular varieties. It is also clear that
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)={\operatorname{Br}}(X)
if
X
is regular.
We shall refer to
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
as the ‘Levine-Weibel Brauer group’ of
X
.

13.2.
The Brauer-Manin pairing

For the rest of §
13
, we shall work with the following set-up.
Let
X
be a geometrically integral projective curve over
k
and
\pi\colon X_{n}\to X
the normalization of
X
. It is clear that
{X}_{n}
is geometrically
integral. We shall assume that
{X}_{n}
is smooth over
k
(this is automatic if

p=1
).
We let
K
denote the function field of
X
. We let
S
be the singular locus of
X

with the reduced closed subscheme structure
and let
E
be the scheme theoretic inverse image of
S
in
{X}_{n}
. We let

X^{o}=X_{\rm reg}
be the regular locus of
X
. We represent this datum in the
Cartesian squares

(13.1)

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{X}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X^{o}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X^{o}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{j}

We have recalled the definition of the Levine-Weibel Chow group
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)

and the isomorphism
{\operatorname{\rm cyc}}_{X}\colon{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Pic}(X)

after Lemma
5.4
. We let
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)_{0}={\rm Ker}(\deg\colon{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)\to{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
.
We shall use
{\rm Pic}(X)
and
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)
interchangeably.
In order to construct a duality between the Levine-Weibel Brauer group and
the Picard group of
X
, we need to give a new description of the latter group.

For a point
x\in S
, we let

E_{x}=\pi^{-1}(x)
and
\widehat{K}_{x,\infty}={\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}\widehat{K}_{y}
.
We let
U^{0}_{x,\infty}={\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}U^{0}_{x,y}
.
We have the canonical maps
K^{\times}\hookrightarrow\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}\twoheadrightarrow{\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}}/{U^{0}_{x,\infty}}
. We let
\delta_{x}
be the composite map.
Let
\delta={\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}\delta_{x}
,

Lemma 13.3
.

There is a canonical exact sequence

(13.2)

0\to k^{\times}\to K^{\times}\xrightarrow{({\rm div},\delta)}{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\underset{x\in S}{\prod}}\frac{\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}}{U^{0}_{x,\infty}}\right)\to{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)\to 0.

Proof.

We consider the diagram

(13.3)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X,S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\rm div}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{K^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{({\rm div},\delta)}
\textstyle{{{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus\left({\underset{x\in S}{\prod}}\frac{\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}}{U^{0}_{x,\infty}}\right)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

where the vertical arrows are the canonical inclusions and

\overline{{\rm CH}^{LW}_{0}(X)}
is defined to make the bottom row exact.
The top row is exact by definition of
{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)
.
It is clear that the left square is commutative. This yields a unique
homomorphism
\psi_{X}\colon{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)\to\overline{{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)}
such that the
right square also commutes.

To prove that
\psi_{X}
is bijective, it is equivalent to show that

(13.4)

0\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X,S}\to K^{\times}\xrightarrow{\delta}{\underset{x\in S}{\prod}}\frac{\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}}{U^{0}_{x,\infty}}\to 0

is exact.
Now, it is clear that if
f\in K^{\times}
is such that
\delta_{x}(f)=0
for every

x\in S
, then
f\in{\underset{x\in S}{\bigcap}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X,x}
(see the first
paragraph of §
13.1
). But it is not
hard to see that
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X,S}={\underset{x\in S}{\bigcap}}{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X,x}
,
using the fact that
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X,x}
is the set of rational functions on
X
which are
regular without a zero or a pole in a neighborhood of
x
. It remains to show that

\delta
is surjective.

We let
I_{x}\subset\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}
be a conductor ideal for the normalization

\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}}\hookrightarrow\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},E_{x}}}={\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},y}}
.
Then
I_{x}\twoheadrightarrow I_{x}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},y}}\subset\widehat{{\mathfrak{m}}_{y}}
for every
y\in E_{x}
, where the
latter is the maximal ideal of
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},y}}
. It is also clear that

\sqrt{I_{x}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},y}}}=\widehat{{\mathfrak{m}}_{y}}
. As

I_{x}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}I_{x}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},y}}
, it follows
that
(1+I_{x})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}(1+I_{x}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{n},y}})
.
In particular,
{\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}(1+\widehat{{\mathfrak{m}}_{y}}^{n})\subset U^{0}_{x,\infty}

for all
n\gg 0
. In other words, we have a factorization

(13.5)

K^{\times}\to{\underset{x\in S}{\prod}}\frac{\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}}{U^{n}_{x,\infty}}\twoheadrightarrow{\underset{x\in S}{\prod}}\frac{\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}}{U^{0}_{x,\infty}}

for all
n\gg 0
, where we let

U^{n}_{x,\infty}={\underset{y\in E_{x}}{\prod}}(1+\widehat{{\mathfrak{m}}_{y}}^{n})
.
We are now done because the left arrow is well known to be surjective as an application
of the approximation lemma in number theory (e.g., see
[
28
, Lem. 6.3]
).
This proves the exactness of (
13.4
).

To conclude the proof of the lemma, it remains to show that

k^{\times}={\rm Ker}({\rm div})\bigcap{\rm Ker}(\delta)
. But this is easy because we have
shown that
{\rm Ker}({\rm div})\bigcap{\rm Ker}(\delta)={\rm Ker}({\rm div})\bigcap{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{X,S}

and the latter is
k^{\times}
because
X
is geometrically integral.
∎

We now define the Brauer-Manin pairing for
X
as follows.
Let
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
. We define the associated character

\chi_{w}\colon{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus({\underset{x\in S}{\prod}}\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty})\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
exactly as we did to define the pairing (
9.4
) in
§
9.2
. It follows from the definition of
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
that

\chi_{w}
factors through
{\mathcal{Z}}_{0}(X^{o})\bigoplus{\underset{x\in S}{\prod}}\frac{\widehat{K}^{\times}_{x,\infty}}{U^{0}_{x,\infty}}
. The proof of the claim that

\chi_{w}\circ({\rm div},\delta)=0
is identical to the proof of Proposition
9.4
,
mutatis mutandis. We have thus shown the following.

Proposition 13.4
.

There exists a canonical bilinear pairing

(13.6)

{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)\times{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

13.3.
Perfectness of the pairing

We now state and prove the main result of §
13
. We shall continue to work
with the set-up described in §
13.2
.
Since
X
is geometrically integral, it follows from
[
29
, Thm. 9.2.5, 9.4.8]

that there is a group scheme
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X)
over
k
which is locally of finite type
and whose identity component
{\mathbf{Pic}}^{0}(X)
is a smooth quasi-projective group scheme over

k
, and
{\mathbf{Pic}}(X)(k)\cong{\rm Pic}(X)
. One also has that

{\mathbf{Pic}}(X_{k^{\prime}})\cong{\mathbf{Pic}}(X)_{k^{\prime}}

for every field extension
{k^{\prime}}/k
. It follows that
{\rm Pic}(X)
is equipped with its
canonical adic topology and
{\rm Pic}^{0}(X)
is its open subgroup. We shall let

{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
have the discrete topology.

Lemma 13.5
.

The pairing (
13.6
) is continuous.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the image of the induced homomorphism

\beta_{X}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)\to{\rm Pic}(X)^{\vee}
lies in
{\rm Pic}(X)^{\star}

(cf. §
4.2
).
To that end, we choose a conductor ideal sheaf

{\mathcal{I}}_{Z}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
associated to
\pi
so that we have an isomorphism

(1+{\mathcal{I}}_{nZ})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{*}((1+{\mathcal{I}}_{nD}))
for every
n\geq 1
, where
we let
Z\subset X
be the closed subscheme defined by

{\mathcal{I}}_{Z}
and
D
the scheme theoretic inverse image of
Z
under
\pi
.
It follows from Corollary
6.10
that the canonical map

{\rm Pic}(X_{n}|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X_{n})
has a factorization

{\rm Pic}(X_{n}|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X_{n})
in which all maps are
topological quotient maps between adic spaces.

We now let
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
and let
\chi_{w}\colon{\rm Pic}(X)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
be
the associated character. Since
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X^{o})
, it lies in
{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{n}|D)
for some
conductor closed subscheme
Z\subset X
and
D=Z\times_{X}X_{n}
as above.
It is easily seen that the composite map
\chi_{w}\colon{\rm Pic}(X_{n}|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X)\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}
is same as the one in (
9.8
) (see Remark
9.1
).
Proposition
11.2
then implies that this composite map is continuous.
Since
{\rm Pic}(X_{n}|D)\twoheadrightarrow{\rm Pic}(X)
is a topological quotient, it follows that

\chi_{w}
is continuous on
{\rm Pic}(X)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Let the notations be as in the proof of Lemma
13.5
.
From (
13.5
), one also deduces the following.

Lemma 13.6
.

For all
m\gg 0
, we have
{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm lw}(X)\subseteq{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{n}|mD)
.

Theorem 13.7
.

The pairing (
13.6
) induces a perfect pairing of
topological abelian groups

{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)\times{\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)^{\rm pf}\to{{\mathbb{Q}}}/{{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Proof.

The continuity of the pairing follows from Lemmas
2.5
and

13.5
. As in the proof of Theorem
1.6
, it
suffices to show that the map

\beta_{X}\colon{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)\to F
is bijective if we let

F={\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)^{\star}
when
p>1
and
F=({\rm CH}^{{\rm lw}}_{0}(X)^{\star})_{\rm tor}
when
p=1
.

Using Lemma
13.6
, we choose conductor
subschemes
Z\subset X
and
D=Z\times_{X}X_{n}\subset X_{n}
such that

{\operatorname{Br}}^{\rm lw}(X)\subseteq{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{n}|D)
.
We let
G={\rm CH}_{0}(X_{n}|D)^{\star}
(resp.
({\rm CH}_{0}(X_{n}|D)^{\star})_{\rm tor}
) if
p>1
(resp.

p=1
). We consider the commutative diagram

(13.7)

\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{n}|D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{X_{n}|D}}
\textstyle{G.}

The right vertical arrow is injective by Corollary
6.10
.
Since
\beta_{X_{n}|D}
is injective by Lemma
12.1
, it follows that

\beta_{X}
is injective. To prove its surjectivity, we let
\chi\in F
.
If we consider
\chi
as an element of
G
, then Lemma
12.2

says that
\chi=\beta_{X_{n}|D}(w)
for some
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}(X_{n}|D)
. In this case,
it follows directly from the definition of
{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
and Lemma
13.3

that
w\in{\operatorname{Br}}^{{\rm lw}}(X)
. This implies subsequently that

\beta_{X}(w)=\chi
. This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎
